<meta>Following the title page, this leaf provides introductory verses in both the original Greek and two Latin variations. These poems, known as tetrastichs (four-line stanzas), act as a formal invitation to the reader, characterizing the study of hieroglyphs not just as linguistics, but as the uncovering of divine mysteries and ancient oracles.</meta>

## TO HORAPOLLO
### A four-line poem. <note>original Greek: τετράστιχον (tetrastichon)</note>

->If you desire to perfectly know the mysteries of the sun-folk,<- <note>The Greek text uses "heliakos" (sun-like/solar), a poetic reference to the Egyptians as a people of the sun.</note>
->and their sacred paintings, O youth?<-
->Come, take up this book and study it with diligence;<-
->from here, learn the divine oracles.<-

---

## FOUR-LINE POEM TO HOR-
### Apollo.

->Do you wish, reader, to be taught the images of the Pharian race,<- <note>"Pharian" refers to the island of Pharos in Alexandria, used here as a literary term for Egypt.</note>
->from which very many secrets may be gathered?<-
->Read this writer; he will provide wonderful oracles,<-
->as one who carries before him that sacred name, Apollo.<- <note>The poem plays on the author's name, "Horapollo," suggesting that because he shares a name with the god of prophecy (Apollo), his book contains oracular truths.</note>

---

## ANOTHER WHICH
### approaches the Greek more closely. <note>This second Latin version is a more literal translation of the Greek poem above, whereas the first was a more creative adaptation.</note>

->Do you wish, reader, to know fully the mysteries of the Pharian race,<-
->and the writings marked in secret ways?<-
->Come now, read through this little book with an attentive mind;<-
->from here, you may learn the sacred oracles.<-

<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter of the 1551 edition of Horapollo's *Hieroglyphica*. # IN COMMENDATION OF HORUS,
Jean Carvin to the Reader,
An eleven-line poem in eleven-syllable meter. <note>original Greek/Latin: "Ἑνδεκάστιχον ἑνδεκασύλλαβον." This is a formal poetic structure where each of the eleven lines contains exactly eleven syllables.</note>

If sacred Egyptian monuments delight you, friend reader,
I pray you, look upon those things which
Horus Apollo now brings back to you.
For with sacred marks <note>The "sacred marks" are the hieroglyphs themselves, which Renaissance scholars believed were symbols of divine truths rather than phonetic writing.</note> he reveals and teaches
who is the highest god, the author of this world;
who is the sister of Phoebus, and who the goddess of Cyprus; <note>In classical mythology, Phoebus (the Sun) has a sister, Diana (the Moon). The goddess of Cyprus is Venus.</note>
fierce Mars, rejoicing in the blood of men,
and the one ready to carry the pious commands of the gods; <note>This refers to Mercury, the messenger god, often associated with the Egyptian god Thoth.</note>
how the Earth is balanced in the center of the world's
middle; and nearby: the sea, fire, air,
and whatever things are carried out within the hollow orb of heaven.

## BY THE SAME TO JEAN MERCIER
->A four-line poem.<- <note>Jean Mercier (c. 1510–1570) was a celebrated French scholar of Hebrew and a teacher of Jean Carvin. These poems highlight the personal and professional bond between the two editors.</note>

I believe you have drunk from the sweet nectar of
Pallas and Jupiter; for you devote yourself to sacred things day and night. <note>Pallas (Athena) and Jupiter represent the pinnacle of wisdom and divine law.</note>
You teach sacred things, you write, you read, you meditate constantly.
Rightly, therefore, you shall be worthy of a sacred name.

## JEAN MERCIER TO THE SAME.

I know not what star joins me to you in a constant bond,
and knits us together in eternal friendship.
I first guided your steps in your tender youth;
now, even as a man, I depend on you with anxious care.
Therefore, if I am able to meditate or write anything sacred,
attribute these things, received from the Almighty, to yourself.

# TO THE REVERED
## Father in Christ, Jean de Saint-Gelais,
## a man most celebrated for both his learning
## and the integrity of his character, 
## the most worthy Bishop of Uzès, 
## Jean Mercier sends his fondest greetings. <note>original Latin: "S. D. P." abbreviating "Salutem Dicit Plurimam." This was a standard formal greeting in Latin correspondence.</note>

SINCE I have always been persuaded, most excellent Prelate, <note>A "prelate" is a high-ranking member of the clergy, referring here to the Bishop.</note> from my "tender little nails," as they say, <note>original Latin: "a teneris unguiculis." This is a classical idiom, used by authors like Cicero and Horace, meaning "from earliest childhood."</note> by a certain instinct of nature, and since I have felt I must insist with a firm mind upon the opinion of those who have taught that nothing more outstanding than virtue and refinement <note>original Latin: "humanitate." In the Renaissance, this term referred to the "studia humanitatis" or the humanities—the study of classical literature, history, and moral philosophy intended to cultivate a well-rounded human being.</note> can be acquired in this life: for this reason it has come to pass that, by every path and effort, I have always striven toward those studies which provide people with the means to attain these things. For who is there, who [common...] <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page, in the middle of the word "vulgarem" (common/vulgar).</note>

->**DEDICATORY**<-
<sig>A iii</sig>

[...is so] ignorant of that most famous and celebrated proverbial verse among the Greeks: *Education is the staff of life* <note>original Greek: βακτηρία γάρ ἐστι παιδεία βίου (baktēria gar esti paideia biou). This proverb suggests that learning is the support upon which a person leans throughout their journey.</note>; to say nothing of the testimonies from so many authors encountered everywhere, with which books are filled, the voices of the wise are filled, and finally, all of antiquity is filled. 

And I do not know whether the administration of the state can do without these studies. Although this could be abundantly confirmed by the many things written by Plato, and by other masters of philosophy and humanistic learning <term>humanitatis: referring to the "studia humanitatis," the Renaissance curriculum of grammar, rhetoric, history, poetry, and moral philosophy intended to create a well-rounded citizen.</term>, certainly one of the Hebrew sages seems to have shown this most clearly in these words. I have not hesitated to write them down in their own native language (since I understand that you take primary delight in it, and have been diligently engaged in its study for many years):

->**On three things the world stands: on the Torah, on the service of God, and on the practice of kindness.**<- <note>original Hebrew: על שלשה דברים העולם עומד על התורה ועל העבודה ועל גמלת חסדים (Al shlosha devarim ha-olam omed: al ha-Torah, ve-al ha-avodah, ve-al gemilut hasadim). This is a famous quote from the Pirkei Avot, or "Ethics of the Fathers," a foundational text of Jewish rabbinic ethics.</note>

# EPISTLE

...by which he says the world subsists, and he commemorates the knowledge of the law <note>original: *legis*, referring here to the Torah mentioned in the Hebrew quote on the preceding page.</note>, which is certainly supported most strongly by the protection of these letters <note>original: *harum literarum*, referring to the study of literature and the humanities.</note>. Indeed, those who followed Stoic philosophy <term>Stoic philosophy: an ancient Greek school of thought that taught the development of self-control and fortitude as a means of overcoming destructive emotions.</term> seem not to have been far from the mark when they constantly asserted that virtue is the most perfect, the chief, and even the only good. On this subject, there exists among the Greeks a most beautiful epigram:

> O you who are experts in the Stoic stories, O you who have placed the most excellent doctrines in your sacred pages: that virtue is the soul's only good. For she alone has saved the lives of men and preserved cities. But while the pleasure of the flesh is a beloved goal for other men, this one [virtue] was the work of one of the daughters of Memory <note>original Greek: Ω῀ σοϊκῶν μύθων εἰδήμονες... This poem is attributed to Antipater of Sidon. The "daughters of Memory" are the Muses; the poet suggests that while others pursue physical pleasure, the Stoics pursue a virtue that earns a place in history and song.</note>.

->Which we have translated in this manner:<-

> *O! you in whose studies the Stoic sect is rooted,*
> *Placing marvelous doctrines in your sacred books:*
> *Namely, that the soul's only good is radiant virtue,*
> *Which alone protects and nurtures the life of men.*
> *But though delightful pleasure may be the goal for others,*
> *Only one of the daughters of Mnemosyne <term>Mnemosyne: the personification of Memory in Greek mythology and mother of the Muses.</term> is responsible for this.*

<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Bishop of Uzès. Having established the philosophical importance of virtue and the humanities on the previous page, the author now shifts to an autobiographical account of his education in Paris, Avignon, and Toulouse, describing the tension between his love for the liberal arts and his parents' desire for him to pursue a profitable career in law.</meta>

### DEDICATORY [EPISTLE]

In the name of this virtue, then, since I considered that no labor of either mind or body should be avoided, it seemed right from the very beginning to travel to **Paris** <note>original Latin: *Lutetia*, the ancient Roman name for Paris, which the author calls the "fertile mother of all good studies."</note>. There, after I had fully devoted my mind to the **humanities** <term>humanities: known in Latin as *humanitas*, this refers to the study of classical literature, rhetoric, and philosophy, which were thought to "humanize" or refine the student.</term> for several years, I was suddenly snatched away—as if in the middle of a race—by my parents. It is their habit to recall their children with every effort toward the **lucrative arts** <note>original Latin: *artes quaestuarias*, referring to professions such as law or medicine that were pursued for financial gain rather than for the sake of wisdom alone.</note>, or as the famous saying goes, toward things that **"bring in the bread"** <note>original Greek: πρὸς τὰ ἄλφιτα, literally "toward the barley-meal," a common idiom for earning a living.</note>. 

Consequently, I applied myself for some time to the study of law <note>original Latin: *legitimae scientiae*, literally "the lawful science."</note> at **Avignon** and **Toulouse**. But look! Anew and certainly **unexpectedly** <note>original Greek: ἀπροσδοκήτως</note>, my most kind mother <note>The author here personifies the city of Paris as his nurturing intellectual mother.</note> calls me back, as if the faculty of law ought to be completed in the very place where all other disciplines are by far the most perfect. And so, I returned to **Paris**—though I am not sure if I did so more for the purpose of taking what I had learned from the **"responses of the wise"** <term>responses of the wise: *responsa prudentium*, the formal legal opinions provided by ancient Roman jurists, which served as a primary source of law and a core subject for students.</term> and listening to them in the most ample...

<summary>The author recounts his educational journey, explaining how his parents pulled him away from his classical studies in Paris to study law in Avignon and Toulouse for financial reasons, before he eventually returned to Paris.</summary>
<keywords>Paris (Lutetia), Avignon, Toulouse, humanities, legal studies, career vs. passion, autobiographical, education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's autobiographical narrative. After studying law at Avignon and Toulouse as his parents wished, the author describes a turning point where he returned to the "gentler muses" of literature and discovered the ancient Egyptian secrets of Horapollo.</meta>

->### EPISTLE<-

->VI<-

...with the most distinguished senate of all **Europe** <note>original Latin: *Europę*. The author is likely referring to the legal high courts (Parlements) of France, which were often compared to the Roman Senate.</note>, I might strengthen my grasp of legal cases, or whether I should instead give myself over to be refined anew, and refresh my already weary mind with the **gentler Muses** <term>gentler Muses: a classical metaphor for the liberal arts, poetry, and philosophy, which were considered more "humanizing" and pleasant than the rigorous, technical study of law.</term>.

It was here that I happened upon **Horapollo** <note>original Latin: *Orum Apollinem*. Horapollo was the legendary author of the *Hieroglyphica*, a work discovered in 1419 that Renaissance scholars believed provided the key to understanding Egyptian hieroglyphs as divine, philosophical symbols.</note>, a truly ancient author. Good God, what profound learning I found in him! What a great window I discovered had been opened for the understanding of many mysteries; indeed, what was there not of a purer and more hidden **philosophy**?

While I was reading this author most attentively, it often occurred to me that it was not for nothing that **Plato**, **Pythagoras**, and other most eminent men had decided they must travel through **Egypt**. For indeed, just as much pleasure as arose from reading this author, an equal difficulty presented itself on the other hand: I perceived that many passages were mutilated and...

<summary>The author describes abandoning his legal career to return to the humanities, where he discovers the work of Horapollo. He reflects on how this ancient Egyptian wisdom explains why great Greek philosophers like Plato and Pythagoras traveled to Egypt.</summary>

->### DEDICATORY EPISTLE<-

...which could scarcely be restored without access to a most ancient manuscript. I pass over the fact that many things were not very successfully translated by a certain **Bernardino of Vicenza** <note>original Latin: *Bernardino Vincentino*. This likely refers to Bernardino Trevisano, an early 16th-century editor whose work was often criticized by later humanists for its lack of precision.</note>; indeed, many parts were even omitted, which perhaps ought to be blamed on the particular manuscript he happened to find. 

Therefore, when I was asked by certain men—who are most dear to me, and to whom I confess I owe a great deal—to translate this author into the most fitting language and to clarify the work with **scholia** <term>scholia: explanatory notes or commentaries inserted into a text to explain difficult language, historical context, or philosophical meanings.</term> where the subject required it, I could not refuse them. 

Furthermore, while I was considering in whose name I should publish whatever labor I have spent on this project (for there will surely be detractors who, as is their habit with everything else, might interpret this work of ours in the worst possible light), you alone came to mind, most kind **Prelate** <term>Prelate: a high-ranking ecclesiastical official, such as a bishop. In the Renaissance, scholars sought the protection of powerful church figures to shield their work from academic or religious criticism.</term>, who almost by your own...

...claim as your own. Yet perhaps you will wonder how I now, as if uninvited, am creeping into your familiar acquaintance—I, who am perhaps not even known to you by name—and by what right I dare to call your religious devotion and sanctity away to these otherwise profane matters. 

But first, most dear **Prelate** <term>Prelate: a high-ranking church official, here referring to the Bishop of Uzès.</term>, I would have you be persuaded of this: that since I am numbered among your citizens—that is, the people of **Uzès** <note>original Latin: *Uticensium*. This indicates the author is a resident of the diocese of Uzès in southern France.</note>—I owe everything to you as a most upright pastor, and I am most deeply bound to you in all things. 

Furthermore, the fame and splendor of your name have driven me to this; for you, not content with those studies which one might necessarily expect of a prelate, have chosen to immerse yourself even in the inner mysteries of **Greek** and **Hebrew** literature. Witness to this are those whom, out of your **kindness** <note>original Latin: *humanitate*. In the Renaissance, this term referred both to a person's character and to the "humanities"—the study of classical culture.</note>, you have kept for several years in your most celebrated company...

...status you have cherished and most liberally welcomed. Furthermore, regarding your **benevolence** <note>original Latin: *benevolentia*.</note> toward all our countrymen, there is no need for me to discuss it more extensively in this place. To this is added the fact that you have promoted certain members of my own family—those connected to me, indeed, by the closest degree of kinship—to the most distinguished honors in the governing of your city. 

All of these factors now compel me, even if unbidden, to dedicate to your **kindness** <note>original Latin: *humanitati*. This term encompasses both the Prelate's personal character and his refined education in the "humanities."</note> the **original text** <note>original Greek: *αὐτόγραφον* (*autographon*), meaning the Greek text of the author being edited.</note> of **Horus** <note>Referring to Horapollo, the supposed ancient author of a treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> himself, yet far more corrected than it was before, and likewise **translated by us into Latin** <note>original Latin: *latinitate donatum*, literally "gifted with Latinity."</note> and illustrated with **commentaries** <term>commentaries: or *scholia*, which are marginal or explanatory notes added by a scholar to clarify a difficult text.</term>. 

If you receive these works in the spirit with which they are offered—which indeed I do not doubt you will do, given your kindness toward every kind of literature and toward scholarly men—you will first bind me entirely to you, and then you will rouse my spirit to undertake even greater works hereafter.

<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory epistle to the Bishop of Uzès, following the author's offer to dedicate his edition of Horapollo to the Prelate. He transitions from discussing the merits of the work to a final plea for protection against critics and a formal closing.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<header>### EPISTLE</header>

...you will add to this. Although, what can be greater than these matters, which are drawn from the innermost and most essential—if I may use the expression—springs of **natural and moral philosophy**, and indeed for the most part from the axioms and decrees of the **mathematical sciences** <note>original: *matheseωn*, a Latinized Greek term referring to the "mathesis" or the broad field of learning and mathematics.</note>? 

Therefore, such as it is, I offer this entire work to you; I place it under your protection and patronage, so that you might defend and protect me—who, as our **Proculus** <note>Proculus was a famous 1st-century Roman jurist. The author is likely citing a specific legal phrasing regarding the respect due to a superior.</note> says, politely respects your majesty—and our **Horus** <note>Horapollo, the purported author of the hieroglyphic text.</note> from all the madness of malicious men.
<margin>See the law *non dubito*, under the title *Concerning captives and those returned by postliminy*.</margin> <term>postliminy: a legal doctrine (postliminium) where a person captured by an enemy is restored to their previous rights and status upon returning to their own territory.</term>

Moreover, may **God, the Greatest and Best** <note>original Latin: *Deus Opt. Max.* (Deus Optimus Maximus), a common Renaissance adaptation of the Roman title for Jupiter to the Christian God.</note>, grant success to your noble mind, to that sublime height of your honor, and finally to your entire estate. **Much prosperity** <note>original Greek: *εὐόλβια πολὺ* (*euolbia poly*).</note>. Fare well, most worthy Prelate, and receive our little gift with a pure spirit.

->Paris <note>original Latin: *Lutetiae Parisiorum*.</note>. April 19 <note>original Latin: *13. Cal. Maias*, the 13th day before the Kalends of May.</note><-
->1548.<-

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page serves as a preface to the reader, following the formal dedication to the Bishop of Uzès. It explains the editorial methodology used for this specific version of Horapollo's text, transitioning from the personal address to the patron to a general address to the scholarly public.</meta>

# TO THE READER

I would have you advised, reader, that in this edition of Horus <note>original Latin: *Ori*. The author refers to Horapollo, the purported author of this work on Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note>, many things are handled differently than in previous versions. This is because Guillaume Morel <note>Guillaume Morel (1505–1564) was a distinguished French printer and scholar known for his meticulous editions of Greek and Latin texts.</note>, a most learned and diligent man, provided us with an ancient manuscript of this author. From this manuscript, we have restored many passages in the first book (which was the only part contained in that manuscript, and even then, not recorded in its entirety).

Know this, however: that where a different reading occurred in that manuscript, we exercised our judgment and followed those versions which seemed superior. In the meantime, we have added the readings from the ancient codex <term>codex: a manuscript book, typically made of parchment or paper leaves bound together, as opposed to a scroll.</term> to the margin in instances where our own chosen reading was not unsatisfactory. Furthermore, you should be advised that in the ancient manuscript, the titles are not placed before the chapters, but are instead written in the margin. Since this was of little importance, we did not wish to rashly change the custom already established in this edition.

Therefore, kind reader, if you now hold a more corrected <note>original: *castigatiorem*, a technical term in Renaissance editing meaning a text that has been "chastened" or purified of errors through careful comparison of manuscripts.</note> version of Horus, attribute it to venerable antiquity. As for our labor in this matter—and the efforts of others through whose work this more corrected Horapollo now comes to light—we ask that you look upon it with favor.

<summary>The editor explains that this edition of Horapollo has been significantly improved using a newly discovered ancient manuscript provided by Guillaume Morel. He details his editorial methodology, including how he decided between conflicting readings and why he maintained certain formatting traditions.</summary>

<keywords>Horapollo, Guillaume Morel, manuscript restoration, textual criticism, Renaissance printing, codex, editorial methodology</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the alphabetical index of the previous page to the formal title page and the first entry of the treatise. The index entry for "The Sun and Moon denote time" on page 2 directly corresponds to the first chapter presented here.</meta>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO NILOUS <note>original: "ΩΡΟΥ ΑΠΟΛΛΩΝΟΣ ✠ ΝΕΙΛΩΟΥ, Νειλιακοῦ ἱερογλυφικὰ"</note>
Which he himself set forth in the Egyptian tongue, and Philip translated into the Greek dialect. <note>original: "ἃ ἐξήνεγκε μὲν αὐτὸς Αἰγυπτίᾳ φωνῇ, μετέφρασε δὲ Φίλιππος εἰς τὴν Ἑλλάδα Διάλεκτον"</note>

# THE SACRED SCULPTURES OF HORUS APOLLO OF THE NILE <note>original: "ORI APOLLINIS NILIACI Sacræ sculpturæ"</note>
Which he indeed produced in the Egyptian language, but Philip presented in the Greek language. <note>original: "quas ipse quidem Ægyptio sermone prodidit, Philippus uerò lingua Græca donauit"</note>

---

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a square border. It features an Ouroboros (a serpent eating its own tail) forming a circular frame. Inside this circle, three figures stand on a rocky ground: a man on the left with a staff and hat, a central figure in Roman military armor with a halo of solar rays, and a woman on the right in classical drapery. Above the serpent are personified icons of the Sun (left) and a crescent Moon (right), both with human faces.</image-desc>

->How they signify eternity.<- <note>original: "Πῶς αἰῶνα σημαίνουσι"</note>

When signifying **Eternity** <note>original: "ΑΙΩΝΑ" (Aion); referring to an age, an epoch, or infinite time.</note>, they draw the **sun** and the **moon**, because these are eternal elements. But when they wish to represent Eternity in another way, they paint a serpent having its tail hidden beneath the rest of its body, which <note>This describes the *Ouroboros*, the snake consuming its own tail, a symbol of the cyclical nature of the universe.</note>

<summary>This is the title page and opening chapter of Horapollo's Hieroglyphica, introducing the work as a translation from Egyptian to Greek and explaining the symbols for eternity.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, Hieroglyphics, Eternity, Sun, Moon, Ouroboros, Egypt, Philip, Aion, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 1, which introduced the work of Horapollo and began the explanation of symbols for Eternity. The previous page ended mid-sentence describing the serpent with its tail hidden.</meta>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<margin>1</margin>
### In what way they signify eternity. <note>original: "Quo pacto æuum ſignificent"</note>

The Egyptians call this serpent the **Oubaios** <note>original: "οὐβαῖον" (oubaion); likely a Greek transcription of the Egyptian word for the Uraeus cobra.</note>, which in the Greek language is the **Basilisk** <note>original: "βασιλίσκον" (basiliskon), meaning 'little king'</note>. They fashion it out of gold and place it around the statues of the gods. The Egyptians say that **Eternity** <note>original: "αἰῶνα" (aiona); Latin: "æuum"</note> is represented by this animal because, among the three known kinds of serpents, the others are mortal, but this one alone is immortal. Furthermore, it can destroy any other living creature simply by breathing upon it, even without delivering a bite. For this reason, since it appears to hold power over both life and death, they place it upon the heads of the gods.

<summary>This page concludes the first chapter on the symbolism of eternity, identifying the immortal 'Basilisk' or Uraeus serpent as a representation of divine power over life and death.</summary>
<keywords>eternity, serpent, Basilisk, Uraeus, Egyptian gods, immortality, Oubaios, symbolism</keywords>

### How they represent the Universe. <note>original Greek: "πῶς κόσμον" (pōs kosmon)</note>

When they wish to represent the **Universe** <note>The word used is "Cosmos," which to the Greeks implied both the world and a sense of beautiful order.</note>, they draw a serpent biting its own tail, marked with multicolored scales. By these scales, they hint at the stars in the heavens. This animal is very heavy, just as the earth is, and very smooth, like water. Furthermore, every year it sheds its old age by stripping off its skin; in the same way, the annual passage of time in the world undergoes a change and becomes young again. The fact that it uses its own body for food signifies that all things produced in the world through **Divine Providence** <term>Divine Providence: The governance of the world by a higher power (in Greek, "theia pronoia"), ensuring that the cycle of life continues through a deliberate plan.</term> will eventually experience a lessening and be drawn back into the world once more.

Furthermore, just as a serpent casts off its skin and its old age every year, so the turning year, which is produced by the revolution of the world, is renewed through this change and, as it were, grows young again. As for the fact that it uses its own body for food, this signifies that all things produced in the world through **Divine Providence** <term>Divine Providence: Known in Latin as *Providentia*, this refers to the idea that a divine power or God provides for and governs all things in the universe, maintaining the cycle of life.</term> are resolved back into the same world again, undergoing a kind of cycle of consumption and return.

...which is called **Sothis** in Egyptian <note>original Greek: σῶθις</note>, and in Greek **Astromyon** <note>original Greek: ἀστρομύων, meaning "Dog-star," specifically Sirius</note>. This star seems to rule over the others, appearing now larger and now smaller, and sometimes rising with more brilliance, and at other times less so. Furthermore, because we observe signs during the rising <note>original: ἀναβολὴν (anabolēn)</note> of this star regarding everything that is to be accomplished throughout the year, it is not without reason that they call the year **Isis**. And in another way, when they write "Year," they paint a **palm tree** <note>original Greek: φοίνικα (phoinika). In Greek, the word for the mythical bird and the palm tree is the same.</note>, because this tree alone among all others produces one branch at each rising of the moon, so that the year is completed in twelve branches. <margin>branch <note>original Greek: βάσιν (basin), likely a variant for "palm-branch"</note></margin>

### How they represent a year. 3

Furthermore, wishing to demonstrate the **year**, they paint **Isis**—that is, a woman. By this same sign, they also signify the goddess herself. Among them, Isis is a star which is called by the Egyptian name **Sothis**, but in Greek is called **Astromyon**. This star seems to hold the sovereignty among the rest of the stars; as it rises, it is at times larger, at times smaller, sometimes more splendid, and sometimes otherwise. Furthermore, because at the rising of this star we observe by certain signs those things which are to be fulfilled throughout the whole year, they therefore not without reason call the year **Isis**. In another way, indicating the year, they paint a **palm**, because this tree alone of all others produces a single branch <note>The Latin uses "ramos," meaning branches or fronds.</note> at each rising of the Moon, so that by the production of twelve branches, the year is fulfilled.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a palm branch on the left and a crescent moon with its horns pointing downwards on the right, emerging from clouds. Below the branch and the moon are smaller terrestrial plants and rocky ground.</image-desc>

### How they represent a month.

When they want to write "Month," they paint a **palm branch** <note>original Greek: βάϊν (baïn)</note>, or a **moon** turned downwards. They use a palm branch because of the reason previously mentioned regarding the palm tree <note>original Greek: φοίνικος (phoinikos). The author previously claimed the palm tree grows one new branch every month.</note>. They use the moon turned downwards because they say that in its rising, when it reaches fifteen degrees, its horns appear to point upwards; but in its waning, having completed the number of thirty days, its horns lean downwards.

<margin>4</margin>

### How they represent the current year.

When they record the current year, they draw a **quarter of an aroura** <note>original Greek: τέταρτον ἀρούρας (tetarton arouras)</note>. The **aroura** <term>aroura: a traditional Egyptian unit of land area, roughly 2,750 square meters</term> is a measure of land consisting of one hundred cubits. When they wish to express the concept of a "year," they say "a fourth," because they claim that from one rising of the **star Sothis** <note>original Greek: Σώθεως (Sōtheōs). This is the star Sirius; its "heliacal rising" was the most important astronomical event in ancient Egypt, marking the start of the New Year and the Nile flood.</note> until the next rising, a quarter of a day is added. Thus, the year of the god consists of three hundred and sixty-five days <insert>and a quarter</insert>. For this reason, every four years, the Egyptians calculate an intercalary day <note>A "leap day" added to keep the calendar in sync with the solar year.</note>, for four quarters complete a full day.

### In what manner they represent the imminent year. <gloss>5</gloss>

To signify the imminent year, they represent the fourth part of an aroura...

...they paint a part. The **aroura** <note>original Greek: ἄρουρα (aroura)</note>, from which the Latin word for a plowed field <note>original Latin: *arvum*</note> is derived, is a measure of land containing one hundred cubits. Therefore, when they wish to say "annual," they say "a fourth," because they claim that from one rising of the star which we call **Sothis** <note>The star Sirius; its heliacal rising—the first day it becomes visible in the dawn sky—marked the beginning of the Egyptian solar year and the flooding of the Nile.</note> until the next rising, a fourth part of a day is added. Indeed, the year of the Sun god is completed in three hundred and sixty-five days; for this reason, every four years the Egyptians calculate and intercalate <term>intercalate: to insert a day into a calendar to maintain its alignment with the solar year</term> an extra day. For four quarters of a day complete a full day.

...because the animal is long-lived. Furthermore, it is also because the hawk is thought to be an image of the sun, surpassing all other birds in the sharpness of its vision toward the sun's rays. For this reason, physicians use the **hawk-weed** <note>original Greek: ἱερακίᾳ βοτάνῃ (*hierakia botanē*); various species of the genus *Hieracium* were historically believed to sharpen eyesight, as hawks were thought to use them for this purpose.</note> for the healing of the eyes. From this, they also sometimes paint the sun, as the lord of sight, in the form of a hawk. 

And they use it to signify **Height**, because while other animals, when choosing to fly high, move sideways <margin>circumscribed</margin> <note>The marginal note "περιγράφεται" (*perigraphētai*) suggests the movement of other birds is limited or follows a defined circular path rather than a direct ascent.</note>, being unable to travel straight up; the hawk alone flies straight upward. 

And **Lowliness**, because other animals do not descend vertically toward the ground, but move downward at an angle; but the hawk moves straight down toward what is low. 

And **Superiority** <note>original Greek: ὑπεροχὴν (*hyperochēn*), meaning excellence or pre-eminence.</note>, because it seems to excel above all other birds. 

And **Blood**, since they say this animal does not drink water, but blood. 

And **Victory**, because this animal seems to conquer every other bird. For whenever it is pursued by a stronger animal, it turns itself upside down in the air, so that its claws are positioned upward, while it conducts the fight with its wings and back parts directed downward. For thus the animal fighting against it, being unable to do the same, is brought to defeat. 

Furthermore, the hawk is also used to represent the **Soul**, based on the interpretation of its name. For among the Egyptians, the hawk is called **Baieth** <note>original Greek: βαϊήθ (*baiēth*). This is a Hellenized version of the Egyptian concept of the *Ba* (soul/personality).</note>; and this name, when divided, signifies "soul" and "heart." For *bai* is "soul," and *eth* is "heart." And the heart, according to the Egyptians, is the symbol of the soul; so that the composition of the name signifies "soul-upon-the-heart." From...

Furthermore, the hawk, because of its sympathy toward the soul, does not drink water at all, but blood, upon which the soul is also nourished. <note>original Greek: Ἔτι καὶ ὁ ἱέραξ διὰ τὸ πρὸς τὴν ψυχὴν συμπάθειαν... (*Eti kai ho hierax dia to pros tēn psychēn sympatheian...*). Ancient physiology often linked the soul (psyche) directly to the blood.</note>

### 6. What they imply when painting a hawk.

When they wish to signify **God**, or **sublimity**, or **lowliness**, or **excellence**, or **blood**, or **victory**, they paint a hawk.

They use it to represent **God**, both because the animal is prolific and long-lived, and also because it seems, more than all other birds, to be an image of the **Sun**. By a certain peculiar and hidden power of nature, it gazes with the most intent eyes directly into the sun's rays. And for this reason, physicians use the **hawk-weed** <term>hieraceo: also known as Hieracium; an herb historically believed to sharpen the eyesight because hawks were thought to eat it</term> to heal defects of the eyes. From this, they also sometimes paint the sun—as the author and lord of sight—in the form of a hawk.

It signifies **Sublimity**, because while other animals move obliquely <margin>at an angle or sideways</margin> when they wish to be raised on high, and cannot be carried straight upward, the hawk alone flies straight into the heights.

Furthermore, it signifies **Lowliness** or dejection, <note>q: Latin abbreviation for *quod*, meaning "because" or "that"</note> because for the same reason that other animals are carried downward not in a perpendicular manner but transversely and windingly, the hawk alone takes a direct path to the lower regions.

It signifies **Excellence**, because it seems to excel over all other birds.

It signifies **Blood**, because they say this animal does not drink water, but blood.

Finally, it signifies **Victory**, because it seems to conquer all other winged creatures. For when it sees itself oppressed by the power of a stronger creature...

...[the hawk signifies victory] by turning itself backward in the air so that its talons are facing upward, while its wings and tail are turned downward; and since the bird attacking it cannot do the same, the hawk easily puts it to flight and secures victory for itself.

Furthermore, the hawk is used to represent the **soul**, according to the interpretation of its name. Indeed, among the Egyptians the hawk is called **baieth** <note>original: *baieth*. This is an ancient attempt at etymology; in Coptic, *bai* means "soul" and *ēt* can refer to the "heart."</note>, a name which, if divided, signifies "soul" and "heart." For *Bai* is the soul, and *eth* is the heart. According to the opinion of the Egyptians, the heart is the vessel <note>Latin: *ambitus*, meaning the enclosure or perimeter</note> of the soul. Thus, the composition of the name signifies "the soul given to the heart." For this reason, because of the natural sympathy it shares with the soul, the hawk does not drink water at all, but only blood, which is what the soul itself is nourished by. <note>In ancient physiology, the blood was often considered the physical medium or "food" of the soul/vital spirit.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hawk's symbolism, moving from its association with the soul to its representation of the deities Mars and Venus. It also introduces the crow as a symbol of marital harmony.</meta>

<header>HORUS APOLLO</header>

...they depict hawks, comparing the male to **Mars** <note>original: *Ares*</note> and the female to **Venus** <note>original: *Aphrodite*</note>. This is because, unlike other female animals that do not always submit to the male for every mating, the female hawk does. Indeed, she is forced thirty times a day, and even after she has withdrawn, if she is called by the male, she submits again. For this reason, the Egyptians call every woman who is obedient to her husband a "Venus," while they do not use this title for those who are disobedient. This is also why they dedicated the hawk to the **Sun**; for just like the solar cycle <note>referring to the thirty days of a standard Egyptian month</note>, the hawk renders the number thirty in its mating with the female.

In another way of representing **Mars** and **Venus**, they paint two **crows** <note>original: *korōnas*</note> to signify a husband and wife. This is because this animal lays two eggs, from which a male and a female must be hatched. If it should happen—which is rare—that two males or two females are born,

the males, after mating with the females, do not mix with any other crow; nor indeed does the female crow mate with another until death, but they remain solitary after being separated from their mate. Therefore, when people encounter a single crow, they take it as an omen <note>interpretive note: a sign of future events, specifically "widowhood" or bad luck in this context</note>, as they have met an animal living in widowhood. Because of this harmony between the birds, even to this day, the Greeks say in their wedding ceremonies, "Out with the crow, maiden!" <note>original: *ek-kori kori korōnen*. This was a ritual cry at Greek weddings, intended to drive away the "solitary crow" of widowhood and ensure the bride’s lifelong companionship.</note>, though they do so without knowing the reason.

### 7. In what way they represent Mars and Venus.

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of animals as symbols for deities and human behaviors. It concludes the section on the hawk’s association with the Sun and Venus, then introduces the crow as a primary symbol of marital fidelity and the origins of specific Greek wedding customs.</meta>

# HIEROGLYPHICA.

Unlike other living creatures, the female does not submit to the male at every encounter, as is the case in the hawk species. In this genus, even if the female is mated thirty times in a day by the male, if she is called after leaving, she obeys again. For this reason, the Egyptians call every woman who is obedient to her husband a **Venus** <note>original Latin: *Venerem*, referring to the goddess of love, Aphrodite</note>; but those who are not obedient to their husband's word, they do not call by this name. And this was surely the reason why they consecrated the hawk to the **Sun**; for just like the Sun, the hawk completes the number thirty <note>The number thirty represents the days of a standard Egyptian month in the solar calendar</note> in its mating with the female.

When representing **Mars** <note>original: *Martem*, referring to the god of war, Ares</note> and **Venus** in another way, they paint two **crows**, representing a male and a female; because this animal usually lays a pair of eggs, from which a male and a female are born. When, however—though it happens only rarely—it lays either two male or two female eggs, the males joined in marriage to the females never turn to another crow, nor does the female herself, as long as she lives, seek another male; but once separated from their mate, they always live alone thereafter. Hence, when people encounter a single crow, they interpret it as an omen for themselves of a widowed life, because they have met an animal living in widowhood. Consequently, to this day, because of the great harmony between these birds, the Greeks are accustomed to use that word at weddings—though they may be unaware of its origin—"*Ekkori!*" <note>original Greek: *ἐκκορὶ*; a ritual cry translated as "Away with the crow!", used to ward off the ill omen of widowhood from the new couple</note>, for they indeed call the crow *kori* <note>original: *κορὶ*</note>.

### How they represent an only-begotten being.

<margin>the only one of its kind</margin>
To signify an **only-begotten** being <note>original Greek: *μονογενές* (monogenes)</note>, or a **birth**, or a **father**, or the **world**, or a **man**, they paint a **scarab beetle** <note>original: *κάνθαρον* (kantharon)</note>. It represents "only-begotten" because the creature is self-generated <note>the ancients believed scarabs were born without mothers</note>, not being gestated by a female. <margin>only of such a kind</margin> For its birth is uniquely of this sort: when the male wishes to procreate, he takes the dung of an ox and fashions a spherical shape, very much like the form of the **world**. He rolls this with his hind legs from east to west, while he himself looks toward the east, in order to replicate the movement of the world. For the world itself is carried from east to west <note>referring to the apparent daily motion of the sky</note>, while the course of the stars is from west to east. Having buried this sphere in the earth, he leaves it there for twenty-eight days—the same number of days in which the **moon** circles the twelve signs of the zodiac. During this time, the race of scarabs is animated with life. On the

<summary>This page explains the symbolic significance of the scarab beetle as a representation of self-creation, the father, and the world, detailing its unique reproductive cycle linked to the heavens.</summary>
<keywords>scarab beetle, only-begotten, self-generation, cosmology, lunar cycle, Egyptian symbolism, Horapollo</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the scarab beetle's life cycle and its symbolic meanings, picking up from the previous page's discussion of the twenty-eight day gestation period.</meta>

# HORAPOLLO

<margin>the thirty-day period</margin>
<margin>celestial</margin>
On the twenty-ninth day, having opened the sphere, he casts it into the water; for he considers this day to be the conjunction of the **moon** and the **sun**, and furthermore, the birth of the world. When it is opened in the water, living creatures emerge—that is, the **scarab beetles**. They use the beetle to signify **birth** for the reason already mentioned. They use it for **father**, because the scarab takes its origin from a father alone <note>ancient naturalists believed the scarab was exclusively male and self-generating</note>. It signifies the **world**, because it fashions its birth in a world-like shape <note>the spherical dung ball</note>. It signifies **man**, because no female gender is ever born among them. 

There are three forms of these scarab beetles. The first is **cat-shaped** and **rayed** <note>original Greek: *αἰλουρόμορφος καὶ ἀκτινωτή* (ailouromorphos kai aktinote)</note>, which they dedicated to the **sun** because of a symbolic connection. For they say that the male cat changes the pupils of its eyes according to the courses of the sun: for they expand toward the rising of the god, become round at midday, and appear more faint as the sun is about to set. This is why the statue of the god in **Heliopolis** <note>the "City of the Sun" in Egypt</note> is in the form of a cat. Furthermore, every scarab has thirty **fingers** <note>term: *δακτύλους* (daktylous) - referring here to the segments of the beetle's tarsi or legs</note>, corresponding to the thirty days of the month in which the rising sun completes its own course. 

The second type is the **two-horned** and **bull-shaped** beetle, which was consecrated to the **moon**; because of this, the children of the Egyptians say that the **Bull** in the heavens <note>the constellation Taurus</note> is the exaltation of this goddess. 

The third type is **one-horned** and of a **peculiar form**, which they considered to belong to **Hermes** <note>the Greek name for the Egyptian god Thoth</note>, just as the **ibis** bird does.

# HIEROGLYPHICA.

->B<-

<meta>catchword: folis (likely a typesetter's error for "solis," referring to the sun)</meta>

To signify a **father**, or the **world**, or a **man**, they paint a **Scarab beetle**. It represents an **only-begotten** being <note>original Latin: *unigenitum*</note> because this animal is self-generated, as it is not first formed and carried within the womb of a female; for its birth alone is of this unique kind. When the male wishes to procreate offspring, having found ox dung, he fashions a small ball from it in the same shape as the world. When he has rolled it from the east to the west <note>original: *ab ortu in occasum*</note> with his feet turned backward, he himself turns back toward the east, so that he might produce and represent a shape most similar to the celestial orb. For the orb itself is driven by the **East Wind** <term>Subsolanus: the wind "under the sun," blowing from the east</term> toward the **Southwest Wind** <term>Africus: the wind blowing from the direction of the African coast</term>. Yet the course of the stars is in the opposite direction, from the southwest toward the east. 

Therefore, he places this ball, buried in the earth, for **twenty-eight days**. For in just as many days the **Moon** traverses the twelve signs of the **zodiac**, under which the genus of scarabs remains for all that time, becoming animated and receiving the seed of life. On the **twenty-ninth day**, however, he casts the opened ball into the water (for he considers that day to be the one on which the **Moon** meets with the **Sun**, and furthermore, the birth of the world occurs). While it is being opened in the water, living creatures emerge—that is, the scarabs. 

They use the beetle for **Birth** for the reason already mentioned. They use it for **Father** because the scarab has its origin from a father alone. They use it for the **World** because its generation and offspring take on the shape of the world. Finally, they use it for **Man** because there is no female among their kind. 

Furthermore, there are three forms of Scarabs: the first has the appearance of **cats** <note>This refers to the *Ateuchus sacer* or sacred scarab, whose head structure was thought to resemble rays or a cat's face</note> and is distinguished by what look like rays, which they dedicated to the **Sun** because of a certain symbolic meaning. For they say that the male cat changes the pupils of its eyes according to the changing state and course of the sun...

Since she is occupied with the chicks and their food, and lest a lack of food befall the infants, she cuts open her own thigh and allows her children to partake of her blood, so that they do not perish from a lack of nourishment. 

They use it for **Athena** and **Hera**, because it seems to the Egyptians that Athena has received the upper hemisphere of the heaven, and Hera the lower; hence they <margin>indicate</margin> also refer to the heaven <note>The sky or firmament</note> in the masculine, but in the feminine "the heaven"; because the birth of the sun and moon and the rest of the stars is completed within it, which is the work of a female. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the vulture hieroglyph from the previous page, concluding the Greek explanation of why the bird represents "motherhood" and "heavenly things," and beginning a new Latin section (Chapter 10) that systematizes these meanings and elaborates on the biological myths of the vulture.</meta>

[Egypt]ians; thus, when they wish to signify a **mother**, they depict a vulture; for it is the mother of female animals. And for **two drachmas**, because among the Egyptians two lines represent a unit <note>original: *μονὰς* (monas), the mathematical principle of "one" or "unity"</note>; and the unit is the origin of every number. Quite reasonably, then, when they wish to indicate two drachmas, they write a vulture, since she is thought to be both a mother and an origin, just as the unit is.

### 10. What they indicate by depicting a vulture.

Furthermore, when signifying a **mother**, or **sight**, or a **boundary**, or **knowledge of the future**, or a **year**, or **heaven**, or the **merciful**, or **Athena** <note>The Greek goddess of wisdom, associated by the author with the Egyptian Neith</note>, or **Juno** <note>The Roman queen of the gods, associated here with Mut or Hera</note>, or **two drachmas**, they paint a vulture. 

A **mother** indeed, because in this genus of animals there is no male. They are produced in this manner: When a vulture burns with the desire to conceive, opening her womb to the **North Wind** <note>original: *Boreas*</note>, she is impregnated by it for five days, during which she takes neither food nor drink, entirely intent on the procreation of her offspring. There are, furthermore, other kinds of vultures which indeed conceive from the wind, but whose eggs are only for eating, and not likewise suited for receiving and forming offspring. But for those vultures whose mating is not merely "wind-swept" <note>original: *subuentaneus*, referring to "wind-eggs" that are sterile</note> and ineffective, their eggs are especially fit for begetting and raising a brood.

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the vulture hieroglyph, moving from its representation of "sight" into its roles as a symbol for boundaries, prophecy, the calendar year, and unexpected mercy.</meta>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

[...Indeed, when] the Sun is rising, it looks toward the west, and when it is setting, it looks toward the east; and from a sufficiently long distance, it procures the food which may be useful to it.

It signifies a **boundary**, because when the time for concluding a war is at hand, it predefines and circumscribes the place where the battle is to be fought by approaching it seven days beforehand.

It represents **foreknowledge of the future** <note>original: *Praesagium*</note>, both for the reason already mentioned and also because it is accustomed to turn itself toward that part of the army where the greater slaughter will occur, setting aside food for itself from the corpses and providently reserving it for the future. For this reason, ancient kings would send out scouts so that they might learn toward which part of the battle line the vultures looked; from this, they would conclude who was to be defeated and destroyed in a deadly slaughter.

Furthermore, it represents a **year**, since this animal distributes those three hundred and sixty-five days <note>The length of the Egyptian civil year</note> by which the year is completed in this manner: for one hundred and twenty days it remains pregnant, for just as many it nourishes its chicks, and for the remaining one hundred and twenty days it takes care of itself, neither carrying a womb nor being occupied with feeding its offspring, but merely preparing itself for another conception. Those five days of the year which remain <note>These are the five "epagomenal" or "intercalary" days added to the 360-day Egyptian calendar to align it with the solar year</note> it spends on the pressure of the wind and on mating, as has already been stated.

It signifies the **merciful**, which to some will perhaps seem most strange, especially since this animal pursues and kills other creatures with a very hostile spirit. But they were moved to indicate this by the image of a vulture because, during those 120 days in which it raises its young, it almost never flies away, but applies all its care and concern to feeding them... <note>The text ends mid-word; the full Latin word is *adhibeat*, meaning "to apply" or "to bestow."</note>

<page-num>23</page-num>
# HIEROGLYPHICA.

...she provides. But if at that time food is not available to sustain them, she offers her own thigh, cut open, for them to suckle, bestowing her blood so that they are not consumed by the scarcity of food and starvation <note>This myth of the vulture’s self-sacrifice is a precursor to the medieval legend of the pelican feeding its young with its own blood</note>.

It signifies **Minerva and Juno** <note>original: *Mineruam ac Iunonem*</note>, because it appears to the Egyptians that Minerva occupies the upper hemisphere of the sky, and Juno the lower. This is also the reason they consider it absurd to speak of "heaven" in the masculine gender; for since the birth of the Sun, the Moon, and the other stars is completed within it, it is surely a feminine function. Furthermore, the race of vultures, as I have already explained, consists only of females. For this reason, the Egyptians apply the vulture to any living creature of the female sex, as the leader and primary example of that sex. From this—not to run through every single instance and become too long-winded—the Egyptians signify every goddess by the vulture.

In addition, when they wish to suggest a **mother**, they paint a vulture, for the mother is of a feminine nature. They call the heaven "Mother" <note>original: *μητέρα* (mētéra)</note> (for as I said, it does not please them to call the heaven <note>original: *οὐρανὸν* (ouranón)</note> by a masculine noun), because the generation of all these things proceeds from it.

Finally, it signifies **two drachmas**, because among the Egyptians, two lines are a "unity." Unity is the source and principle of any number. Therefore, with the best of reasons, when they wish to indicate two drachmas, they paint a vulture, because it seems to be the author of its own generation—both mother and principle—just as unity is.

### How they depict Hephaestus.

When they depict **Hephaestus** <note>original: *Ἥφαισον* (Hēphaiston); the Greek god of fire, smithing, and craftsmanship</note>, they paint a **beetle** and a **vulture**. For **Athena** <note>original: *ἀθηνᾶν* (Athēnan); the goddess of wisdom and war</note>, they paint a vulture and a beetle. For it seems to them that the world is composed of both a masculine and a feminine nature. In the case of Athena, they include the vulture; for these are the only gods among them who are considered both male and female <note>original: *ἀρσενοθήλεις* (arsenothēleis); meaning androgynous or possessing the characteristics of both sexes</note>.

<margin>11</margin>
### How they signify Vulcan.

When indicating **Vulcan** <note>the Roman name for Hephaestus</note>, they paint a beetle and a vulture. For **Minerva** <note>the Roman name for Athena</note>, however, they paint a vulture and a beetle. For it appears to them that the world consists of male and female. Moreover, they add the vulture to Minerva, because these alone among the gods, in their view, are male and female at the same time. <note>Horapollo explains that because the beetle was thought to be only male and the vulture only female, combining them created a symbol of a deity that transcends a single gender.</note>

### What they signify by drawing a star.

To signify a **cosmic god** <note>original: *θεὸν δὲ ἐγκόσμιον* (theon de egkosmion); this refers to the divine presence within the physical order of the universe</note>, or **fate**, or the **number five**, they paint a **star**. They use it for God because the providence of God assigns the victory by which the movement of the stars and the entire universe is performed. For it seems to them that without God, nothing at all could exist. They use it for **fate** <note>original: *εἱμαρμένην* (heimarmenēn)</note> because fate is established from the arrangement of the stars. And they use it for the **number five** because, despite the multitude of stars in the heavens, only five of them are in motion <note>This refers to the five planets visible to the naked eye—Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—which were known as "wandering stars" in antiquity</note>, and these 
<margin>and fifth</margin>
perform the governance of the world.

### What they imply by painting a star.

<margin>12.</margin>

Indeed, they paint God because the providence of God [brings] victory... <note>The Latin text here mirrors the Greek explanation of the star as a symbol of divine order and planetary motion.</note>

<header>THE HORUS APOLLO</header>

decrees and commands, by which the movement of the stars and the entire universe is carried out. For they believe that without God nothing at all can exist. Moreover, they signify **fate** <note>original Latin: *Fatum*</note> because it is established by the course and arrangement of the stars. And they use it for the **number five** <note>original Latin: *Quinarium numerum*</note> because, although there are very many and indeed innumerable stars in the heavens, only five of these <note>referring to the five planets visible to the naked eye: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> by their motion effect the most beautiful distribution and governance of the world.

# HIEROGLYPHICA.

[The baboon] does not see, nor does he eat. He is distressed, bowed down toward the ground, as if mourning the abduction of the moon <note>The "abduction" or "snatching" of the moon refers to the new moon phase when the moon is invisible.</note>. The female, in addition to being unable to see and <margin>\* and also</margin> suffering the same as the male, <gloss>from</gloss> <margin>\* From her own nature</margin> <margin>Missing in the old manuscript</margin> bleeds from her body. For this reason, even until now, baboons are kept in the temples, so that from them the hour of the conjunction <note>The astronomical meeting of the sun and moon.</note> of the sun and moon may be known. 

They use the baboon to represent the **inhabited world** because they say there were seventy-two ancient regions of the world. <gloss>And</gloss> those baboons raised in the temples and receiving care do not die all at once in a single day like other animals; instead, a part of them dies each day and is buried by the priests, while the rest of the body remains in its natural state. This continues until the seventy-two days are completed, at which point it dies entirely. 

They use it to represent **letters** <margin>Egyptian</margin> because there is a lineage of baboons who understand Egyptian writing. Therefore, when a baboon is first brought into a temple, the priest places a writing tablet before it, along with a reed pen and ink, testing whether it is from that lineage that knows letters and whether it writes. Furthermore, the animal is dedicated to Hermes <note>The author uses the Greek name Hermes for the Egyptian god Thoth, who was the patron of scribes and the inventor of hieroglyphs.</note>, who shares in all forms of writing. 

They use it to represent a **priest** because <margin>perhaps nature</margin> by nature the baboon does not eat fish; indeed, it is said it will not even eat <margin>fishy</margin> bread flavored with fish, just as the priests abstain from such things. It is also <margin>and</margin> born circumcised, which is a practice the priests also observe. 

Finally, it represents **anger** because this animal is more high-spirited and prone to anger than any other.

### What they signify by depicting a Baboon.

To represent the **Moon**, or the **orb of the earth**, or **letters**, or a **priest**, or **anger**, or **swimming**, they depict a **Baboon** <note>The "Cynocephalus" or dog-headed baboon was sacred to Thoth, the Egyptian god of wisdom and the moon.</note>. They use it for the Moon because this animal has a certain sympathy with the meeting <note>The astronomical conjunction or "congress" of the sun and moon.</note> of the Goddess, by which it is naturally affected. For when the Moon, for a period of time, meets with the Sun and remains dark and without light, the male baboon does not look at anything, nor does he eat; instead, with his face cast down toward the earth, he is full of indignation, as if mourning the snatching away of the Moon, and he is saddened. The female baboon, however, besides the fact that she does not turn her eyes anywhere and suffers the same distress as the male, additionally discharges blood from her womb. And for this reason, even to this day, baboons are kept and fed in the sacred temples, so that from them the exact time of the conjunction of the Sun and Moon may be known. 

Moreover, they use it to represent the **orb of the earth** because they say that in ancient times there were seventy-two regions of the inhabited world. These creatures, if they are diligently fed and cared for in the temples, do not die all at once in a single day like other animals; rather, a certain part of them dies and withers away each day and is buried by the priests, while the rest of the body meanwhile persists in its own nature...

# HIEROGLYPHICS

...th <note>Completing "earth" from the previous page; original Latin: *tellur-te*</note>, and this for seventy-two days, which having been completed, it then utterly perishes. They depict **Letters**, because among the Egyptians there is a certain kinship with a breed of **baboons** <note>original Latin: *cynocephalorum*, literally "dog-headed ones"</note> who know how to read and write. For this reason, as soon as a baboon is brought into the sacred temple, the priest places a writing tablet before it, together with a reed pen and ink, specifically to put it to the test to see if it belongs to that breed of baboons who are skilled in letters; and so, it writes <note>original: *pingit*, literally "paints," referring to the brush-like strokes of Egyptian scribal work</note> letters upon that tablet. Furthermore, this animal is dedicated to **Mercury** <note>The Roman name for Thoth, the Egyptian god of wisdom, who was frequently depicted as a baboon or an ibis</note>, who is the sharer of all learning.

A **sacrificing priest** is signified because the baboon by its nature abhors the eating of fish; and even if it should happen to feed on fish at times, the animal is nevertheless not sluggish or given over to torpor, just like the foreign priests <note>original Latin: *externi sacerdotes*. In this context, it refers to the Egyptian priests who maintained strict dietary purity, distinguishing them from "external" or common people</note>. Add to this that it is born **circumcised**, a condition which the priests take great care to observe and carry out with the highest zeal.

They use it to represent **Anger**, because this animal, more than all other creatures, is extremely irascible and prone to indignation. 

Furthermore, it represents **Swimming**, because while other animals contract dirt and squalor from frequent swimming, this animal alone reaches the place it intended by swimming, and yet it is not changed from its natural color or stained by any filth.

## How they write the rising of the moon.

<margin>by its posture</margin>
When they wish to represent the rising of the **Moon**, they once again paint a **baboon** <note>original Greek: *kynokephalon*, literally "dog-headed one"</note> in the following posture: standing upright with its hands raised toward heaven, and wearing a crown <note>original Greek: *basileion*, referring to a royal diadem or insignia</note> upon its head. They draw this figure to represent the rising [of the moon] because the baboon is seen to be, as it were, praying to the goddess, since both the animal and the moon share in the nature of light.

## How they represent the two equinoxes.

<margin>two</margin>
When they wish to signify the **Equinoxes** <note>original Greek: *isēmerias*, the two times a year when day and night are of equal length</note>, they again paint a seated **baboon** <note>original Greek: *kynokephalon*</note>. For during the two equinoxes of the year, twelve times during the day—once for each **hour** <margin>This is missing in the old edition</margin>—it urinates; and it does the very same thing during the two nights. Therefore, not without reason, the Egyptians carve a seated baboon on their **water-clocks** <term>hydrologia: a clepsydra, an ancient timepiece that measured the passage of hours by the regulated flow of water</term>. They make the water flow out from its private member, because as I said before, the animal signifies the twelve hours of the equinox. And so that the **water** <margin>water / hole / exists</margin> does not flow out too widely through the **mechanisms** by which the water is released into the clock, nor again too narrowly, for there is a specific need for both [to be avoided]. <note>The text here describes the technical calibration of a water-clock, suggesting the baboon's "member" acted as the spout or regulator of the flow.</note>

### How the two equinoxes are shown.

<margin>16</margin> Again, when signifying the **equinoxes**, they paint the same animal, a seated **baboon** <note>original Latin: *cynocephalum*</note>. For during the two equinoxes of the year, it passes urine twelve times a day—certainly once every hour—and it does the very same thing at night. Therefore, not without merit, the Egyptians carve a seated baboon on their **water-clocks** <term>hydrologiis: ancient devices, also known as clepsydrae, that measured time by the regulated flow of water</term>, from whose member the water flows out. This is because, as I have already said, the animal signifies the twelve hours into which the day and night are equally divided at the time of the equinox. Furthermore, lest that hole—constructed with sharp and clever skill through which the water flows and is discharged into the **clock** <term>horologium: any device used to tell time; here specifically the water-clock</term>—should become either wider or narrower (for both variations are of great consequence: if it is wider, since it pours out water with great speed, it does not correctly achieve the measure and dimension of the hour; but if it is narrower, it releases the water-duct more slowly and gradually than is proper), they devised this remedy: they shave off all the hair down to the tail, and according to its thick-

## How they signify anger.

When they wish to signify **anger** <note>original Greek: *thymon*; this term encompasses spirit, courage, and passionate rage</note>, they paint a **lion**. For the animal has a large head and fiery pupils, and its face is round, with hair radiating around it in imitation of the **sun**. For this reason, they place lions beneath the throne of **Horus** <note>the Egyptian sky god, often represented as a falcon or a child, associated here with the sun and kingship</note>, demonstrating the animal’s symbolic relationship to the god. Horus is indeed the sun, so named because he rules over the **seasons** <note>original Greek: *hōrōn*; Horapollo is using a Greek pun to link the name of the Egyptian god Horus with the Greek word for hours or seasons, *hōrai*</note>.

<margin>16</margin>

## How they signify the soul, or
## excellence of the spirit.

When they wish to signify the **soul** <note>Latin: *animum*; in this context, it refers to the vital spirit, courage, or the seat of emotions</note>, they paint a **lion**. For this animal has a large head and fiery pupils, and a round face with hair scattered all around it in the manner of rays, in imitation of the **Sun**. For this reason, they place lions beneath the throne of **Horus** <note>the Egyptian god of the sky and kingship</note>—that is, the Sun—demonstrating the very great likeness of this animal to the god. Among them, the Sun is called Horus because he presides over the **hours** <note>original Latin: *horis*; the author is highlighting a linguistic pun where the name of the god Horus is linked to the Greek word for hours or seasons, *hōrai*</note>.

### How they write "strength."

When writing **strength** <note>original Greek: *alkēn*; meaning physical prowess, valor, or defensive force</note>, they paint the **foreparts** <note>the front half of the body</note> of a lion, because these members of its body are the most powerful.

### How they denote strength.
When denoting **strength** <note>original Latin: *robur*; implying physical power, hardiness, or the strength of oak</note>, they paint the **front limbs** of a lion, because these parts of its body are the most robust of all.

### How they write "the vigilant one."
<margin>*</margin>
When they write of a **vigilant person** <note>original Greek: *egrēgorota*; referring to someone who is awake, alert, or watchful</note>, or even a **guardian**, they 
<margin>⊕</margin>
paint the **head** of a lion. This is because the lion, while **awake**, closes its eyes; but while **sleeping**, it keeps them open <note>This was a common ancient belief in natural history, famously repeated by writers like Pliny the Elder, suggesting lions were perpetual sentinels</note>, which is a sign of guarding. For this reason, they have symbolically placed lions as **guardians** on the locks of sacred temples.

->How they represent the rising of the Nile.<-

<margin>
1 with a lion
2 the god makes
3 remaining
4 the ancients
5 and in places full of wine, the animal
6 heart
</margin>

When they wish to signify the **rising of the Nile**—which they call *Noun* <note>original: *noun*; likely referring to the Egyptian word for 'water' or the primordial waters, 'Nun'</note> in the Egyptian language, which when translated means **"New"**—they sometimes depict a **lion**, sometimes **three large water-jars** <note>original: *hydrias*; large ceramic vessels for carrying water</note>, and at other times **heaven and earth gushing forth water**. 

They use a **lion** as a symbol because when the **sun** <note>original: *helios*</note> enters the constellation of **Leo** <note>original: *Leonta*; the Lion</note>, it causes the **rising of the Nile** to increase. Indeed, while the sun **remains** in this zodiac sign, the volume of the "new" water often overflows to double its size. 

For this reason, the overseers of royal works <note>original: *basilikon ergon*; state-appointed architects or engineers</note> used to fashion **water-spouts** <note>original: *choledras*; specifically the gutters or gargoyles on temples</note> and the inlets for sacred springs in the **shape of lions**. This custom has been preserved even to this day as a prayer for an abundance of moisture.

As for the **three jars**, or the depiction of heaven and earth pouring out water: they liken the jar to a **heart** <note>original: *kardia*</note> that possesses a **tongue** <note>original: *glossa*</note>. They use the heart because, in their view, it is the ruling organ <note>original: *hegemonikon*; the governing or commanding part of the soul in Stoic and ancient thought</note> that controls the tongue.

<vocab>Nile, rising/inundation, lion, jar, sun, zodiac sign, water-spout, royal, heart, tongue</vocab>

It also describes the use of lion-headed water spouts in architecture and the symbolic link between water jars and the human heart.</summary>

<margin>In Egypt</margin>
...of the body. Just as the **Nile** is established as the ruler among the Egyptians. And they use a **tongue** <note>original: γλώσσῃ (glōssē)</note>, because this part always exists in moisture, and they call it the **mother of existence**. By force and superiority, and being neither more nor less, the peak of the rising occurs in this very month. Since they have designated it from the **Egyptian land** itself, because this land is naturally productive of water. Another theory is that it comes from the **Ocean** <note>original: ὠκεανοῦ (ōkeanou); the ancient belief that a great river-ocean encircled the world</note>. For indeed, water arrives in Egypt from there at the time of the rising. A third theory is that it comes from **rains** which occur in the southern parts of **Ethiopia** at the time of the Nile’s rising. That Egypt itself generates the water can be learned from this fact: in the rest of the world’s climates, the flooding of rivers occurs in the **winter** due to continuous rains. But the land of the Egyptians alone—
<margin><unclear>... ... ...</unclear></margin>
since it lies in the center of the inhabited world, just like the so-called **pupil** <note>original: κόρη (korē); literally "maiden," the common Greek term for the pupil of the eye</note> in the eye—brings about the rising of the Nile for itself in the **summer**.

### 20 How they signify the rise and overflow of the Nile.

Furthermore, to signify the **inundation of the Nile**—which they call *Nun* <note>original: Nun</note> in the Egyptian name, which if you interpret it, sounds like **"New"**—they sometimes depict a **lion**, sometimes **three large water-jars**, and at other times **heaven and earth gushing forth a bounty of water**. They depict a **lion** because when the **Sun** enters the constellation of **Leo** <note>the Lion</note>, it creates a more ample inundation of the Nile. For as long as...

<summary>This page explains various ancient theories for the Nile's summer flood and justifies the use of the lion and the tongue as symbols for moisture and the "mother of existence." It famously compares Egypt’s central geographical importance to the pupil of the human eye.</summary>

<keywords>Nile inundation, Leo (constellation), Nun (New Water), Ethiopia, eye pupil metaphor, Egyptian geography, water-jars</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and symbolic explanation of the Nile’s annual flood, specifically focusing on the astrological timing (the Sun in Leo) and the anatomical symbols—the heart and tongue—used to represent the river's life-giving power. It provides a three-fold explanation for the source of the floodwaters.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
-># HIEROGLYPHICA.<-

<sig>C iiij</sig>

For when the **Sun** lingers in this sign <note>The zodiac sign of Leo</note>, the water of the **Nile** often increases to double its usual volume. For this reason, those who oversee the sacred works are accustomed to fashioning the pipes and channels of the sacred fountains in the shape of a **Lion**. Therefore, even to this day, when they pour out prayers for an excessive inundation, they are accustomed to using the sign of the **Lion**. 

Moreover, they represent three **water jars** <note>original: hydrias; large vessels used for carrying water</note>, or heaven and earth which pour out an abundance of water, making a symbol very similar to the **Nile**: a **heart** provided with a **tongue**. They use a **heart** because they consider this the foremost and most powerful part of the entire body, just as the **Nile** is the leader and prince of all **Egypt**. They use a **tongue**, however, because while it delights in always being in moisture, they additionally call it the "mother" and the "cause" of the state of things. 

Furthermore, they paint three **water jars**, and neither more nor less, because according to their opinion there is a triple cause that produces the inundation. One cause they ascribe to the **Egyptian land**, which produces water from within itself. The second they ascribe to the **Ocean** <note>The ancients often believed a Great Ocean-River encircled the world and fed its major rivers</note>, from which the water surges into Egypt at the time of the flood. The third they ascribe to the **rains**, which occur in the southern parts of **Ethiopia** at the time when the **Nile** swells. 

That Egypt produces water from itself is easy to perceive from this: while in other regions of the globe rivers usually overflow in **winter** due to heavy and constant rains, the region of the **Egyptians** alone—since it is like the **navel** <term>umbilicus orbis; the metaphorical center or "birth-point" of the world</term> and center of the whole world, no different than the **pupil** in an eye—creates the inundations of the Nile for itself in the **summer**.

<vocab>Nile, Egypt, inundation, Lion, water jar, navel of the world, heart, tongue, Ethiopia</vocab>

<summary>This page explains why the Lion and symbols of the heart and tongue represent the Nile's summer flood, citing the river's central importance to Egypt's survival. It offers three scientific theories for the flood: groundwater, oceanic surge, and Ethiopian rainfall.</summary>

<keywords>Nile flood, Leo zodiac, Egyptian irrigation, sacred fountains, anatomy symbolism, Ethiopia, geography, umbilicus orbis</keywords>
