<image-desc>A woodcut illustration enclosed in a rectangular frame. It depicts a burning censer (a thymiaterium or acerra) resting on a stone block or altar. ### How they represent Egypt.

When they represent **Egypt**, they paint a **burning censer** <note>original: thymiaterium; a vessel used in antiquity for burning incense during sacrifices</note> and a **heart** above it. They do this to show that just as the heart of a **jealous man** <note>The author uses "jealous" (Greek: zelotypou) to evoke a state of being "on fire" with intense passion or heat</note> is always burning, so **Egypt**, because of its inherent heat, always gives life to the things that exist within or around it.

---

<margin>21</margin>
### How they designate Egypt.

When designating **Egypt**, they paint a **burning censer** <note>original: acerra; a Latin term for an incense box or altar burner</note> and a **heart** above. This suggests that just as the heart of the **jealous man** is in a perpetual state of heat and flame, so **Egypt**, because it is excessively hot, continually gives life to and procreates those things that are within it.

### How they represent a man who has not traveled abroad from his homeland.

To represent a man who has never traveled away from his homeland, they paint the **head of a donkey** <note>original: onokephalon; literally "donkey-head"</note>. They do this because such a person neither hears any histories <note>In this context, the Greek "historia" refers to accounts, inquiries, or stories of the world's events</note> nor perceives the things that happen in foreign lands.

---

<margin>22</margin>
->How they represent a man who has never set out<-
->on a journey away from his fatherland.<-

Signifying a man who has never traveled, having never left his native soil, they paint a **Donkey-head** <note>original: Onocephalū</note>. This is because he neither hears any history, nor knows those things which take place among foreign nations.

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO</header>
<meta>Woodcut illustration, Greek text followed by Latin translation. This is Chapter 23 of Horapollo's Hieroglyphica.</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts two disembodied human heads in profile, set against a background of rolling hills and stylized clouds. On the left is the head of a bearded man facing right (inward). On the right is the head of a woman with hair tied back, facing right (outward).</image-desc>

->How they represent a protective charm.<-

When they wish to write the symbol for a **protective charm** <note>original: "phylaktērion," from which we get the word "phylactery"—an amulet or charm worn for protection</note>, they paint **two human heads**, the one of a male looking inward, and the female looking outward. For in this way, they say, no **demonic spirits** <note>original: "daimoniōn"; in this context, these are supernatural entities or influences that could cause harm</note> will touch them; because even without the use of written letters, they protect themselves <note>original: "phylaktēriazousi"</note> with these two heads.

---

<margin>23</margin> ->How they represent a safeguard and defense.<-

Wishing to indicate a **safeguard** and a **remedy**, they paint **two human heads**, one of a male looking inward, the other of a female looking outward. For they assert that in this way, no demon <note>original: "dęmonem"</note> can invade. Thus, even without letters, they defend themselves from every danger <note>original: "discrimine"</note> and snare with these two heads.

<vocab>protective charm, head, demonic spirit, safeguard, remedy, demon, protection, Horapollo</vocab>

<summary>This page describes a hieroglyphic symbol consisting of a male and female head used as a protective charm to ward off demons and spiritual dangers. It emphasizes that the visual symbol itself holds power even without written words.</summary>
<keywords>protection, amulet, phylactery, male and female heads, demons, safeguards, Egyptian symbolism, Horapollo</keywords>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>HIEROGLYPHICS.</header>
<meta>catchword: to fail along with</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration set within a rectangular border, depicting a frog or toad in profile facing right. It is situated in a marshy habitat with tall aquatic plants, specifically bulrushes or cattails (Typha latifolia), growing behind it.</image-desc>

### How they depict an unformed man.

When they represent an **unformed man** <note>original: "aplaston," meaning something not yet molded or fully shaped, like raw clay</note>, they paint a **frog**; because its birth is brought about from the **mud of the river**. For this reason, it is sometimes seen with one part of its body being a frog, while the rest remains **earth-like** in appearance, so that as the river *recedes* <note>original: "ekliponti"</note>, the creature also *fails along with it* <note>original: "synekleipein"; the author suggests the creature's life is tied directly to the moisture of the Nile</note>.

<margin>24</margin>
### In what way they show a man who is still imperfect, and a raw and unformed fetus.

To indicate a man whose limbs are not yet fully **formed in the womb**, they paint a **frog**, because since it is procreated from the mud of the river, sometimes one part of it is indeed a frog, while the other appears to be something earthy: so much so that when the river **fails** <note>original: "deficiente," referring to the drying up or receding of the Nile</note>, the creature itself also **perishes**.

<vocab>Hieroglyphica, Horapollo, frog, rana, βάτραχος, embryology, spontaneous generation</vocab>

<summary>This page explains how the frog serves as a symbol for an unformed or "incomplete" human, such as a fetus, based on the ancient belief that frogs were spontaneously generated from Nile mud. The text describes a creature caught in mid-transformation, part animal and part earth, which lives and dies according to the river's flow.</summary>

<keywords>frog, unformed man, fetus, Nile mud, spontaneous generation, Horapollo, Egyptian symbolism, biology</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS.
<page-num>45</page-num>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration set in a natural landscape. The scene features a disembodied hand and a disembodied eye floating in the air. ### How they represent speaking.

To write the concept of **speaking** <note>original Greek: "to legein"</note>, they paint a **tongue** and a **bloodshot eye** <note>original: "hyphaimon ophthalmon." Ancient writers believed the eyes were direct reflections of the soul's internal state; a "suffused" or red eye indicated intense emotion or active thought</note>. They assign the primary role in speech to the tongue, but the secondary role to the eyes. <margin>26</margin> For in this way, the **words** <gloss>*</gloss> of the soul are fully established, changing along with its movements—this is even referred to as a "different kind of speech" <note>This suggests that for the Egyptians, facial expressions and gestures were considered a parallel form of language to vocalized words</note> among the Egyptians. To signify speaking in another way, they paint a **tongue** with a **hand** underneath it. They give the primary place of speech to the tongue, but the secondary place to the hand, as the hand **completes** <gloss>*</gloss> <margin>unfolding</margin> the desires of the tongue.

---

->How they represent discourse.<-

<margin>26</margin>
Furthermore, when designating **discourse** <note>original Latin: "sermonem"</note>, they paint a **tongue**, and beneath it a **bloodshot eye**. They assign the primary parts of speech to the tongue, and the secondary parts to the eyes...

<summary>This page describes two hieroglyphic methods for representing "speaking": combining a tongue with a bloodshot eye (representing the soul's expression) or a tongue with a hand (representing the execution of thoughts into action).</summary>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO
<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>HORAPOLLO</header>

...assigning these roles. For in this way, the **discourses** <note>original Latin: "sermones"</note> of the soul itself are perfectly established when they are adapted and varied according to its movements and perceptions; especially since, among the Egyptians, the **soul** <note>original Latin: "animus"</note> is called "another speech." Furthermore, when indicating speech in another way, they paint a **tongue** and a **hand** beneath it. They assign the primary role in speaking to the tongue, but the secondary role to the hand, as the hand carries out the desires of the tongue.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a hilly landscape with rocks, trees, and clouds. In the center of the scene is a rectangular inset containing the Roman numerals "ML XXXX V" (representing the number 1,095).</image-desc>

->How they represent speechlessness.<-

To write the concept of **speechlessness** <note>original Greek: "aphonian"</note>, they write the number **one thousand and ninety-five**. This is the number for a **three-year period** <term>trietēs</term>, since a year consists of 365 days <note>365 days multiplied by 3 years equals 1,095 days</note>. If a child has not spoken within this length of time, it is noted as being **hindered in its tongue** <note>In the ancient world, three years was considered the developmental milestone by which a child should begin to speak; failure to do so was seen as a sign of a physical or spiritual impediment</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on symbols for speech—emphasizing the connection between the soul, the tongue, and the hand—and introduces the numerical symbol (1,095) used to represent speechlessness based on a three-year developmental window.</summary>
<keywords>speech, soul, tongue, hand, speechlessness, aphonia, three-year period, 1095 days, Egyptian symbols, child development</keywords>

<header>HIEROGLYPHICS.</header>

### How silence is represented.

But when signifying **silence** <note>original Latin: "silentium"</note>, they paint the number **1,095**, which is a three-year duration if a year is constituted of three hundred and sixty-five days. Since a young child does not speak within this period of time, it is used to indicate that his **tongue is hindered** <note>Ancient observers noted that children typically begin to form complex speech around age three; before this milestone, they were considered "silent" in a symbolic sense</note>.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration showing a stormy scene. In the center, dark, swirling clouds emit several sharp, radiating beams of light or lightning that strike down toward a choppy sea or river. On the left, a creature (resembling a sphinx or a seated quadruped with a human-like head) sits on a rocky bank looking toward the water.</image-desc>

### How they represent a voice from afar.

When they wish to show a **voice from afar**, which is called ***ouaie*** <note>original Greek: "οὐαιέ"</note> among the Egyptians, they write the **voice of the air**, that is, **thunder** <note>original Greek: "brontēn"</note>, than which nothing is louder, greater, or more powerful.

### How they represent a distant voice.

Wishing to signify a **distant voice**, which is called ***vaeè*** <note>original Latin: "væè"</note> by the Egyptians, they paint a **sound of the air**, that is, **thunder**, than which nothing resonates with more greatness or force. <note>The author connects the natural phenomenon of thunder—the loudest sound in nature—with the concept of a voice that carries over a vast distance.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the symbolic representation of silence via the number of days in three years and introduces thunder as the symbol for a "voice from afar."</summary>

### How they represent ancient origin.

When they write about **ancient origin** <note>original Greek: "archaiogonian"</note>, they paint a **bundle of papyrus**, signifying by this the **first nourishments** <note>In antiquity, the papyrus plant was not only used for writing material but was also a primary food source for the inhabitants of the Nile Delta, who ate the stalks raw, boiled, or roasted</note>; for one could not find the beginning of food or of biological descent <note>The author suggests that because papyrus was seen as the primordial food, it represents the very dawn of human existence, which is lost to time</note>.

---

<margin>29</margin>
### In what way they represent ancient origin.

In noting **ancient origin** <note>original Latin: "antiquam originem"</note>, they paint a **bundle of papyrus** <note>original Latin: "papyri fasciculum"</note>, indicating by this the **first upbringing** <note>original Latin: "educationem"</note>, the beginning of which—just like birth itself—no one can easily discover.

This entry reflects the Greek and Roman fascination with the Nile as the cradle of civilization.</summary>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>HIEROGLYPHICS</header>
<sig>D</sig>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration framed by a single line border. It depicts a landscape featuring a body of water in the foreground. On the left, a jagged cliff rises from the shore, topped with a small tuft of vegetation. In the center distance, there is a large, flat-topped rocky outcrop. ### How they represent taste.

To signify **taste** <note>original Greek: "geusin"</note>, they paint the **front of a mouth** <note>literally "the beginning of the mouth"</note>, since all taste is preserved as far as that point. I speak here of a **complete taste** <note>referring to the full sensory experience of flavor</note>. But to signify **incomplete taste**, they paint a **tongue beneath the teeth**, because all taste is performed through these <note>This distinction suggests the physical mechanics of eating versus the actual perception of flavor</note>.

---

<margin>30</margin>
### In what way they represent taste.

Indicating **taste** <note>original Latin: "gustum"</note>, they paint the **opening of the mouth** <note>original Latin: "oris initium"</note>. For every taste is retained up to that point. I understand this to mean **perfected taste**. For to indicate **imperfect taste**, they paint the **tongue pressed against the teeth**; for by these, all taste is completed <note>The Latin "absolvitur" here suggests the final physical processing of food by the teeth and tongue</note>.

->How they represent pleasure.<-

<margin>numbers</margin> When they wish to signify **Pleasure** <note>original Greek: "hēdonēn"</note>, they write the **number sixteen**; for from this number of years, men have the beginning of sexual intercourse with women and the procreation of children.

---

->In what way they represent pleasure.<-

<margin>31</margin> Wishing to demonstrate **Pleasure** <note>original Latin: "voluptatem"</note>, they paint the **number sixteen**: for indeed from that number of years, men begin to be captivated by the company of women and by the desire for procreating offspring.

<header>HIEROGLYPHICS.</header>
<sig>D ij</sig>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a rustic landscape with a large, gnarled, leafless tree on the left and rolling hills in the distance. ## How they represent sexual union.

When they wish to indicate **sexual union** <note>original Greek: "synousian"</note>, they write the **number sixteen twice**. This is because we have already stated that sixteen represents **pleasure**; and since sexual union consists of **two pleasures**—that of the man and that of the woman—they therefore add another sixteen to the first.

---

<margin>32</margin>
## In what way they represent sexual intercourse.

They paint the same **number XVI doubled** <note>original Latin: "geminum"</note> whenever they wish to signify the **joining of man and woman** <note>original Latin: "viri foeminaeque congressum"</note>. For since **pleasure** <note>original Latin: "voluptas"</note>, as we have said, takes its beginning from the **sixteenth year** <note>In ancient thought, the sixteenth year was often associated with the onset of puberty and the legal or biological capacity for sexual maturity</note>, and since the act of intercourse consists of a **dual pleasure**—namely that of the male and the female—they quite rightly add another sixteen to the depiction.

<summary>This page explains that the number sixteen represents pleasure (referencing the age of maturity), and therefore the doubling of this number is used as a hieroglyph for sexual union between a man and a woman.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, hieroglyphics, number symbolism, sixteen, sexual union, pleasure, puberty, XVI</keywords>

### How they represent a soul living here for a long time.

<margin>34</margin> When they wish to write [the symbol for] a **soul** <note>original Greek: "psychēn"</note> <gloss>*</gloss> living here for a long time, or an **inundation** <note>original Greek: "plēmmyran," referring specifically to the rising of the Nile</note>, they depict the **Phoenix** bird. They use it for the **soul**, because this creature is the longest-lived of all things in the world. They use it for an **inundation**, because the Phoenix is a symbol of the **Sun** <note>original Greek: "hēliou"</note>, and nothing in the world is greater than the sun. <margin>all things</margin> For the sun surmounts all things and <gloss>*</gloss> searches out all things. Thus, it shall be called "great" <note>original Greek: "polys"; the author suggests a linguistic or symbolic link between the "greatness" of the sun and the magnitude of the flood</note>.

### How they represent one who returns after a long time from a foreign land.

<margin>See page 154.</margin>
When they wish to indicate one who has been away for a long time or returns from a foreign land, they once again paint the bird called the **phoenix**. For when the time of its fated destiny <note>referring to its death and rebirth cycle</note> is about to overtake it, it returns to **Egypt** every five hundred years. If it happens to pay its debt to nature <note>original Greek: "to chreōn," a common euphemism for death</note> while within the borders of Egypt, it is buried with secret religious rites. Whatever the Egyptians perform for their other sacred animals, these same honors must be given to the phoenix; for it is said to rejoice in the **sun** more than any other living thing.

### How they represent one who, at last, returns home after a long time from a foreign land.

Furthermore, to signify one who, having traveled abroad for a long time, finally returns to his native soil, they again paint the bird called the **phoenix**. For this bird returns to **Egypt** only after five hundred years, when the moment of its death is near. There, if it pays its debt to nature <note>original Latin: "naturae debitum persoluerit," a classical euphemism for dying</note>, it is buried with great solemnity and ritual. Indeed, whatever the Egyptians religiously observe for their other sacred animals, these same honors must also be granted to the phoenix. It is said, in fact, to delight in the **Sun** among the Egyptians more than among all other nations; for this reason, the **Nile** overflows for them because of the heat of this God <note>The Sun, whose heat was believed to trigger the annual flood</note>, the reasoning for which was provided by us just a little earlier.

HIEROGLYPHICS.

### How they represent a heart.

When they wish to write the word **heart**, they paint an **ibis**. For this animal is sacred to **Hermes** <note>original Greek: Ἑρμῇ (*Hermē*). The Greeks identified their god Hermes with the Egyptian god Thoth, who was the scribe of the gods and presided over the "weighing of the heart" in the afterlife</note>, who is the master of every heart and of all reason. This is because the ibis, in its own form, is *similar to the heart*; regarding this matter, there is a very extensive account widely circulated among the Egyptians.

---

<margin>35</margin>

### How they paint a heart.

To indicate a **heart**, they paint an **Ibis**, because this animal is indeed attributed and dedicated to **Mercury** <note>The Roman name for Hermes/Thoth</note>, the protector and governor of the heart and of all reason. For the Ibis, by its own nature, is in many respects very similar to a heart; regarding this matter, many discourses are held among the Egyptians.

HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a landscape under a cloudy sky. Fine vertical lines descend from the clouds, representing falling dew. ### How they represent education.

<margin><unclear>And education...</unclear></margin> When writing the word **education** <note>original Greek: παιδεία (*paideia*). This term refers to the holistic education and upbringing of a Greek citizen, encompassing both knowledge and character</note>, they paint the **sky shedding dew**. This signifies that just as dew, when it falls, reaches all plants, it softens those that are naturally capable of being softened, while those that remain hard by their own nature are unable to experience the same effect as the others. So it is with people: education is provided to everyone in common, but the gifted student seizes it like dew, while the untalented person is unable to do so.

---

<margin>36</margin> ### How they represent instruction and learning.

To indicate **learning** <note>original Latin: *doctrinam*. The Latin translator uses *eruditio* and *doctrina* to capture the breadth of the Greek *paideia*</note>, they paint the sky pouring forth dew, implying that just as falling dew is spread across all plants, it only softens those whose nature is such that they can be softened, while the others indeed from their own [nature]... <note>The Latin text on this page ends mid-sentence</note>

<summary>This page explains that the hieroglyph for education consists of the sky shedding dew, using the metaphor of dew softening only receptive plants to illustrate how learning depends on an individual's natural aptitude.</summary>

### How they represent Egyptian letters.

To signify **Egyptian letters**, or a **sacred scribe** <note>original Greek: ἱερογραμματέα (*hierogrammatea*). These were high-ranking temple officials who specialized in hieroglyphs and religious texts</note>, or an **end** <note>original Greek: πέρας (*peras*), meaning a boundary or completion</note>, they paint **ink**, a **sieve**, and a **reed** <note>original Greek: σχοινίον (*schoinion*). The Egyptian pen was typically a rush or reed with the end bruised to form a fine brush</note>. They represent Egyptian letters this way because everything written by the Egyptians is performed with these tools. For they write with a reed, and nothing else. They include a sieve because the sieve, being the primary tool for bread-making, is made from reeds. They show by this that anyone who has their food <note>meaning a secure livelihood or sustenance</note> will learn letters, but he who does not have it will practice another craft; from which also...

<meta>This page continues the explanation of why education is termed "full nourishment" and details the tools of a sacred scribe, specifically focusing on Chapter 37 of Horapollo's Hieroglyphics.</meta>

HORAPOLLO

Education among them is called *Sbo* <note>original Greek: σβῶ. This corresponds to the Coptic word *sbō*, meaning "instruction" or "teaching"</note>, which, when interpreted, means "full nourishment." They also use these symbols for a **sacred scribe** <note>original Greek: ἱερογραμματέα (*hierogrammatea*), the high-ranking priests who managed the temple libraries and sacred scripts</note>, since he distinguishes between life and death. For there is among the sacred scribes a sacred book called *Ambres* <note>original Greek: ἀμβρὴς. This likely refers to a specific Egyptian medical or ritual manual used by the "House of Life" to determine if a patient would recover</note>, through which they judge a bedridden patient—whether they will live or not—making their determination from the manner in which the sick person lies. Finally, it represents an **end** <note>original Greek: πέρας (*peras*), meaning a limit or completion</note>, because he who has learned his letters has arrived at a calm harbor of life, no longer wandering amidst the evils of existence.

<margin>37</margin>
### ->How they represent Egyptian letters.<-

Furthermore, when they wish to signify **Egyptian letters**, or a **sacred scribe**, or an **end**, they paint **ink**, a **sieve**, and a **reed**. They use these for Egyptian letters because all writing among them is completed with these tools; for they write with a reed <note>original Latin: *iunco*. The Egyptian pen was made from a specific type of rush or reed</note>, and not with anything else. They also add a sieve, because the sieve, being the primary instrument for making bread, is itself made from reeds. They imply, therefore, that whoever has an abundance of food <note>original Latin: *victus*, meaning basic sustenance or livelihood</note> will apply his mind to the study of letters, but he who does not have it will occupy himself with other crafts. Whence also education among them is called *Sbo*, a name which, if you translate it, sounds like "full nourishment." They use it for a **sacred scribe**, however, because he discerns between life and death. For there is a book among the sacred scribes which they call the *Sacred Ambres*, by whose help they deliver a judgment on a bedridden patient: whether he is vital <note>capable of living</note> or not, perceiving this from certain signs in the patient’s reclining. Finally, it signifies an **end**, because he who has learned letters has reached a tranquil port of life, and is no longer wandering or tossing about in the inconveniences of life.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic association between writing tools (reed, ink, and sieve) and the concept of education as "nourishment." it also describes the "Sacred Ambres," a book used by Egyptian scribes for medical prognosis.</summary>

### How they represent a sacred scribe.

They use it for a **prophet** because the dog, more than any other animal, gazes intently at the statues of the gods, just as a prophet does. ORI APOLLINIS

<margin>burials <note>original Greek: κηδείας (*kēdeias*). The marginalia also contains the word "heart" (καρδίας), likely a scribal error or a variant reading, as the following text discusses the spleen and funeral rites.</note></margin>
<margin><gloss>...at the time of death, they mostly become splenetic; for by smelling the exhalations of the dogs being dissected, they are affected by the animal. ### 38 How they represent a sacred scribe.

When they wish to represent a **sacred scribe** <term>original Greek: ἱερογραμματέα (*hierogrammatea*); a high-ranking official responsible for sacred texts and temple records</term>, or a **prophet**, or an **embalmer** <note>Latin: *vespillonem*; a funeral director or one who carries the dead</note>, or the **spleen**, or the **sense of smell**, or **laughter**, or **sneezing**, or a **magistrate**, or a **judge**, they paint a **dog**.

They use it for a **sacred scribe** because it is necessary for someone who wishes to fulfill this duty properly and perfectly to practice and study many things, to "bark" <note>original: *allatrare*; likely a metaphor for the constant vocal recitation and chanting required in scribal training</note> continually, and to be uncompromising, showing favor to no one, just like dogs.

They use it for a **prophet** because the dog, more than any other animal, gazes intently at the statues of the gods, just as prophets are accustomed to do. 

They use it for an **embalmer of sacred things** <note>Latin: *pollinctorem*; one who prepared bodies for the funeral</note> because he likewise contemplates the naked and dissected bodies <note>Latin: *simulacra*; while often meaning "images" or "statues," here it refers to the forms or remains upon which the embalmer performs his rites</note> upon which he performs the final duties. 

They use it for the **spleen** because, among all animals, the dog has the smallest and lightest spleen; and whether the dog meets its death or falls into rabies, it is said to originate from the spleen. Indeed, even those who attend to the dog's burial, when they are approaching their own ends, for the most part become **splenetic** <term>afflicted with ailments of the spleen</term>. This is because they are infected by the heavy breath and vapors that rise from the incised and dissected dog. 

Furthermore, they use it for the **sense of smell**, **laughter**, and **sneezing**, because those who are completely "splenetic" can neither smell, nor laugh, nor even sneeze.

<summary>This page explains the multiple symbolic meanings of the dog in hieroglyphics, ranging from the professional traits of scribes and prophets to physiological observations about the spleen and the sense of smell.</summary>

<keywords>sacred scribe, prophet, embalmer, dog symbolism, spleen, medical folklore, Egyptian hierarchy, Horapollo</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the dog as a primary symbol in Egyptian hieroglyphs. HIEROGLYPHICA.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large dog sitting on the left, looking towards the right. On the right, a human figure reclines at the base of a gnarled tree. The figure is draped in elaborate, flowing royal robes but appears partially unclothed beneath them. The scene illustrates the symbolic relationship between the dog and the office of a magistrate or judge.</image-desc>

### By what animal they show a magistrate or a judge.

When they wish to represent a **magistrate** <term>original Greek: ἀρχὴν (*archēn*); a position of authority or a high official</term> or a **judge**, they draw a **dog** and place a **royal robe** <note>original Greek: βασιλικὴν στολὴν (*basilikēn stolēn*)</note> nearby, along with a **naked figure**. This is because, just as the dog (as I mentioned before) gazes with a keen eye upon the statues of the gods, so too the magistrate, in his role as a judge, in the most ancient of times, would behold the **king naked** <note>The "nakedness" of the king suggests a lack of artifice or the judge's ability to see the ruler's true character and the unvarnished truth of the law</note>. It is for this reason that they include the royal robe in this depiction.

---

<margin>39</margin>
### In what way they indicate a magistrate, or a judge.

When they designate a **magistrate** or a **judge**, they paint a **dog** and also a **royal robe** placed next to a **naked figure**. This is because, just as the dog—as I said above—gazes with the sharpest eyes at the images of the gods, so also the magistrate, who was also a judge, in more ancient times, would behold the **king naked**... <note>The Latin text ends mid-sentence at "spe-", likely intended to be *spectabat* (beheld/watched).</note>

<summary>This page explains the hieroglyphic use of the dog to represent a judge or magistrate, illustrating the official's duty to see the "naked truth" and maintain a keen, watchful eye over the law, much like a dog watches over sacred statues.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, hieroglyphics, dog symbolism, magistrate, judge, justice, royal robe, Egyptian law, symbolic vision</keywords>

...beheld. And for that reason, they add a **royal robe** <note>original Latin: *regiam prætextam*; refers to the bordered toga worn by high-ranking Roman officials, here used to translate the concept of Egyptian royal garments</note> to the dog.

### How they signify a shrine-bearer.

When they wish to signify a **shrine-bearer** <term>original Greek: παστοφόρον (*pastophoron*); a class of Egyptian priest responsible for carrying the sacred shrine or the "pastos" (the veil or inner shrine) during processions</term>, they draw a **guardian of a house**, because the temple is guarded by him.

<margin>40</margin>
### In what way they signify him who carries the sacred mantle.

Signifying him who carries the **sacred mantle** <note>original Latin: *sacrum pallium*; the translator uses "mantle" or "veil" to explain the function of the priest who carries the divine enclosure</note>, they paint a **guardian of a house**, for the sacred place is guarded by him.

<meta>This page continues the sequence of Hieroglyphic symbols from Horapollo’s treatise. The previous page (62) discussed the symbol for a "pastophorus" or shrine-bearer. This page moves to chapter 41, which explains the symbolic representation of a timekeeper or observer of the hours.</meta>

HIEROGLYPHICA.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular border depicting a man in mid-16th-century European dress (doublet, hose, and a cap). He is standing in a landscape with a large tree to the left and water in the background. He holds a small rectangular object up to his mouth with both hands, appearing to consume it. This is a literal representation of "eating the hours."</image-desc>

## How they represent a timekeeper.

To signify a **timekeeper** <term>original Greek: ὡροσκόπον (*hōroskopon*); in an Egyptian context, this refers to a "horoscopus" priest who observed the stars and the sun to determine the hours for temple rituals</term>, they paint a man **eating the hours**. This is not because a man literally eats hours—for that is impossible—but because human nourishment is apportioned and provided to us by the hours <note>original Greek: *αἱ τροφαὶ... ἀπὸ τῶν ὡρῶν περίζονται*; this suggests that the seasons and the specific times of day dictate the availability and regulation of food</note>.

---

<margin>41</margin>
## In what way they express him who observes the reckoning of the hours.

Representing an **observer of the hours** <note>original Latin: *horarum inspectorem*</note>, they paint a man feeding on hours, not that a man consumes hours (for that can by no means happen), but because food is prepared and set before men at fixed hours <note>original Latin: *statis horis*; the Latin translator emphasizes the regularity of mealtimes as the link between "eating" and "time"</note>.

<summary>This page describes the hieroglyphic symbol for a timekeeper, represented by a man "eating" the hours to symbolize how human life and nourishment are regulated by the passage of time.</summary>
<keywords>timekeeper, horoscopus, hours, nourishment, Horapollo, Hieroglyphica, Egyptian priesthood, symbolism of time</keywords>

ORI APOLLINIS

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a large cluster of stylized, curling flames or thick foliage rising from a base on the left, set beside a flowing body of water on the right. In the background, there are clouds and a sparse landscape.</image-desc>

### How they signify purity.

To represent **purity** <term>original Greek: ἁγνείαν (*hagneian*); referring to ritual or moral holiness</term>, they paint **fire and water**, because every act of purification <term>original Greek: καθαρμὸς (*katharmos*)</term> is completed through these two elements <note>This reflects a common ancient belief that fire and water are the primary agents of cleansing—water for washing away physical or ritual stains, and fire for spiritual or alchemical refinement</note>.

<margin>42</margin>
### In what way they indicate purity.

In marking **purity**, they paint **fire and water**, because by these elements all ritual cleansing <note>original Latin: *lustratio*</note> and atonement <note>original Latin: *expiatio*</note> are performed.

<summary>This page explains that the Egyptians represented the concept of purity through the symbols of fire and water, identifying these as the essential elements for ritual cleansing and expiation.</summary>
<keywords>purity, fire, water, purification, ritual cleansing, elements, Horapollo, symbolism</keywords>

HIEROGLYPHICS.
E

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration enclosed in a rectangular frame. It depicts a large, scaly sea monster with fins and a wide-open mouth, emerging from choppy waters. ### How they hint at the unlawful, or an abomination.

In signifying the **unlawful** <term>original Greek: ἀθέμιτον (*athemiton*); that which is forbidden by divine law</term>, or even an **abomination** <term>original Greek: μῦσος (*mysos*); a source of ritual defilement or loathing</term>, they 
<margin>*</margin> paint a **fish**. This is because the eating of fish is detested and considered 
<margin>*</margin> a **pollution** <note>original: μεμιᾶσθαι (*memiasthai*)</note> within the sacred temples <note>Ancient Egyptian priests were famously required to abstain from fish, as they were often associated with the chaotic god Set or seen as consumers of the members of Osiris</note>. For every fish is a **bringer of emptiness** <note>original: κενοποιὸν (*kenopoion*); likely meaning they strip the waters bare or are spiritually "void"</note> and **cannibalistic** <term>original Greek: ἀλληλοφάγον (*allēlophagon*); literally "one who eats its own kind"</term>.

---

### In what way they secretly indicate a wicked and abominable thing.

In pointing toward a **wicked deed** <note>original Latin: *nefarium*</note>, or even an 
<margin>43</margin> **abomination**, they paint a **fish**, because those who are occupied with sacred rites <note>referring to the priesthood and their ritual restrictions</note> shrink from and execrate the eating of them. For every fish is of such a nature that it consumes whatever it encounters and turns its **ferocity against its own kind**.

<summary>This page explains that the fish serves as a symbol for the "unlawful" or "abominable" due to the ancient Egyptian priestly taboo against eating them and the fish's perceived nature as a cannibalistic predator.</summary>

### How they write "mouth."

When they wish to write the word **mouth** <note>original Greek: στόμα (*stoma*)</note>, they paint a **serpent**, because the serpent possesses strength in no other part of its limbs except for the **mouth** alone <note>The author suggests that because a snake lacks legs or arms, its entire essence and power—specifically its ability to bite and deliver venom—is concentrated in its mouth</note>.

### In what way they paint the mouth.

In signifying the **mouth** <note>original Latin: *os*</note>, they paint a **serpent**, since the serpent is powerful in no other member than the **mouth**.

### How they represent hearing.

When they wish to represent **hearing** <note>original Greek: ἀκοήν (*akoēn*)</note>, they paint a **bull's ear** <note>original Greek: ταύρου ὠτίον (*taurou ōtion*)</note>. For whenever the female is in heat <note>original Greek: ὀργῶσα (*orgōsa*), implying a state of biological readiness or excitement</note> for conception (and she remains in heat for no more than three hours), she bellows at her loudest. If the bull does not appear during this time, her womb <note>original Greek: τὴν φύσιν (*tēn physin*), literally "nature," here referring to the reproductive organs</note> closes up until the next cycle. This, however, rarely happens; for the bull hears her from a great distance, and sensing that she is in heat, he arrives at a run for the union. This animal alone, among others...

### How hearing is represented.

<margin>46</margin>To represent **hearing**, they paint the **ear of a bull**. For when a cow is inflamed with the urge to conceive and is spurred on—and she remains in heat for no more than three hours—she then bellows with great intensity. If the bull does not reach her during this window of time, her reproductive tract <note>original Latin: *vas genitale*, literally "genital vessel"</note> closes until the next opportunity for mating; however, this happens only rarely. The bull hears her immediately, even from a very long distance, and understanding that she is stirred by Venus <note>original Latin: *venere excitari*; the author uses the name of the goddess of love to describe sexual arousal</note>, he soon comes running. This trait is unique to this animal alone among all living creatures during the act of mating.

### How they represent the penis of a prolific man and one abundant in seed.

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>HIEROGLYPHICS.</header>

<sig>E iiij</sig>
<meta>catchword: if (original: ἐὰν)</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape under a sky containing both the sun (with a face) and a crescent moon. In the foreground, a quail-like bird is shown in a field with sparse vegetation and trees, appearing to look or react toward the celestial bodies.</image-desc>

### How they represent impurity.

To represent **impurity**, they paint a **quail** <note>original: ὄρτυξ (*ortyx*)</note>. They do this because when the moon rises, the bird looks intently at the goddess and lets out a cry, not to bless or honor her, but in a hostile manner. There is a very clear sign of this: for digging up the earth with its front legs, it veils its own pupils <note>The Greek text literally says "paints its own pupils," likely meaning it kicks up dust to cover its eyes</note>, as if it were indignant and unwilling to see the rising of the goddess. It performs these same actions at the rising of the divine sun.

Because of this behavior, the ancient kings—when the **time-keeper** <term>original: ὡροσκόπος (*horoskopos*); a priest or official who observed the stars to determine the exact hour</term> signaled the rising to them—would sit near this animal. By observing the bird, which acted as a sort of **gnomon** <note>A *gnomon* is the part of a sundial that casts a shadow; here, the bird acts as a living pointer or indicator of the time</note>, they could know the exact moment of the sun's rising. 

For this reason, the priests eat this bird alone among all the fowl without it being **sealed** <note>original: ἀσφράγιστον (*asphragiston*). In Egyptian ritual, animals for sacrifice were inspected for purity and marked with a seal. To eat something "unsealed" implies it is already considered common or spiritually "unclean"</note>; for it appears to hold a certain enmity toward the goddess. And the same applies to those in the *desert, wherever* one may find them.

<margin>the desert, if</margin>

...having drunk from a water-source, it stirs up the depths with its beak and mixes silt into the water, and with its feet it throws dust into it, so that the water might not be drinkable for any other animal. ### 48 How they represent impurity.

Furthermore, to represent an impure and wicked soul, they paint a **quail** <note>original: *coturnix* (Latin); *ὄρτυξ* (*ortyx*, Greek)</note>. This is because when the Moon rises, the bird looks toward the goddess with fixed eyes and cries out—not to offer a blessing, nor to follow her with a favorable shout or congratulation. There is a very clear proof of this: digging into the earth with its front legs, it fixes its own pupils into the ground as if "painting" them there, as if indignant and unwilling to look upon the goddess as she rises. It does the very same thing at the rising of the divine Sun. 

For this reason, the ancient kings—when the **time-keeper** <term>original: *horarum observator*; a priest or official who watched the stars to determine the exact hour</term> announced the rising to them—would sit near this animal. By using the bird as a kind of **gnomon** <note>the pointer or rod on a sundial that casts a shadow to indicate time</note>, they could know the timing and nature of the sunrise with great accuracy. Because of this, the priests eat this bird alone among the winged creatures without it being **marked** <note>original: *non signatam*; in Egyptian ritual, animals fit for consumption or sacrifice had to be inspected and "sealed" with a mark of purity. The quail was considered so naturally hostile to the gods that it was exempt from this honor</note>, because it seems to hold a certain grudge against the goddess. For whatever place it finds in the desert flowing with water, after it has drunk, it disturbs the water with its beak, mixes in mud, and with its feet—

### 49 ->How they represent destruction.<-

To denote **destruction** <note>original: *ἀφανισμόν* (*aphanismon*), meaning a vanishing or blotting out</note>, they paint a **mouse** <note>original: *μῦν* (*myn*)</note>, since by eating everything, it defiles and renders things useless. They use this same symbol when they wish to write the word **judgment** <note>original: *κρίσιν* (*krisin*)</note>. For when many different loaves of bread are laid out, the mouse chooses the purest of them to eat; therefore, a "judgment of the bakers" <note>a metaphorical trial to see who baked the best bread</note> is said to take place among the mice.

### 51 ->How they depict knowledge.<-

When they depict **knowledge** <note>original: *γνῶσιν* (*gnōsin*), meaning recognition or the act of knowing</note>, they paint an **ant** <note>original: *μύρμηκα* (*myrmēka*)</note>. For whatever a man might hide securely, the ant finds it out. Not only for this reason, but also because, unlike other animals, when it is gathering food for itself for the winter, it never misses the location, but returns to it without failing.

<margin>51</margin>
->How they paint knowledge.<-

In painting **knowledge** <note>original: *cognitionem*</note>, they paint an **ant** <note>original: *formicam*</note>. For if a man has hidden anything safely, the ant knows of it. And not for this reason alone, but because, beyond other living creatures, while gathering food for itself for the winter, it does not wander from the place where it stored it, but reaches it without any error.

->How they represent a foolish person.<-

53 When they depict a **pelican**, they signify someone who is both **mindless** and **imprudent**. For although it is able, after the manner of other birds, to lay its eggs in higher places, it does not do so; instead, it digs into the earth and places its young there. When men discover this, they smear the place with dried cow dung and set fire to it. When the pelican sees the smoke, it tries to extinguish the fire with its wings, but the flapping of its wings actually fans the flames. When its wings are caught in the fire, it is easily captured by the bird-catchers. For this reason, the priests did not think it appropriate to eat this bird, since it throws itself into danger entirely for the sake of its children. The common people of Egypt, however, do eat it, saying that it undertakes this struggle not through any sense of judgment or prudence—like the fox-geese—but merely out of goodwill and a wonderful piety toward its offspring.

---

<margin>÷ Consider the sinner to be similar: after he has been cleansed by baptism, and while he is able—in the manner of the Saints—to store up his eggs (that is, his works) in higher places (namely in merits, charity, and finally in heaven), he does not do so. Instead, he lusts after the "dug-up earth"—that is, temporal goods (if it is right to call them that)—and seeks them with great labor. (As Christ said: "Do not store up for yourselves treasures on earth, etc. But in heaven, etc." <note>A reference to Matthew 6:19-20.</note>) Seeing this, the demons apply the fire of concupiscence <note>In this context, *concupiscence* refers to an intense worldly or sensual longing that distracts from spiritual goals.</note>, which the sinner does not flee because of his love for his "children" (that is, temporal goods); instead, he fans the fire even more by the flapping of his wings (that is, his pride). For as Paul says: "Those who want to become rich fall into temptation and into snares." <note>A reference to 1 Timothy 6:9.</note> And thus they capture him.</margin>

<meta>Catchword: and into snares.</meta>

### How they represent gratitude.

<margin>through writing</margin>
When they want to write **gratitude** <note>original: εὐχαρισίαν (*eucharistian*)</note>, they paint a **hoopoe** <note>original: *κουκουφὰν* (*koukouphan*)</note>, because this is the only one of the irrational animals that, after it has been raised by its parents, returns the same favor to them when they grow old. For in the very place where it was raised by them, it builds a nest for them and \* plucks their old feathers and provides food, until the parents grow new plumage and are able to sustain themselves. For this reason, the hoopoe is the crowning ornament <note>original: *περιποίησίς* (*peripoiêsis*); referring to the finial or head of a staff</note> upon the **divine scepters** <note>The "was-scepter" in Egyptian art is often topped with the head of a creature that Horapollo identifies here as the hoopoe.</note>.

---

<margin>54</margin>
->How they indicate a grateful mind.<-

When they indicate a **grateful mind**, they paint a **Hoopoe** <note>original: *Cucupha*</note>, because this alone among the mute animals, after it has been raised by its parents, returns a like gratitude to them when they are worn out with old age.

This carving proclaims the piety of children toward their parents; and this animal, by a certain wonderful instinct, teaches children that lesson which the children of this unhappy age follow least of all, having become disciples of the Pharisees, concerning whom the Lord speaks in the Gospel.

### How they represent the unjust and the ungrateful. <margin>55</margin>

To represent the **unjust and the ungrateful**, they depict two claws of a **river-horse** <note>original: *equi fluuiatilis*; the Latin literal translation of the Greek *hippopotamos*</note> pointing downwards. For this creature, when it has reached the flower of its youth, tests its father by fighting him to see if it has become the more powerful. If the father yields and assigns a territory to him, the son enters into a union with his own mother and allows the father to live. But if the father does not permit him to be joined to the mother, the son—being stronger and more robust—kills him. Furthermore, they depict its two claws curved especially downwards so that those who see this and understand its cause might be more inclined toward **doing good**.

<meta>This page continues the Renaissance edition of Horapollo’s Hieroglyphica. This page introduces the dove as a dual symbol of filial ingratitude and physical/spiritual purity.</meta>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORUS APOLLO

->How they represent one who is ungrateful toward his own benefactors.<-

To signify a person who is **ungrateful** and hostile toward those who have done him good, they paint a **dove** <note>original Greek: περιστερά (*peristera*)</note>. For the male, once he has become the stronger, drives his own father away from the mother and thus joins with her in marriage. Yet, this animal is also considered to be **pure**; for whenever there is a pestilential condition <note>original: λοιμώδους καταστάσεως (*loimōdous katastaseōs*); refers to a plague or a "miasma" in the air</note> and every living and inanimate thing is diseased, those who eat this bird are the only ones who do not partake in such a sickness. Therefore, during such times, nothing else is served to the **king** as food except only the dove. For this same reason, it is given to those living in states of **purity**, because they serve the gods. It is also recorded that this animal has no **gall** <note>In ancient natural history, the lack of a gall bladder was thought to make the dove "sweet" and free from bitterness or anger.</note>.

---

->How they represent one who is ungrateful to those<-
->who have deserved well of him.<-

Signifying one who is **ungrateful** and dangerous to those by whom he has been treated with kindness, they paint a **dove** <note>original Latin: *columbam*</note>. For when the male has grown more robust, he expels the father from the mother's company and is thus joined to her in wedlock. However, this animal appears to be **pure**. Indeed, when the condition of the air is pestilential and all things, both animate and inanimate, are affected by it, whoever feeds upon this animal is kept immune from this plague. And so, at that time, nothing other than doves is served to the **king** for his meal; the same is done for those who remain pure and chaste so that they may minister to the gods. It is also handed down that this animal lacks **gall**.

<summary>This page describes the dove as a symbol of ingratitude due to its perceived "Oedipal" behavior, while paradoxically highlighting its extreme purity and medicinal value during plagues.</summary>

<keywords>dove, ingratitude, purity, plague, royal diet, gall, Horapollo, Egyptian symbolism, filial conflict</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS.

F ij

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two "impossible" scenarios: on the left, a headless man in 16th-century attire walks on land; on the right, a pair of disembodied feet walk upon the surface of water.</image-desc>

->How to represent the impossible.<-

To signify that which is **impossible** <note>original Greek: ἀδύνατον (*adynaton*)</note>, they paint a man’s **feet** walking upon **water**; or when they wish to represent the same thing in another way, they paint a **headless man** walking. Since both of these occurrences are inherently impossible, they have reasonably adopted them for this purpose.

<margin>57</margin>
->How to represent that which cannot be done.<-

To represent that which cannot be done <note>original Latin: *id quod fieri non potest*</note>, they paint the **feet** of a man walking in **water**. And wishing to signify the same thing in another way, they paint a **man without a head** walking; since both of these belong to the category of things which cannot happen <note>original Latin: *impossibilia*</note>, they have been rightfully chosen to indicate this.

<summary>This page explains two hieroglyphic symbols for the concept of "impossibility": feet walking on water and a headless man walking.</summary>
<keywords>impossible, walking on water, headless man, Horapollo, Egyptian symbols, Renaissance emblems</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the discussion of symbols for impossibility (feet on water and the headless man) from the previous page. # OF HORUS APOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a serpent coiled in a spiral (Ouroboros style) with its tail near its mouth. Within the center of the coils, the letters "A P I S" are inscribed. The serpent is set against a landscape of rocky hills and foliage.</image-desc>

### How [to represent] a very bad king.

To represent a **very bad king** <note>original Greek: κάκισον (*kakiston*). This is likely a historical printer's error for κόσμου (*kosmou*, "of the world"), as the surrounding context describes a "world-ruler."</note>, they depict a **serpent** shaped in the form of the **world** <note>original Greek: κοσμοειδῶς (*kosmoeidos*)</note>, placing its **tail** in its **mouth**. They write the **name of the king** in the middle of the coil, subtly hinting that the king **rules the world**. Among the Egyptians, the name of the serpent is **Meisi**.

<margin>58</margin>
### In what way a very bad king [is represented].

When they wish to signify a **very bad king** <note>original Latin: *regem pessimum*</note>, they paint a **snake** in the shape of a **circle**, bringing its **tail** to its **mouth**. They write the **name of the king** in the middle of the loop, obscurely suggesting that they are painting a king who **commands the world** <note>original Latin: *orbi imperitantem*</note>. Furthermore, the snake’s name among the Egyptians is **Meisi**.

<summary>This page describes the use of the Ouroboros (a snake biting its tail) to represent a king, specifically one who rules the entire world, though the text contains a historical error labeling him as a "very bad" king.</summary>
<keywords>Ouroboros, Meisi, king, world ruler, serpent symbolism, Horapollo, Egyptian hieroglyphs, Apis</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous entry concerning the serpent symbol (the Ouroboros) used to represent a world-ruler. It introduces a second, related way to represent a king using a serpent, focusing on the specific quality of vigilance and the role of the sovereign as a universal protector.</meta>

# HIEROGLYPHICS

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a man in Renaissance-era attire standing before a stone gateway or building. He holds a long staff or pole. To his left, a serpent or dragon-like creature is coiled on the ground, looking upward. In the background, there are additional stone structures with arched windows.</image-desc>

->### How [to represent] a king as a guardian.<-

When signifying a **king as a guardian** in another way, they depict a **wakeful serpent**. In place of the **name of the king**, they depict the word **Guardian** <note>original Greek: φύλακα (*phylaka*)</note>. For this serpent is the **guardian of the entire world** <note>original Greek: τοῦ παντὸς κόσμου (*tou pantos kosmou*)</note>, and it signifies that the king must be **watchful at all times**.

---

->### In what way a king [is represented as] a guardian.<-

In another way also, when signifying a **king as a guardian** <note>original Latin: *regem custodem*</note>, they paint a **vigilant snake**, but instead of the king’s name they add the word **Guardian**. For this is the **guardian of the whole world**. Therefore, it is necessary that the king be **constantly vigilant**.

<term>Vigilant snake</term>: In ancient lore, snakes were often chosen as symbols of watchfulness because they lack movable eyelids and appear to never close their eyes, suggesting a state of eternal wakefulness. 

<meta>Signature mark: F iij</meta>
<meta>Catchword: 49</meta>

<note>The vocabulary below highlights the key terms used in both the Greek and Latin versions of the text.</note>
**Technical Terms:** **King** (βασιλεύς / rex), **Guardian** (φύλαξ / custos), **Serpent/Snake** (ὄφις / anguis), **Vigilant** (vigilantem), **Hieroglyphics** (Hieroglyphica).

<summary>This page describes how a wakeful serpent serves as a hieroglyphic symbol for a king in his role as a guardian. The symbol emphasizes that a ruler must remain constantly vigilant over his domain, just as the serpent was believed to never sleep.</summary>
<keywords>king, guardian, serpent, vigilance, world ruler, Horapollo, symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of serpent symbolism from page 85. While the previous page described the serpent as a symbol of the king as a "guardian" or "watchful protector," this page introduces a new hieroglyphic variation representing the king specifically as a "world-ruler" (kosmokratōr), focusing on the relationship between the sovereign's residence and the world at large.</meta>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large serpent or dragon coiled around a two-story house. The house has a stone foundation and a wooden upper level with windows and a tiled roof. In the background, a tower is visible on a rocky outcrop.</image-desc>

->### How they signify a ruler of the world.<-

Again, when they consider and wish to signify the king as a **ruler of the world** <note>original Greek: κοσμοκράτορα (*kosmokratōra*)</note>, they depict him as a **serpent**. In the center of the serpent's coils, they display a **great house**, and this is done without reason <note>In many manuscripts of this text, this phrase appears as "not without reason" (*ouk alogōs*), though here the negation is absent in the Greek OCR.</note>; <margin>\* in the</margin> for the **royal house** governed by him is in the world.

---

<margin>60</margin> ->### In what way they denote the lord of the world.<-

Again, when thinking of the king as the **ruler of the orb** <note>original Latin: *orbis moderatorem*</note> and indicating him as such, they paint him as a **snake**, but in its center they fashion and represent a **large house**. This is certainly not without reason, for the **royal house** <note>original Latin: *regia domus*</note> which is governed by him is situated within the world.

**Technical Terms:**
**World-ruler** (κοσμοκράτωρ / mundi dominus): A title implying universal sovereignty.
**Serpent/Snake** (ὄφις / anguis): Here used as a symbol of the horizon or the boundaries of the world encircling the royal residence.
**Royal house** (βασίλειος οἶκος / regia domus): Refers to the palace or the seat of government, which is the heart of the world the king rules.

<summary>This page explains a hieroglyph for a universal monarch, consisting of a serpent coiled around a house to represent a king whose authority encompasses the entire world.</summary>
<keywords>world-ruler, serpent, royal house, sovereignty, Horapollo, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of symbols related to kingship. While the previous page used the serpent and the house to represent the king's dominion over the physical world, this page shifts focus to the social order and the relationship between a monarch and their subjects through the symbol of the bee.</meta>

# HIEROGLYPHICS
F iiij
<meta>catchword: How</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting an apiary. Two large, woven straw beehives (skeps) sit upon a wooden bench. A swarm of bees is shown flying around the hives and a nearby flowering plant.</image-desc>

->### How they signify a people obedient to a king.<-

When they wish to indicate a **people obedient to a king**, they depict a **bee** <note>original Greek: μέλισσαν (*melissan*)</note>. For this is the only one among the other animals that has a **king** <note>Ancient naturalists, including Aristotle and Pliny, traditionally identified the leader of the hive as a "king" rather than a queen.</note>, whom the rest of the multitude of bees follows, just as men obey a king. They also suggest, through the sweetness of the **honey** <margin>from the goodness, a good king</margin> and the power of the creature’s **sting**, that a king should be both **benevolent** and **firm** <note>original Greek: εὔτονον (*eutonon*), implying vigor or tension, like a well-strung bow</note> in his administration.

---

->### How they signify a people compliant to their ruler.<-

To suggest a people who are **submissive to the commands of their leader**, they paint a **bee**. Indeed, these alone out of every kind of living creature have a **king** whom the remaining multitude of bees follows, and whom they obey just as the common people obey a king. They further imply, through both the remarkable **usefulness of honey** and the **force** which this creature possesses in its **sting**, that a king must be simultaneously **beneficial and powerful** in managing the affairs of state.

**Technical Terms:**
**Bee** (μέλισσα / apis): A classic symbol of industry, social organization, and monarchy.
**King** (βασιλεύς / rex): Here referring to the leader of the hive, symbolizing the sovereign.
**Honey** (μέλι / mel): Represents the "sweetness" or kindness of a good ruler's actions.
**Sting** (κέντρον / aculeus): Represents the king's power to punish or enforce the law.

<summary>This page explains the bee as a hieroglyphic symbol for an obedient populace, drawing a parallel between the organization of a beehive and a well-governed human kingdom.</summary>
<keywords>bee, kingship, obedience, honey, sting, governance, Horapollo, social order</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a square border depicting a serpent or eel-like creature with a mammalian head (possibly a basilisk or a specific symbolic eel) coiled on a riverbank or shore. In the background are jagged rocks or cliffs and sparse vegetation.</image-desc>

->### How they signify a king ruling a part of the world.<-

When they wish to signify a **king** who rules not the whole world, but only a **part** of it, they depict a **halved serpent** <note>original Greek: ἡμίτομον ὄφιν (*hēmitomon ophin*)</note>. They indicate the **king** by the use of the animal, but signify that he does not rule the whole world by the fact that the creature is shown **halved**.

<margin>62</margin>
->### How a king ruling a part of the globe is shown.<-

Furthermore, wishing to signify a **king** ruling not the entire globe, but only a **part**, they paint a **serpent cut in half** <note>original Latin: anguem dimidiatum</note>. They designate the king indeed through the animal itself, but show he does not rule the whole world by the fact that it is depicted as **bisected**.

<gloss>Horapollo: The purported author of this treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphs.</gloss>
<gloss>Basileus: The Greek term for "king" or "emperor."</gloss>
<gloss>Anguis/Serpent: In this symbolic system, the serpent represents power and eternity, often associated with the Egyptian uraeus.</gloss>

<summary>This page explains that a serpent cut in half is used to symbolize a king whose power is limited to a specific region rather than the entire world. It continues the book's tradition of using various forms of serpents to represent different aspects of royal authority.</summary>
<keywords>king, sovereignty, serpent, world rule, dominion, Horapollo, hieroglyphs, symbolism</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a square border depicting a large, scaled serpent or dragon coiled amongst rocky terrain and waves. A small cloud with a face (representing wind or a deity) is visible in the upper right corner.</image-desc>

->### How they signify the Ruler of All.<-

They signify the **Ruler of All** <note>original Greek: παντοκράτορα (*pantokratora*)</note> through the **perfection** <note>In this context, "perfection" refers to the animal being shown whole and complete.</note> of the animal, depicting the **serpent whole** once again. Among them, this represents the **spirit** <note>original Greek: πνεῦμα (*pneuma*)</note> that permeates the entire world.

<margin>63</margin>
->### How they signify the one who is widely powerful over all things.<-

Moreover, they represent the **governor of all** <note>original Latin: omnium moderatorem</note> through the **completeness** of the same animal, painting the **serpent whole**. Likewise, among them, this is the **spirit** which spreads throughout the entire universe.

<gloss>Pantokrator: A Greek term meaning "Almighty" or "Ruler of All," often used in later Christian contexts but here referring to a universal monarch.</gloss>
<gloss>Pneuma/Spiritus: The "breath" or "spirit" believed by many ancient philosophers (such as the Stoics) to animate and hold the physical universe together.</gloss>
<gloss>Snake/Serpent: Here, the wholeness of the serpent’s body reflects the totality of the cosmos and the unbroken reach of a supreme ruler's power.</gloss>

<summary>This page explains that a complete, un-bisected serpent represents a ruler with universal authority, symbolizing the "spirit" that pervades the entire world. It serves as a direct contrast to the symbol of the regional king described on the preceding page.</summary>
<keywords>serpent, universal ruler, Pantokrator, world spirit, pneuma, sovereignty, Egyptian symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 89, which concluded the discussion on the "Ruler of All" symbolized by a whole serpent. # THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a pair of human feet standing in a body of water, likely a river or stream. Reeds and grasses grow along the banks, and a tree stands on the right side. In the background, there are rolling hills and foliage.</image-desc>

->### How they signify a fuller.<-

When they wish to signify a **fuller** <note>original Greek: γναφέα (*gnaphea*). A fuller was a specialized craftsman who cleaned, shrunk, and thickened cloth by treading on it in vats of water and alkaline solution.</note>, they paint **two human feet in water**. They indicate this because of the similarity to **Hermes** <note>original Greek: ἑρμοῦ (*Hermou*). Hermes—the messenger god—is famously associated with the feet and travel; however, in the Egyptian context of this work, he is identified with Thoth, the god of wisdom and letters who was believed to oversee various crafts.</note>.

### 64 How a fuller.

To indicate a **fuller** <note>original Latin: fullonem</note>, they paint the **twin feet of a man in water**: they indicate this from a resemblance to **Mercury** <note>original Latin: Mercurii; the Roman equivalent of the Greek Hermes.</note>.

<gloss>Fuller: In the ancient world, laundry was a labor-intensive process. Fullers used their feet to stomp on fabric to remove oils and dirt, which explains the choice of "feet in water" as a literal and symbolic sign for the profession.</gloss>
<gloss>Hermes / Mercury: The author likely makes a connection here between the motion of the feet and the god of movement and travel. In ancient symbolic logic, the god's influence was seen in the rhythmic, "running" motion of the fuller's feet.</gloss>
<gloss>Hieroglyphics: While Horapollo presents these as ancient Egyptian symbols, they often reflect a mix of genuine Egyptian tradition and later Greek philosophical interpretation.</gloss>

<summary>This page describes the hieroglyph for a fuller—two feet in water—and links the symbol to the god Hermes or Mercury, likely due to the god's association with the feet and motion.</summary>
<keywords>fuller, feet, water, Hermes, Mercury, Thoth, trades, craftsmanship</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICA.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a celestial landscape. On the left, a personified sun with a face radiates light and stars. On the right, a crescent moon also features a profile face. Between them, numerous stars fill the sky above a landscape of rolling hills and a fortified town or castle in the distance.</image-desc>

->### How they signify a month.<-

When they depict a **month** <note>original Greek: Μῆνα (*Mēna*)</note>, they paint the **shape of the moon** <note>original Greek: σελήνης σχῆμα (*selēnēs schēma*)</note>, just as it has been previously set forth. This represents only **twenty-eight equinoctial days** <note>original Greek: εἴκοσι καὶ ὀκτὼ ἡμέρας ἰσημερινὰς (*eikosi kai oktō hēmeras isēmerinas*). In ancient astronomy, "equinoctial days" refers to a standard 24-hour period regardless of the season.</note>, with the day consisting of twenty-four hours, during which the moon rises. For the **remaining two days**, the moon is in setting <note>This refers to the "dark of the moon" or the new moon phase, where the moon is not visible to the naked eye, completing the roughly 30-day lunar cycle.</note>.

<margin>65</margin>
->### In what manner a month.<-

To express a **month** <note>original Latin: mensem</note>, they paint the **figure of the Moon** <note>original Latin: Lunæ figuram</note> in the same manner that was explained above. It encompasses only **twenty-eight equinoctial days**, the day being composed of twenty-four hours, during which the moon rises and is visible in our hemisphere; however, for the **remaining two**, it is in setting.

<gloss>Hieroglyphica: This work, attributed to an Egyptian priest named Horapollo, attempts to explain the symbolic meaning of hieroglyphs through a Greek and Latin lens, often blending genuine Egyptian tradition with Hellenistic philosophy.</gloss>
<gloss>Astronomy: The text reflects the ancient observation of the lunar cycle. A lunar month is approximately 29.5 days; the author accounts for the 28 days of visibility and the period where the moon "disappears" before the new crescent appears.</gloss>

<summary>This page explains the symbolic representation of a month using the moon, detailing the 28-day period of visibility and the two days during which the moon is hidden.</summary>
<keywords>month, moon, lunar cycle, astronomy, equinoctial days, Horapollo, timekeeping</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a crocodile on a riverbank or rocky shore. The crocodile is shown with its mouth open, revealing sharp teeth. In the background, there are architectural ruins including a stone archway and some foliage.</image-desc>

### How they signify a rapacious person, or one who is prolific, or mad.
When they wish to signify a **rapacious person** <note>original Greek: Ἅρπαγα (*Harpaga*), referring to someone greedy or grasping</note>, or one who is **prolific** <note>original Greek: πολύγονον (*polygomon*)</note>, or **mad** <note>original Greek: μαινόμενον (*mainomenon*)</note>, they paint a **crocodile** <note>original Greek: κροκόδειλον (*krokodeilon*)</note>. This is because the animal is remarkably prolific, producing many offspring, and is prone to fits of madness. For whenever it fails to seize the prey it desires, it becomes enraged and rages against itself <note>This reflects an ancient belief that the crocodile, when frustrated in the hunt, would exhibit self-destructive fury.</note>.

---

<margin>66</margin>
### In what manner a rapacious, fruitful, or raging person is signified.
Furthermore, wishing to suggest one who is **rapacious** <note>original Latin: rapacem</note>, **fruitful** <note>original Latin: fœcundum</note>, or **insane** <note>original Latin: insanum</note>, they paint a **crocodile**. This is because this animal is exceptionally fruitful and procreates many children; moreover, it is frequently driven into a frenzy. For whenever it has missed the prey it sought, it rages against itself in indignation.

<vocab>Horapollo: The purported author of the *Hieroglyphica*, likely a 5th-century Egyptian priest. Hieroglyphica: A treatise exploring the symbolic meanings of Egyptian hieroglyphs, which influenced Renaissance art and emblem books. Prolific: Producing many offspring or results.</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that the crocodile serves as a symbol for greed, high fertility, and self-destructive rage. It describes the creature's supposed habit of turning its anger inward when a hunt fails.</summary>
<keywords>crocodile, greed, fertility, madness, Horapollo, Egyptian symbolism, animal behavior, Renaissance emblems</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICA.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape with rolling hills, a distant fortified city or castle on a cliff, and clouds in the sky. Floating in the center of the sky are two large, disembodied human-like eyes looking forward.</image-desc>

->How they signify the sunrise.<-

When they wish to signify the **sunrise** <note>original Greek: ἀνατολή (*anatole*), referring to the rising of the sun or the East</note>, they paint **two eyes of a crocodile** <note>original Greek: δύο ὀφθαλμοὺς κροκοδείλου (*duo ophthalmous krokodeilou*)</note>. This is because the eyes of this animal are the first parts to appear from the deep <note>referring to the water's depths</note> before the rest of its body becomes visible.

<margin>67</margin>
->In what manner the rising is shown.<-

To represent the **rising** <note>original Latin: *ortum*, meaning the rising of a star or the sun</note> of the sun, they paint the **twin eyes of a crocodile**, for the eyes of this animal emerge as if from the very bottom of its body <note>The ancient observer noted that as a crocodile surfaces, its prominent eyes break the water's surface first, much like the sun appearing over the horizon</note>.

<vocab>**Hieroglyphics** (sacred carvings), **Sunrise** (the act of rising), **Rising** (emergence), **Crocodile**, **Eyes**</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that the Egyptians used the image of two crocodile eyes to symbolize the sunrise, based on the observation that a crocodile's eyes are the first part of its body to emerge from the water.</summary>
<keywords>sunrise, crocodile eyes, symbolism, Horapollo, emergence, Egyptian imagery</keywords>

### HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a crocodile with a curved, scaly body resting on a riverbank. In the background, there is a gnarled tree trunk, a body of water with a small boat, and distant buildings or ruins on a shoreline.</image-desc>

->How they signify the sunset.<-

When they wish to signify the **sunset** <note>original Greek: δύσιν (*dusin*), referring to the setting of the sun or the West</note>, they depict a **hunched crocodile** <note>original Greek: κροκόδειλον κεκυφότα (*krokodeilon kekuphota*)</note>; for this animal is naturally prolific and is inclined to move downwards <note>original Greek: κατωφερὲς (*katopheres*), describing a downward physical orientation or a tendency toward base instincts</note>.

<margin>68</margin> ->In what manner the sunset is shown.<-

To mark the **sunset** <note>original Latin: *occasum*, the "falling" or setting of the sun</note>, they paint a **crocodile bent and stooping forward**. This is because the animal gives birth with ease and is greatly inclined toward lust <note>original Latin: *venerem*, literally "Venus," used here to mean sexual desire or procreation</note>.

# HIEROGLYPHICS.

### How they represent darkness.

When they wish to speak of **darkness** <note>original Greek: σκότος (*skotos*), meaning gloom or the absence of light</note>, they depict the **tail of a crocodile**. This is because the crocodile cannot bring any animal it catches to disappearance and destruction in any other way, except by striking it with its own tail to render it powerless. For the strength and courage <note>original Greek: ἀνδρεία (*andreia*), literally "manliness" or "valor"</note> of the crocodile resides in this part of its body. Although there are many other symbols found in the nature of crocodiles, those mentioned in the first book are sufficient <note>This refers to the author's previous categorizations of the crocodile's traits elsewhere in the treatise</note>.

<margin>69</margin>
->How darkness is shadowed forth.<-

Furthermore, to signify darkness, they paint the tail of a crocodile. For the crocodile does not lead any animal it has seized to its end and utter destruction <note>original Latin: *internecionem*, suggesting a complete slaughter</note> in any other way, unless it first [strikes] with its tail...
<meta>The catchword "cæ-" indicates the next page begins with "cædere," the Latin word for striking or killing.</meta>

### OF HORUS APOLLO OF THE NILE, ON THE
### *Interpretation of the sacred letters carved*
### *among the Egyptians, the Second*
