# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORUS APOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a square border depicting a large five-pointed star in the sky, radiating numerous beams of light downward toward a landscape. Below the star, a figure appears to be reclining or sleeping on the ground amidst rolling hills and sparse vegetation. Clouds are visible in the upper corners.</image-desc>

### What they signify by drawing a star. <note>original Greek: Τί ἀσέρα [ἀστέρα] γράφοντες δηλοῦσιν</note>
A **star** <note>The Greek text here uses "ἀσὴρ" and "ἀσέρα," which are likely scribe or printer errors for "ἀστήρ" and "ἀστέρα," the standard words for star.</note> drawn among the Egyptians sometimes signifies a **god**, sometimes **night**, sometimes **time**, and sometimes the **soul of a male human**. <note>The Egyptians used a five-pointed star hieroglyph, known as *Seba*, to represent these concepts. The association with the soul reflects the belief that the deceased became "imperishable stars" in the night sky.</note>

---

### What they indicate by painting a star. <note>original Latin: Quid stellam pingentes, innuant</note>
A star depicted by the Egyptians now signifies a **god**, now **night**, now indeed **time**, and at times also the **soul of a male human**. <note>original Latin: Depicta Ægyptiis stella nūc deum significat, nunc noctem, nunc verò tempus, interdum etiā hominis masculi animam.</note>

<summary>This page describes the multiple meanings of the star hieroglyph, which serves as a symbol for divinity, the passage of time, and the human soul.</summary>
<keywords>star, Seba, Egyptian hieroglyphics, soul, divinity, time, night, Horapollo</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS.
G ij
<meta>catchword: What; bleed-through from reverse page visible</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting an eagle or large bird of prey perched on a rocky outcrop. In the background, there is a body of water and a stylized tree or palm to the right.</image-desc>

### ->What an eagle’s chick signifies.<- <note>original Greek: τί ἀετοῦ νεοσσόν [transcribed as νεοσσόμ]</note>

<margin>See our observations, 2.</margin>
An **eagle’s chick** signifies something that **begets males**, and something **circular**, or the **seed of a man**. <note>The eagle was often used in ancient symbolism to represent strength and paternity. The "circular" meaning might refer to the shape of the egg or the bird's high, soaring flight patterns. The Greek word used here is "ἀῤῥενογόνον" (arrenogonon), which specifically refers to the production of male offspring.</note>

---

### ->What the eagle’s chick signifies.<- <note>original Latin: Quid aquilæ pullum</note>

The **eagle’s chick** also connotes a **male**, and something **circular**, or the **human seed**. <note>original Latin: Et aquilæ pullus masculum, & orbiculatum quippiam vel semen hominis connotat.</note>

<summary>This page defines the symbolic associations of an eagle’s chick, which represents masculinity, circularity, and human procreation.</summary>

# OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a pair of human feet standing close together on a patch of ground. The background shows a landscape with rolling hills or water, and a sky filled with stylized clouds. A small plant or flower grows to the right of the feet.</image-desc>

### ->What two feet joined and standing signify.<- <note>original Greek: Τί δύο πόδας συνηγομένους καὶ βεβηκότας</note>

Two **feet joined and standing together** signify the **course of the sun** during the **winter solstice**. <note>The term "winter solstice" (original Greek: χειμεριναῖς τροπαῖς) literally translates to the "winter turnings," the point where the sun appears to stand still before reversing its path. The image of feet "standing together" likely represents this moment of solar pause or the restricted movement of the sun at its lowest point in the sky.</note>

### ->What twin feet joined and standing mean.<- <note>original Latin: Quid geminos pedes iunctos ac stantes</note>

Twin **feet**, joined and firmly set together, signify the **sun’s course** when it is at the **winter solstice**. <note>original Latin: Solis cursum cum in solstitio est hyberno. The Latin translation mirrors the Greek, reinforcing the idea of the sun's "standing" or "fixed" position during the shortest days of the year.</note>

<term>Horapollo</term> — The purported author of the *Hieroglyphica*, a work that attempted to explain Egyptian symbols to a Greek-speaking audience in late antiquity.
<term>Solstice</term> — Derived from the Latin *sol* (sun) and *sistere* (to stand still).

<summary>This page explains that the image of two feet joined together is a hieroglyphic symbol for the sun's path during the winter solstice.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, Hieroglyphica, Sun, winter solstice, solar course, feet, Egyptian symbolism, Greek translation, Latin translation</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICA

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a muscular, bearded nude man standing in a landscape. He leans on a staff with his right hand and rests his left hand on his hip. In the background, there is a river, a stone bridge with arches, and buildings resembling a fortified city or castle under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

### ->What a human heart hanging from a throat signifies.<- <note>original Greek: Τί ἀνθρώπου καρδίαν φάρυγγος ἠρτημένην</note>

A **human heart hanging from a throat** signifies the **mouth of a good man**. <note>In ancient thought, the "mouth" often refers to a person's speech or discourse. This symbol suggests that a virtuous person's words are physically and morally bound to their heart, implying that they speak only what is true and sincere.</note>

### ->What a human heart suspended from the jaws means.<- <note>original Latin: Quid hominis cor faucibus appensum</note>

A **human heart suspended from the jaws** designates the **mouth of a good man**. <note>original Latin: boni viri os designat. This Latin translation follows the Greek closely, reinforcing the moral connection between the seat of the soul (the heart) and the organ of expression (the mouth/jaws).</note>

<term>Hieroglyphics</term> — (from Latin *Hieroglyphica*) The "sacred carvings" of Egypt. By the time this text was written, the original phonetic use of hieroglyphs was largely forgotten, and they were interpreted as purely symbolic or allegorical puzzles.
<term>Heart</term> — (Greek: *kardia*; Latin: *cor*) In the ancient world, the heart was considered the center of thought, memory, and moral character, rather than just a pump for blood.
<term>Jaws/Throat</term> — (Greek: *pharynx*; Latin: *fauces*) These terms refer to the physical apparatus of the voice. To "hang the heart" from these parts suggests that every word spoken is supported by the heart's integrity.

<summary>This page describes a hieroglyphic symbol consisting of a heart hanging from a throat, which represents the honest and virtuous speech of a "good man." It reflects the ancient belief that the heart is the source of a person's true character and that speech should be an extension of that inner truth.</summary>

<keywords>heart, throat, speech, virtue, moral character, Horapollo, symbolism, Egyptian hieroglyphs, integrity</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a suit of armor (cuirass and helmet) resting on the ground or a base. To the right, a disembodied hand emerges from a cloud holding a large upright bow. Another hand appears to be holding a shield or part of the armor on the left. Clouds are visible in the upper background.</image-desc>

### ->How to represent the mouth of war.<- <note>original Greek: Πῶς πολέμου στόμα</note>

To represent the **mouth of war**, they depict **human hands**, one holding a **shield** <note>original Greek: ὅπλον (*hoplon*), which refers to a piece of armor or specifically a large shield used by infantry.</note> and the other a **bow**.

### ->How to represent the battle line and the front of an army drawn up for battle.<- <note>original Latin: Quomodo belli aciem, ac frontem exercitus ad prælium instructi</note>

They indicate the **battle line** <note>The Latin *aciem* refers to the sharp edge of a blade, but metaphorically describes an army drawn up in a sharp, ready-to-strike formation.</note> by depicting **human hands**, one holding a **shield** and the other a **bow**.

<term>Mouth of War</term> — (Greek: *polemou stoma*) A metaphorical expression referring to the vanguard or the very front line of an army where the initial "bite" of combat occurs.
<term>Hands</term> — In this symbolic system, disembodied hands represent action and preparation. The pairing of a defensive tool (the shield) and an offensive tool (the bow) captures the dual nature of a military engagement.
<term>Horapollo</term> — The purported author of the *Hieroglyphica*, a work that attempted to explain Egyptian symbols to a Greek-speaking audience, often blending genuine Egyptian tradition with later Greek philosophical interpretations.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic representation of an army's front line (the "mouth of war") using the imagery of human hands grasping a shield and a bow. It demonstrates how abstract military concepts were translated into visual allegories during the Renaissance interpretation of hieroglyphics.</summary>

<keywords>Horapollo, Hieroglyphica, mouth of war, battle line, military symbolism, shield, bow, Greek translation, Latin translation</keywords>

### ->What the finger signifies.<- <note>original Greek: τί δάκτυλον; original Latin: Quid digitum</note>

### ->A finger signifies a human stomach.<- <note>original Greek: Ἀνθρώπου στόμαχον δηλοῖ δάκτυλος; original Latin: Hominis stomachum demonstrat digitus</note> 6

<term>Finger</term> — (Greek: *daktylos*) In this symbolic system, the finger represents the stomach. Ancient commentators suggested this was because the finger is involved in "tasting" or because they believed the finger had a unique relationship to the process of digestion.

->† What a genital organ held in the hand signifies.<- <note>original Greek: Τί αἰδοῖον χειρὶ κρατούμενον.</note>

A genital organ held in the hand signifies a man’s self-control. <note>original Greek: Αἰδοῖον χειρὶ κρατούμενον, σωφροσύνην δηλοῖ ἀνθρώπου.</note>

<margin>7</margin> ->What a private part held by the hand signifies.<- <note>original Latin: Quid pudendum manu compressum.</note>

A penis restrained and covered by the hand signifies a man’s self-restraint. <note>original Latin: Penis manu cohibitus ac tectus, continentiam hominis innuit.</note>

<term>Self-control</term> — (Greek: *sophrosyne*) In Greek ethics, this is the virtue of moderation and temperance, characterized by the ability to master one's desires and impulses through reason.
<term>Self-restraint</term> — (Latin: *continentia*) The Latin translation emphasizes "holding together" or containing oneself, particularly in the face of physical temptation.
<term>Genital organ</term> — (Greek: *aidoion* / Latin: *pudendum*) Both the Greek and Latin terms used in the text carry a sense of "shame" or "modesty," referring to parts of the body that were traditionally covered in public.

# HIEROGLYPHICA

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of an anemone plant with two large, drooping flowers and jagged leaves, set against a landscape with a distant hill and some grass tufts.</image-desc>

->+ How they signify disease.<- <note>original Greek: Πῶς νόσον δηλοῦσιν.</note>

**The flowers of the anemone signify a man's disease.** <note>original Greek: Ἄνθη ἡ ἀνεμώνης, νόσον ἀνθρώπου σημαίνει.</note>

->How they signify disease.<- <note>original Latin: Quomodo morbum significent.</note>

The flowers of the anemone, moreover, signify a person's sickness. <note>original Latin: Flores autem anemones, morbum hominis significant.</note> <insert>The reason for this is hidden from me. Look to <term>Dioscorides</term>; perhaps you will deduce the meaning from the description of the medicinal properties of the anemone, Book 2, chapter 167, on my page 253.</insert> <note>original Latin: Causa latet me, Vide Dioscoridem, fortè ex descriptione Virtutum anemones eam auguraberis...</note>

<term>Dioscorides</term> — Pedanius Dioscorides was a Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD. His encyclopedia *De Materia Medica* was the standard European text on herbal medicine for over 1,500 years.
<term>Anemone</term> — Derived from the Greek word for "wind" (*anemos*), the anemone or "windflower" was often associated in antiquity with the fleeting nature of life and the onset of illness due to its delicate, easily scattered petals.
<term>Hieroglyphica</term> — This refers to a work attributed to Horapollo, which claimed to explain Egyptian hieroglyphs. While many of its explanations are based on symbolic folklore rather than linguistic facts, it was highly influential during the Renaissance.

<summary>This page explains that the anemone flower serves as a hieroglyphic symbol for human disease. The text includes a scholar's marginal note admitting uncertainty about the symbol's origin and directing the reader to the botanical works of Dioscorides for further research.</summary>

<keywords>anemone, disease, sickness, botanical symbolism, Dioscorides, windflower, Horapollo, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

### + What a private part held in the hand signifies. <note>original Greek: Τί αἰδοῖον χειρὶ κρατούμενον.</note>
**A private part held in the hand signifies a man’s temperance.** <note>original Greek: Αἰδοῖον χειρὶ κρατούμενον, σωφροσύνην δηλοῖ ἀνθρώπου.</note>

### 7 What a private part pressed by the hand signifies. <note>original Latin: Quid pudendum manu compressum.</note>
A penis restrained and covered by the hand suggests the self-control of a man. <note>original Latin: Penis manu cohibitus ac tectus, continentiam hominis innuit.</note>

<term>Aidoion</term> — Derived from the Greek word *aidos* (meaning "shame" or "reverence"), this term refers to the genitals. In ancient thought, these were the "private parts" that a person of character was expected to govern.
<term>Sophrosyne</term> — A major Greek virtue representing "soundness of mind." It is often translated as temperance, moderation, or self-restraint. To the Greeks, mastering one's physical impulses was the mark of a civilized man.

<term>Horapollo</term> — The purported author of the *Hieroglyphica*. While the text claims to explain ancient Egyptian symbols, it is largely a product of Late Antiquity, blending genuine Egyptian lore with Greek philosophical interpretations.

<summary>This page describes an allegorical symbol of a hand restraining the genitals, which Horapollo interprets as a representation of human temperance and self-control.</summary>

->### + How they signify disease.<- <note>original Greek: Πῶς νόσον δηλοῦσιν.</note>

**The flowers of an anemone signify disease in a human being.** <note>original Greek: Ἄνθη δ’ ἀνεμώνης, νόσον ἀνθρώπῳ σημαίνει.</note>

->### 8 How they signify disease.<- <note>original Latin: Quomodo morbum significent.</note>

Moreover, the flowers of the anemone signify a man’s disease. <note>original Latin: Flores autem anemones, morbum hominis significant.</note> <insert>The reason for this is hidden from me; see Dioscorides. Perhaps you will deduce the reason from his description of the *Virtues of the Anemone*, Book 2, chapter 167, page 253 in my edition.</insert> <note>original Latin: Causa latet me, Vide Dioscoridem, fortè Ex descriptione Virtutum anemones eam auguraberis...</note>

<term>Hieroglyphica</term> — A work traditionally attributed to Horapollo of Egypt, attempting to explain the symbolic meanings of Egyptian hieroglyphs through a Hellenistic lens.
<term>Anemone</term> — From the Greek *anemos* (wind), often called the "windflower." In this context, it is used as a symbol for illness, perhaps due to the fragile or fleeting nature of its petals, which are easily scattered by the wind.
<term>Morbus</term> — Latin for sickness or disease. In the symbolic language of this text, the physical state of the flower reflects a state of human health.
<term>Dioscorides</term> — Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and pharmacologist. His work *De Materia Medica* was the primary source for botanical and medicinal knowledge in the Renaissance.
<term>Virtutum anemones</term> — Literally "The Virtues of the Anemone." In Renaissance herbalism, "virtues" referred to the specific medicinal properties or powers a plant possessed to heal or affect the body.

<summary>This page explains that the anemone flower is a symbol for human disease in the hieroglyphic tradition. A handwritten insertion directs the reader to the works of the ancient physician Dioscorides to find a scientific or medicinal justification for this symbolic link.</summary>

->### How they signify sickness.<- <note>original Greek: Πῶς νόσον δηλοῦσιν.</note>

**The flowers of the anemone signify a man’s sickness.** <note>original Greek: Ἄνθη ἡ ἀνεμώνης, νόσον ἀνθρώπου σημαίνει.</note>

->### How they signify disease.<- <note>original Latin: Quomodo morbum significent.</note>

Moreover, the flowers of the anemone signify a man’s disease. <insert>The reason for this is hidden from me; see the works of Dioscorides; perhaps from his description of the *Medicinal Virtues of the Anemone* you will be able to deduce the meaning. See Book 2, chapter 167, page 253 in my own copy.</insert>

<term>Hieroglyphica</term> — The title of the work attributed to Horapollo, an Egyptian author of the 5th century. This text was a cornerstone for Renaissance thinkers who believed Egyptian hieroglyphs were purely symbolic or mystical rebuses.
<term>Anemone</term> — Known as the "windflower" (from the Greek *anemos*), it was noted in antiquity for the speed with which its petals fall. Its fragile nature likely contributed to its use as a symbol for the onset of illness or the frailty of the human body.
<term>Morbus</term> — The Latin term for "disease" or "sickness." In this context, it refers to the physical corruption of the body that the anemone symbolizes.
<term>Dioscorides</term> — Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician whose encyclopedia of herbal medicine, *De Materia Medica*, was the standard reference for botanists and doctors throughout the Middle Ages and the Renaissance.
<term>Virtues of the Anemone</term> (Virtutum anemones) — In early science, "virtues" referred to the inherent powers or medicinal properties of a plant. Dioscorides noted that the anemone could be used to treat inflammations and clear the skin, but it was also known for its potency.

<summary>This page describes the anemone flower as a hieroglyphic symbol for human sickness and disease. The commentator notes his uncertainty regarding the origin of this symbol and suggests readers consult the ancient botanist Dioscorides for further clues.</summary>

# OF HORAPOLLO

<page-num>106</page-num>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a human spinal column (vertebrae) standing upright in a landscape setting with small plants and a low wall in the background.</image-desc>

->### How to represent the loin of a man.<- <note>original Greek: πῶς ὀσφὺν ἀνθρώπου.</note>

When they wish to depict the **loin**, or the **upright stance** of a man, they draw the **backbone**. <note>original Greek: τὸ νωτιαῖον ὀστοῦν.</note> For some say that the **seed** <note>original Greek: τὸ σπέρμα; here referring to semen or the generative essence.</note> is carried from there.

<margin>9</margin> ->### How the loin of a man is represented.<- <note>original Latin: Quo modo lumbum hominis.</note>

Wishing to mark the **loin** or the **standing posture and constitution** of a man, we paint the **bone of the back's spine**. For there are those who say that the seed flows down from this place.

<term>Loin</term> — In ancient anatomy, the "loins" (the area of the lower back and hips) were considered the seat of a person's physical strength and their procreative power. 

<term>Backbone</term> — The spinal column represents "stability" or "constitution." In the symbolic logic of this text, the spine is the central pillar that allows a human to stand upright, distinguishing them from animals.

<term>The Seed from the Spine</term> — This reflects an ancient medical theory (often associated with the Hippocratic school or certain Pythagorean beliefs) that semen was a drop of brain or spinal marrow. Because the "seed" was thought to travel through the spine, the backbone became a symbol for the source of life and inheritance.

<summary>This page explains why a human backbone is used in hieroglyphs to represent the loins or a man's physical constitution, citing the ancient belief that semen originates in the spinal column.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, hieroglyphics, anatomy, backbone, spine, loins, semen, human constitution, stability</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICA.

<page-num>107</page-num>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape with rolling hills, trees, and clouds. In the center of the sky, a single bone (likely a quail's bone) is suspended horizontally.</image-desc>

->### How to represent permanence and safety.<- <note>original Greek: πῶς διαμονὴν, καὶ ἀσφάλειαν σημαίνῃ.</note>

The painted **bone of a quail** <margin>they signify <note>original: σημαίνουσι; a plural variation of the verb.</note></margin> signifies **permanence** and **safety**, because the bone of this animal is **difficult to affect**. <note>original Greek: δυσπαθές (dyspathes); describing something that is hardy, resilient, or resistant to external suffering and decay.</note>

---

->### How they signify something permanent and safe.<- <note>original Latin: Quomodo permanens aliquid & tutum significent.</note>

A painted **bone of a quail** indicates something **stable** and **safe**, because the bone of this animal is with difficulty affected or suffers anything.

<vocab>Hieroglyphics, quail (Greek: ortyx), Quail (Latin: Coturnix), permanence (Greek: diamone), safety (Greek: asphaleia), bone</vocab>

<term>Quail (Ortyx / Coturnix)</term> — In the ancient world, the quail was often observed for its migratory stamina and perceived hardiness. Here, the author focuses on its skeletal strength as a metaphor for resilience.

<term>Permanence and Safety</term> — In the symbolic language of Horapollo, physical traits of animals (like the density of a bone) are used to represent abstract human virtues or states of being. If a bone is "difficult to affect" or break, it becomes a logical symbol for a "safe" or "permanent" condition.

<summary>This page describes the quail's bone as a hieroglyphic symbol for permanence and safety, attributing this meaning to the bone's natural durability. It demonstrates the ancient practice of using biological resilience to represent abstract concepts of stability.</summary>

<keywords>quail, bone, permanence, safety, stability, resilience, Horapollo, animal symbolism</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<page-num>108</page-num>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two men in long robes and hats standing outdoors near a building with a window. They appear to be in conversation, with one man gesturing towards the other. In the background, a landscape with hills is visible.</image-desc>

->### How they represent concord.<- <note>original Greek: Πῶς ὁμόνοιαν.</note>

**Two men** who are held in high esteem indicate **concord**. <note>original Greek: Ἄνθρωποι δύο ἀξιούμενοι (Anthrōpoi dyo axioumenoi). The word 'axioumenoi' refers to men who are "deemed worthy" or of high social standing.</note>

---

->### In what way they represent concord.<- <note>original Latin: Quo modo concordiam.</note>

**Two men**, magistrates adorned with their **insignia**, suggest **concord**. <note>original Latin: magistratus ornati insignibus. This specifies that the men are civic officials wearing the symbols of their rank and office.</note>

<term>Concord (Homonoia / Concordia)</term> — A state of agreement or harmony, particularly between citizens or within a government. In ancient symbolism, the image of two leaders standing together suggests a unified state where there is no internal strife.

<term>Insignia</term> — Decorative marks, badges, or specific clothing that indicate a person's high rank or membership in a specific group. In this woodcut, the hats and long robes serve as these markers of status.

<term>Magistrates</term> — Civil officers or administrators. The Latin translation of the Greek "worthy men" specifically identifies these figures as government officials, emphasizing that concord is a vital political virtue.

<summary>This page explains how the image of two high-ranking men or magistrates is used as a symbol for concord and social harmony. It illustrates the shift from physical symbols (like bones) to social symbols (like officials) in the Hieroglyphica.</summary>

<keywords>concord, harmony, magistrates, insignia, Horapollo, symbolism, civic virtue</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 108, which described the symbolism of "two worthy men" or magistrates as a representation of concord and civil harmony. Page 109 presents the opposing social state—disorder and the mob—symbolized by an individual soldier in action.</meta>

# HIEROGLYPHICA

<page-num>109</page-num>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a soldier in classical armor and a plumed helmet standing beneath a tree. He is drawing a large bow and aiming an arrow toward the right. In the background, a landscape with rolling hills and a body of water is visible.</image-desc>

->### How they represent a crowd.<- <note>original Greek: πῶς ὄχλον (pōs ochlon).</note>

**A man fully armed**, who is also **shooting arrows**, signifies a **crowd**. <note>original Greek: Ἄνθρωπος καθωπλισμένος, καὶ τοξεύων, ὄχλον σημαίνει. The term 'kathōplismenos' implies being equipped with a full suit of heavy armor.</note>

---

->### In what way they represent a crowd and a tumult. 12<- <note>original Latin: Quo modo turbam ac tumultum. The number 12 refers to the chapter or entry number in this edition.</note>

**An armed man** who is shooting arrows signifies a **tumult**. <note>original Latin: Armatus homo sagittasque iaculans, tumultum significat. The Latin 'tumultum' carries a stronger sense of an uprising or a chaotic disturbance than the Greek 'ochlon'.</note>

<term>Crowd / Mob (Ochlos)</term> — In this context, the term refers to a large, often unorganized group of people. In ancient political thought, the "ochlos" was frequently viewed with suspicion as a source of instability.

<term>Tumult (Tumultus)</term> — A Latin term for a sudden breakout of violence, a riot, or a civil uprising. It often specifically referred to an emergency state of war or a rebellion within or near Roman territory.

<term>Armed Man (Armatus homo)</term> — While a single soldier appears in the image, he represents the many. The act of shooting arrows—projectiles that fly in many directions—serves as a metaphor for the scattered, unpredictable, and dangerous nature of a rioting crowd. Just as a soldier brings violence to a scene, a mob brings "tumult" to a city.

<vocab>Hieroglyphics, crowd/mob, tumult/uproar, armed man, arrow</vocab>

<summary>This page explains how the image of a single armed soldier firing arrows is used to symbolize a chaotic crowd or a civil uprising. It contrasts the orderly "concord" of the previous page with the "tumult" of social disorder.</summary>
<keywords>mob, riot, soldier, archery, symbolism, social disorder, Horapollo</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<page-num>110</page-num>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape with a large, disembodied human finger pointing downwards from the clouds. Below, there is a fortified city or castle with towers and a bridge over a river, set against a backdrop of hills and trees.</image-desc>

->### How they represent measurement.<- <note>original Greek: πῶς ἀναμέτρησιν (pōs anametrēsin). This refers to the act of surveying or calculating dimensions.</note>

->**A man’s finger** signifies **measurement**.<- <note>original Greek: Ἀνθρώπου δάκτυλος, ἀναμέτρησιν σημαίνει (Anthrōpou daktylos, anametrēsin sēmainei).</note>

### In what way they represent dimension.
<margin>13</margin> **A human finger** marks **dimension**. <note>original Latin: Hominis digitus dimensionem notat. The number 13 identifies this as the thirteenth entry in this book.</note>

<term>Measurement (Anametrēsis)</term> — A Greek term used for surveying land or measuring proportions. In the ancient world, the human body was the primary standard for almost all physical measurements, from the width of a finger to the length of a foot.

<term>Finger (Digitus)</term> — The "digit" or finger-breadth was one of the smallest standard units of measure in antiquity (approximately 1.9 cm or 0.75 inches). In this symbolic system, a single finger represents the foundational principle of geometry and construction: the ability to divide and quantify the world.

<summary>This page describes the human finger as a hieroglyphic symbol for measurement and dimension. It reflects the ancient Egyptian and Greco-Roman reliance on the human body as the basis for mathematical and architectural standards.</summary>

<keywords>Horapollo, hieroglyphics, measurement, finger, dimension, surveying, units of measure, geometry</keywords>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape with rolling hills, trees, and a small building or tower in the distance. In the sky, a radiant sun with a human face is positioned next to a large, multi-pointed star. Clouds frame the celestial bodies.</image-desc>

<margin>
<unclear>depiction</unclear> <note>original Greek: ἀπεικονισμῷ (apeikonismō), meaning a representation or a likeness.</note>
</margin>

->† ### How they represented a woman.<- <note>original Greek: Πῶς γυναῖκα ἔθυον (Pōs gynaika ethyon). The Greek verb literally means "sacrificed," but in this context and based on the Latin below, it is likely a transcription error or variant for "depicted" or "wrote."</note>

When they wish to signify a **woman**, they use the **circle of the sun** together with a **star**, along with the **disk of the sun** itself **cut in two**.

->### How they represent a betrothed woman.<- <note>original Latin: quomodo desponsatam mulierem.</note>

Wishing to show a **betrothed woman**, they paint the **circle of the sun**, together with a **star** and the **sun’s disk** itself **divided in two**. <note>The "betrothed woman" or "desponsata mulier" refers to a woman who has been promised in marriage but is not yet wed.</note>

<term>Hieroglyphica</term> — The title of the work by Horapollo, an author who attempted to explain Egyptian hieroglyphs as purely symbolic or allegorical puzzles rather than a phonetic writing system.

<term>Betrothed woman (Desponsatam mulierem)</term> — In Renaissance heraldry and symbolism, a woman promised in marriage. The imagery of a star and a halved sun likely represents the union of two celestial or social "lights" coming together to form a whole.

<term>Divided sun-disk (Disco solis bipartito)</term> — A visual motif where the sun is split into two halves. This may symbolize the transition from a single state to a shared one, or perhaps the "halving" of a woman's identity as she joins another household.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic representation of a woman, specifically one who is betrothed, using celestial imagery of the sun and stars. It reflects the Renaissance fascination with using astronomical bodies to represent human social contracts and identities.</summary>

<keywords>Horapollo, symbolism, woman, betrothal, sun, star, marriage, celestial imagery</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large hawk or eagle with wings spread wide, perched on a rocky outcrop. ->### How they signify the wind.<- <note>original Greek: Πῶς ἄνεμον (Pōs anemon).</note>

A **hawk** flying high toward the **east** <note>original Greek: ἀνατολὴν (anatolēn), referring to the sunrise</note> signifies **winds**. Furthermore, and in another way, a **hawk** with its wings spread out in the air—as if the wind itself possessed wings—signifies the **wind**.

<margin>15</margin> ->### In what way they represent wind.<- <note>original Latin: Quo modo ventum.</note>

A **hawk** <term>Accipiter</term> <note>The Latin term for a hawk or bird of prey; here it translates the Greek ἱέραξ (hierax).</note> stretching toward the **sunrise** on high shows the **winds**. Also, and otherwise: a **hawk** having expanded wings in the air signifies the **wind**, which is seen as having certain wings of its own.

<vocab>Hieroglyphica, Horapollo, hawk, wind</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the symbolic use of the hawk to represent wind in Egyptian hieroglyphics, focusing on its flight toward the rising sun and its outstretched wings. It reflects the classical belief that the physical behavior of animals could embody abstract natural forces.</summary>

<header>THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO</header>

->How they signify a long-lived person.<- <note>original Greek: Πῶς πολυχρόνιον (Pōs polychronion)</note>

A **stag** regrows its antlers every year; when it is depicted, it signifies someone or something that is **long-lived**. <note>Ancient naturalists like Hesiod and Pliny believed stags could live for several centuries. The annual shedding and regrowth of antlers was seen as a symbol of cyclical renewal and enduring life.</note>

<margin>21</margin> ->In what way it signifies the long-lived and lasting.<- <note>original Latin: Quo modo longæuum ac diuturnum</note>

To stags, antlers are reborn every year. This creature, when depicted, signifies a **very long life**.

<summary>This page explains that the stag is used in Egyptian hieroglyphs to represent longevity because of the animal's supposedly long lifespan and the annual renewal of its antlers.</summary>

# HORAPOLLO <note>original: *Ori Apollinis*</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a night scene in a forest. An owl (nycticorax) is perched on a gnarled tree branch on the right, while another bird appears to be in flight or swooping down below it. The landscape includes thick tree trunks with exposed roots on the left and rocky terrain in the foreground.</image-desc>

->How death is signified.<- <note>original Greek: Πῶς θάνατον (*Pōs thanaton*)</note>

The **night-raven** <note>The *nycticorax* is a bird of omen in ancient lore, often associated with the long-eared owl or the night heron.</note> signifies **death**; for it comes suddenly upon its young chicks during the night, just as death comes suddenly upon us.

<margin>25</margin> ->In what way death is signified.<- <note>original Latin: *Quo modo mortem*</note>

The **night-raven** signifies death: for just as this bird attacks its chicks suddenly by night, so also does death unexpectedly invade and overwhelm mankind.

<term>Horapollo</term>: The purported author of the *Hieroglyphica*, a work that attempts to explain Egyptian symbols through a Greek lens.
<term>Nycticorax</term> (νυκτικόραξ): Literally "night-raven." In the ancient world, its cry was considered a portent of death.
<term>Thanatos</term> (θάνατος): The Greek personification or concept of death.
<term>Mors</term>: The Latin word for death.

<summary>This page presents Chapter 25 of the Hieroglyphica, identifying the night-raven as a symbol for death. The bird's habit of attacking chicks unexpectedly in the dark serves as a metaphor for the sudden and unpredictable nature of mortality.</summary>
<keywords>night-raven, owl, death, suddenness, mortality, Horapollo, omens</keywords>

->How love is signified.<- <note>original Greek: πῶς ἔρωτα (*pōs erōta*)</note>

->A **snare** signifies **love**, like a hunt in the<-
->air.<- <note>original Greek: Παγὶς ἔρωτα ὡς θήραν τὸν ἀέρα σημαίνει (*Pagis erōta hōs thēran ton aera sēmainei*). This suggests that love captures the soul much like a fowler's net captures a bird in flight.</note>

### 26. In what way love is signified. <note>original Latin: *Quo modo amorem*</note>

A **snare** signifies **love**, as it does a certain wild beast. <note>original Latin: *Laqueus amorem, ut feram quandam significat*. The Latin implies that love "traps" the person, treating them as prey to be caught.</note>

<term>Hieroglyphica</term>: The title of the work, referring to the "sacred carvings" of Egypt, which the author Horapollo attempts to interpret allegorically.
<term>Snare</term> (Παγὶς / Laqueus): A trap, usually a noose or net. In this context, it symbolizes the entanglement and inescapable nature of romantic desire.
<term>Love</term> (ἔρως / Amor): Specifically referring to passionate or romantic love, which "captures" the heart of the individual.
<term>Wild beast</term> (fera): A metaphor for the object of the hunt; here, the person who is "hunted" and caught by love.

<summary>This page presents Chapter 26 of the Hieroglyphica, explaining that a snare or knotted trap is the symbol for love. The imagery reflects the ancient concept of love as a force that hunts and entangles its subject like a wild animal or a bird.</summary>

# HIEROGLYPHICA <note>Sacred carvings or Egyptian symbols.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a wooden bookshelf or library cabinet. On the top shelf, two large books are open, showing text. On the middle shelf, several bound books are stacked or leaning, with wax seals hanging from ribbons or cords attached to them. A single book with a seal lies on the floor or bottom surface in the foreground.</image-desc>

->How the most ancient is signified.<- <note>original Greek: πῶς παλαιότατον (*pōs palaiotaton*)</note>

**Words** and **leaves**, or a **sealed book**, signifies the **most ancient**. <note>original Greek: Λόγοι καὶ φύλλα, ἢ βιβλίον ἐσφραγισμένον, παλαιότατον δηλοῖ (*Logoi kai phylla, ē biblion esphragismenon, palaiotaton dēloi*). This imagery links the passage of time with the accumulation of written records and the preservation of secret or finished knowledge.</note>

<margin>27</margin> ->In what way the most ancient.<- <note>original Latin: *Quo modo vetustissimum*</note>

**Speeches** and **leaves**, or a **sealed book**, indicate the **most ancient**. <note>original Latin: *Sermones & folia, aut signatus liber, vetustissimum indicant*. In the Renaissance view of Egypt, the "sealed book" was a common metaphor for the mysterious and ancient wisdom of the priests.</note>

<term>Ancient</term> (παλαιότατον / vetustissimum): Referring to the greatest possible age or the origins of time and tradition.
<term>Leaves</term> (φύλλα / folia): This likely refers to papyrus sheets or the pages of a manuscript. In ancient thought, the "leaves of time" or written records were the only way to bridge the gap to the distant past.
<term>Sealed book</term> (βιβλίον ἐσφραγισμένον / signatus liber): A book that is closed and secured with a seal. This represents knowledge that is completed, authoritative, and protected from the corruption of the present day.

<summary>This page presents Chapter 27 of the Hieroglyphica, explaining that ancient things are symbolized by written words, leaves (manuscript pages), or a sealed book. These symbols reflect the importance of written records in preserving the history and secrets of antiquity.</summary>

<keywords>antiquity, ancient, sealed book, manuscript, leaves, Horapollo, symbolism, Egyptian wisdom</keywords>

<term>Siege</term> (πολιορκίαν / obsidionem): The military act of surrounding a city or fortress to compel its surrender.
<term>Ladder</term> (κλίμαξ / scala): A primary tool of ancient and medieval warfare used to scale defensive walls.
<term>Uneven or Steep</term> (ἀνώμαλον / inæqualis ... acclivis): These terms describe the physical properties of the ladder—its rungs create an "uneven" surface compared to a flat path, and its "steep" angle is necessary to reach the battlements.

<summary>This page explains that the image of a ladder is used in hieroglyphic tradition to represent a siege, owing to the ladder's steep and irregular structure used for scaling walls.</summary>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORUS APOLLO <note>original Latin: *Ori Apollinis*. This refers to Horapollo, the purported author of this treatise on Egyptian symbols.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a framed tablet or monument set within an architectural vaulted space. On the tablet, two upright fingers are shown, inscribed with seven small characters or symbols.</image-desc>

->How they represent the infinite, or a muse, or fate.<- <note>original Greek: πῶς ἄπειρον, ἢ μοῦσαν, ἢ μοῖραν (*pōs apeiron, ē mousan, ē moiran*)</note>

<margin>Letters</margin> <note>original Greek: γράμματα (*grammata*)</note>
\* Seven characters <note>original Greek: πράγματα (*pragmata*), literally "things" or "matters," but here referring to the written marks.</note> contained within two fingers signify a muse, or the infinite, or fate.

<margin>29</margin>
->How they represent the inexperienced, a muse, or fate.<- <note>original Latin: *Quo modo inexpertum, musam, aut fatum*. Note that the Latin translator rendered the Greek *apeiron* (infinite/limitless) as *inexpertum* (untested/inexperienced), a common variation in early modern editions.</note>
Seven letters enclosed by two fingers signify a muse, the inexperienced, or fate. <note>original Latin: *Literæ septem duobus inclusæ digitis, musā, inexpertum, aut fatum significant*.</note>

<term>Infinite</term> (ἄπειρον / *apeiron*): That which has no limit or end. In this context, it may refer to the vastness of knowledge or the eternity of fate.
<term>Muse</term> (μοῦσαν / *musa*): One of the nine Greek deities presiding over the arts and sciences; here used as a symbol for education or the written word.
<term>Fate</term> (μοῖραν / *fatum*): The predetermined course of events or the destiny assigned to a person.
<term>Seven Characters</term>: The number seven was often considered sacred or complete in antiquity, representing a full cycle or the totality of knowledge.

<summary>This page describes Chapter 29, explaining that the image of seven characters held between two fingers is a hieroglyphic symbol for a Muse, the concept of the Infinite, or Fate.</summary>

# HIEROGLYPHICS

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape with rolling hills, a small bridge over a stream, and a tree on the right. In the sky above the landscape, two lines are drawn: one vertical straight line intersected by a diagonal line leaning to the left, forming a shape similar to the letter 'X' or a slanted cross.</image-desc>

->What a line bent over another signifies.<- <note>original Greek: Τί σημαίνει γραμμὴ, ἐπικεκαμμένη ἑτέρᾳ (*Ti sēmainei grammē, epikekammenē hetera*)</note>

One straight line, together with a line bent over it, signifies ten flat lines. <note>original Greek: Γραμμὴ ὀρϑὴ μία, ἅμα γραμμῇ ἐπικεκαμμένῃ, δέκα γραμμὰς ἐπιπέδας σημαίνουσι. The phrase "flat lines" (*grammas epipedas*) likely refers to a basic unit of measurement or a surface area in ancient geometry.</note>

<margin>30</margin>
->What a line bent over another signifies.<- <note>original Latin: *Quid linea significet super alteram inflexa*.</note>

A straight line, together with another bent at the top, indicates ten flat lines. <note>original Latin: *Recta linea simul cum altera supernè inflexa, decem planas lineas indicat*.</note>

<term>Line</term> (γραμμὴ / *linea*): In this context, a stroke or mark used in a symbolic or mathematical system.
<term>Bent</term> (ἐπικεκαμμένῃ / *inflexa*): Angled or curved; here describing the intersection of two strokes to form a specific character.
<term>Ten</term> (δέκα / *decem*): The number ten. Horapollo suggests that this specific geometric arrangement was the Egyptian method for representing the decimal base.
<term>Flat lines</term> (ἐπιπέδας / *planas*): Refers to "plane" or "surface" lines in geometry, suggesting these symbols were used in land measurement or calculating areas.

<summary>This page describes Chapter 30 of the Hieroglyphics, explaining how a vertical line intersected by an angled line serves as a symbol for the number ten. It reflects ancient attempts to link Egyptian hieroglyphic or marks to Greek mathematical concepts.</summary>
<keywords>numerology, mathematics, number ten, geometry, symbols, Horapollo</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a swallow in flight over a body of water. In the foreground, a large, gnarled tree stands on the left bank. In the background, there is a hilly landscape with a small cluster of buildings or a villa overlooking the water.</image-desc>

### ->What they signify by depicting a swallow.<- <note>original Greek: Τί δηλοῦσι χελιδόνα γράφοντες (*Ti dēlousi chelidona graphontes*)</note>

When they wish to signify an entire paternal inheritance left to children, they paint a swallow. For that bird rolls herself in mud and builds a nest for her chicks when she is about to die. <note>original Greek: Τὴν ὁλοχερῆ σημαίνειν βουλόμενοι κτῆσιν γονικὴν καταλειφθεῖσαν τοῖς υἱέσι, χελιδόνα ζωγραφοῦσιν... (*Tēn holocherē sēmainein boulomenoi ktēsin gonikēn kataleiphtheisan tois huiesi...*). Ancient lore suggested the swallow worked most feverishly on its nest toward the end of its life to ensure the survival of its brood.</note>

<margin>31</margin> 

### ->What they imply by painting a swallow.<- <note>original Latin: *Quid innuunt, hirundinem pingentes*.</note>

Wishing to imply all the wealth of parents left to children, they paint a swallow. For that bird, now close to death, wallows in mud and prepares a shelter for her chicks. <note>original Latin: *Omnes parentum opes filiis relictas volentes innuere, hirundinem pingunt...* The Latin translation mirrors the Greek, emphasizing the preservation of "opes" (wealth/resources) for the next generation.</note>

<term>Swallow</term> (χελιδών / *hirundo*): In ancient symbolism, the swallow was often associated with the home and family due to its nesting habits near human dwellings.
<term>Inheritance</term> (κτῆσιν γονικὴν / *opes relictas*): Specifically a "paternal possession" or "ancestral estate." Horapollo uses the swallow's nest-building—using mud to create a solid, lasting structure—as a metaphor for a father building a fortune or home to leave behind.
<term>Nest</term> (φωλεὸν / *latebras*): Literally a "den," "hiding place," or "nest." The text implies a place of safety and provision.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic link between the swallow and the concept of a paternal inheritance, based on the bird's industrious nest-building for its young. It highlights the ancient belief that the swallow prepares a lasting home for its offspring as its final act.</summary>

<keywords>swallow, inheritance, paternal estate, nest-building, symbolism, Horapollo, family legacy</keywords>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular frame depicting a dark bird, likely a black dove or pigeon, standing on a rocky or sandy shore. In the background, there is a body of water, a large rocky outcrop or mountain, and several trees, including what appears to be a palm tree.</image-desc>

### ->What a black dove signifies.<- <note>original Greek: Τί μέλαιναν περιστεράν (*Ti melainan peristeran*)</note>

When they wish to signify a widowed woman who remains in that state until death, they paint a black dove; for this bird does not consort with another mate as long as she remains a widow. <note>original Greek: Γυναῖκα χήραν ἐπιμείνασαν ἄχρι θανάτου θέλοντες σημῆναι, περιστεράν μέλαιναν ζωγραφοῦσιν... (*Gunaika chēran epimeinasan achri thanatou thelontes sēmainein, peristeran melainan zōgraphousin...*). In ancient natural history, the dove was a symbol of marital devotion, and it was widely believed that they did not take new mates after the death of the first.</note>

---

### ->What a black dove means.<- <note>original Latin: *Quid nigram columbam*.</note>
<margin>32</margin>

Wishing to represent a widow who remains in that same condition of life until death, they paint a black dove. For as long as she is a widow, she is not joined with another husband. <note>original Latin: *Mulierem viduam, quæ ad mortem usque in eo permanferit vitæ statu, volentes significare, nigram pingunt columbam...* The Latin translation by 16th-century humanists often standardized the Greek into more formal, legalistic phrasing.</note>

<term>Black Dove</term> (μέλαινα περιστερά / *nigra columba*): While white doves often represented peace or purity, the black dove here serves as a specific "hieroglyph" for mourning and the solemnity of widowhood.
<term>Widow</term> (χήρα / *vidua*): From the Greek *chēra*, meaning "bereft" or "separated." The text emphasizes the "virtuous widow" trope common in both pagan and early Christian thought.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic use of the black dove to represent a widow who remains faithful to her deceased husband until her own death. It highlights the bird's supposed natural instinct for lifelong monogamy as a moral exemplar.</summary>
<keywords>black dove, widowhood, fidelity, Hieroglyphica, animal symbolism, marriage</keywords>

THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape with a large, gnarled tree on the left and rocky terrain. In the foreground, an ichneumon (a mongoose-like creature) is shown crawling on the ground near a body of water or a low-lying area.</image-desc>

### ->What an ichneumon signifies.<- <note>original Greek: Τί ἰχνεύμονα (*Ti ichneumona*)</note>

When they wish to represent a weak man, one who is unable to help himself through his own power but instead requires the assistance of others, they paint an **ichneumon**. <note>The "ichneumon" is the Egyptian Mongoose (*Herpestes ichneumon*). In ancient Greek and Roman natural history, it was famously regarded as the mortal enemy of the crocodile and the cobra.</note> For when that animal sees a serpent, it does not attack it until it has called others to its aid with a cry; only then does it set itself against the serpent. <note>original Greek: ...ἐκεῖνη γὰρ ὅταν ἴδῃ ὄφιν οὐ πρότερον ἐπιτίθεται αὐτῷ ἀλλὰ βοῇ τοὺς ἄλλους ἐπικαλουμένη, τότε ἐναντιοῦται τῷ ὄφει. (*...ekeinē gar hotan idē ophin ou proteron epitithetai autō alla boē tous allous epikaloumenē, tote enantioutai tō ophei.*)</note>

<margin>33</margin>
### ->What an ichneumon means.<- <note>original Latin: *Quid ichneumonem*.</note>

Wishing to show a man who is powerless, and who cannot bring aid to himself by his own efforts but must be protected by the help of others, they paint an **ichneumon**. This creature, having spotted a serpent, does not immediately rush upon it; but rather, having first summoned others with a shout, it only then resists the enemy. <note>The Latin translation by Renaissance humanists uses *inualidum* (infirm/weak) and *opem* (aid), emphasizing the social or political necessity of alliances for those lacking individual strength.</note>

<term>Ichneumon</term> (ἰχνεύμων): Literally "the tracker." While modern biology identifies it as a solitary hunter, ancient accounts often emphasized its cleverness and perceived "social" strategies in overcoming much larger, more dangerous reptiles like the asp or the crocodile.

<term>Serpent</term> (ὄφις / *serpens*): In this context, the serpent represents a danger too great for a single individual to overcome, symbolizing a threat that requires a community to defeat.

<summary>This page describes Chapter 33 of Horapollo's Hieroglyphics, explaining that the ichneumon (mongoose) represents a person who needs the help of others to overcome challenges, based on the animal's supposed habit of calling for reinforcements before fighting a snake.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, Hieroglyphics, Ichneumon, Mongoose, Serpent, Symbolism, Cooperation, Weakness, Egyptian Animals</keywords>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting an origanum (oregano) plant growing in a landscape. At the base of the plant and in the foreground, several ants are shown crawling on the ground, appearing to move away from the plant.</image-desc>

### What they signify by depicting oregano. <note>original Greek: Τί δηλοῦσιν ὀρίγανον ἱερογλυφοῦντες (*Ti dēlousin origanon hieroglyphountes*)</note>

When they wish to represent the **departure of ants**, they depict oregano. <note>Origanum is a genus of herbaceous plants including oregano and marjoram, which were believed in antiquity to have pest-repellent properties.</note> For this plant causes the ants to leave when it is placed in the spot from which they emerge.

---

<margin>34</margin>
### What they denote by carving oregano in sacred rites. <note>original Latin: *Quid notent, origanum in sacris insculpentes*.</note>

Desiring to signify the absence and dispersal of ants, they paint oregano among the sacred carvings. For indeed, if this plant is set in the place from which the ants come forth, it drives them away. <note>The Latin text uses the word *pingunt* (they paint), suggesting that by the Renaissance, "hieroglyphics" were understood as both carved and painted symbols. This entry reflects ancient "sympathetic magic" or natural history—the idea that the physical properties of a plant (its scent) determine its symbolic meaning (the absence of the pest it repels).</note>

<summary>This page describes the use of the oregano plant as a hieroglyphic symbol for the departure or absence of ants, based on its natural property as a repellent.</summary>
<keywords>oregano, ants, repellent, hieroglyphics, Horapollo, natural history, symbolism of absence</keywords>

->HORAPOLLO<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a crocodile and a scorpion in combat. The crocodile is positioned on the right, facing left with its mouth open, while the scorpion is on the left, facing the crocodile. They are set against a landscape with rocks and sparse vegetation.</image-desc>

### What a scorpion and a crocodile signify. <note>original Greek: Τί σκορπίον, καὶ κροκόδειλον (*Ti skorpion, kai krokodeilon*)</note>

When they wish to represent **a man who is an enemy facing another enemy who is his equal**, they paint a scorpion and a crocodile; for each of them kills the other. But if they wish to signify the opposition and destruction of one by the other, they paint either a crocodile or a scorpion. However, if they mean **one who kills quickly**, they paint a crocodile; but if they mean **one who kills slowly**, they paint a scorpion, because of its sluggish movement. <note>The crocodile was viewed in the ancient world as a symbol of swift, overwhelming force, whereas the scorpion, though deadly, was perceived as a slow-moving creature that took time to reach its target.</note>

<margin>35</margin>
### What the scorpion and the crocodile signify. <note>original Latin: *Quid scorpium & crocodilum*.</note>

Desiring to indicate an enemy engaging with an equal enemy, they paint a scorpion and a crocodile: for each brings mutual destruction to the other. But if they would signal one as the victor who has removed his enemy, they paint either a crocodile or a scorpion: if he has removed him quickly, a crocodile; if slowly, a scorpion, on account of its difficult and slow movement. <note>The Latin text mirrors the Greek, reinforcing the idea of "mutual destruction" (*mutuum affert exitium*). This reflects a common theme in Renaissance emblem books where two equally matched forces result in a stalemate or mutual ruin.</note>

<summary>This page explains the symbolic use of the crocodile and scorpion to represent equal enemies, with the crocodile signifying a quick death and the scorpion a slow one.</summary>
<keywords>crocodile, scorpion, enmity, mutual destruction, speed of death, Horapollo, Hieroglyphica, symbolism</keywords>

->HIEROGLYPHICA.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular frame depicting a weasel or similar small carnivorous mammal in a landscape. In the background, there is a church with a steeple, several small buildings, and rolling hills under a cloudy sky. The animal is shown in profile, walking towards the right.</image-desc>

### What a weasel signifies. <note>original Greek: Τί γαλῆ (*Ti galē*)</note>

When they wish to signify a **woman performing the works of a man**, they paint a weasel; for this creature possesses a genital part like a small bone, just as a male does. <note>Ancient naturalists, including Aristotle, noted the presence of a small bone in the genitalia (the baculum in males or baubellum in females) of certain small mammals like the weasel, which led to symbolic associations with "masculine" traits in females.</note>

### What the weasel signifies. <note>original Latin: *Quid mustellam*.</note>

<margin>36</margin>
Where they wish to express a woman who performs the duties of a man, they paint a weasel: because this female possesses a genital part like a small bone, <note>original Latin: *pudendum habeat velut ossiculum*</note> just like the male.

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORUS APOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a crocodile and a scorpion in a rocky landscape. The crocodile is positioned in the foreground, facing left, while the scorpion is situated near its head. In the background, there are large, stylized rock formations and sparse vegetation.</image-desc>

->### What a scorpion and a crocodile signify. <note>original Greek: Τί σκορπίον, καὶ κροκόδειλον (*Ti skorpion, kai krokodeilon*)</note><-

When they wish to signify an **enemy encountering another enemy of equal strength**, they paint a scorpion and a crocodile; for each of these kills the other. But if they wish to signify something that is the opposite and destructive to the other, they paint either a crocodile or a scorpion. Specifically, if they mean one who kills **swiftly**, they paint a crocodile; but if one who kills **slowly**, they paint a scorpion, because of its sluggish movement. <note>Ancient observers believed the crocodile was a symbol of lightning-fast strikes, whereas the scorpion, despite its lethal venom, was viewed as a slow-moving creature.</note>

<margin>35</margin> ->### What the scorpion and the crocodile signify. <note>original Latin: *Quid scorpium & crocodilum*.</note><-

When they wish to indicate an enemy clashing with an equal enemy, they paint a scorpion and a crocodile: for each brings about the other's mutual destruction. But if they would signify a victor who has removed an enemy, they paint either a crocodile or a scorpion: if he removed him quickly, a crocodile; if slowly, a scorpion, because of its difficult and sluggish movement. <note>original Latin: *ob eius difficilem tardumque motum*</note>

<summary>This page explains Chapter 35 of the Hieroglyphica, using the crocodile and scorpion to represent mutual enmity and the varying speeds—swift or slow—of a person's destruction.</summary>
<keywords>crocodile, scorpion, enmity, destruction, speed, symbolism, Horapollo, Hieroglyphica</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS.
I iij <margin>36</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a square border depicting a weasel or similar small carnivorous mammal walking in a landscape. In the background, there is a village with a church steeple, trees, and rolling hills under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

->### What a weasel signifies. <note>original Greek: Τί γαλῆ (*Ti galē*)</note><-

When they wish to signify a **woman performing the deeds of a man**, they paint a weasel; for this animal has male genitalia, like a small bone. <note>Ancient Greek natural history, including writers like Aristotle and Aelian, often recorded unusual or mistaken beliefs about the anatomy and reproductive habits of the weasel (galē), sometimes suggesting they were hermaphroditic or possessed peculiar skeletal structures.</note>

---

->### What the weasel signifies. <note>original Latin: *Quid mustellam*.</note><-

When they want to represent a woman who performs the works of a man, they paint a weasel: because it possesses the male reproductive organ as if it were a small bone. <note>original Latin: *maris pudendum habeat velut ossiculum*</note>

<vocab>**Hieroglyphics**, **weasel** <note>Greek: *galē*; Latin: *mustella*</note>, **woman**, **the works of a man**</vocab>

# OF HORAPOLLO

->### What a pig signifies. <note>original Greek: τί χοῖρον (*ti choiron*)</note><-

When they wish to signify a **completely ruined man**, they paint a pig; for the nature of the pig is such. <note>The Greek term *exōlē* refers to someone who is utterly depraved or headed for total destruction; in ancient thought, the pig was often associated with filth and base instincts.</note>

---

<margin>37</margin>
->### What a pig signifies. <note>original Latin: *Quid porcum*</note><-

Wishing to indicate a **destructive and pestilent man**, they paint a pig: because the nature of the pig is of that kind. <note>The Latin translation expands on the Greek by using "perniciosum & pestiferum," suggesting a person who is not just ruined himself, but who brings ruin and "pestilence" to those around him, much like a wild boar rooting up a garden.</note>

<summary>This page presents Chapter 37 of the Hieroglyphica, explaining that a pig (or boar) is the symbol for a person of ruinous or destructive character. This reflects ancient views on the animal's perceived nature as dirty or harmful.</summary>

135
# HIEROGLYPHICS
<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>HIEROGLYPHICS.</header>
<sig>I iiij</sig>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large, muscular lion in a rocky landscape with palm-like trees in the background. The lion is shown in a dynamic pose, looking back towards its tail, which is curled over its back. At the lion's feet are two smaller lion cubs.</image-desc>

->### How they represent immoderate anger. <note>original Greek: πῶς θυμὸν ἄμετρον (*pōs thymon ametron*)</note><-

If they wish to represent **immoderate anger**, to the point that the angry person even develops a fever from it, they depict a lion **stirring up** <note>original: ἐκσοίζοντα (*eksoizonta*). This term is somewhat rare and implies a vigorous shaking or rousing action.</note> its own cubs. They use a lion because of its inherent spirit and anger; and they show the cubs being stirred because the bones of lion cubs, when struck together, emit fire. <note>In ancient natural history, it was believed that the intensity of a lion's "heat" was so great that their very bones acted like flint and steel.</note>

---

<margin>38</margin>
->### How immoderate anger is signified. <note>original Latin: *Quomodo immodicam iram*</note><-

But if they signify an immoderate wrath, from which the person moved by it has contracted a fever, they paint a lion **striking its own cubs with its tail**. <note>The Latin translator here interprets the Greek "stirring" specifically as the lion whipping its cubs with its tail to rouse them, or perhaps itself, into a frenzy.</note> A lion indeed on account of its wrathful nature, but the cubs beaten by the tail because if the bones of cubs are struck against each other, they emit fire.

<vocab>Hieroglyphics (Hieroglyphica), Lion (Leo), Wrath/Anger (iracundia), Cubs (catulos), Fire (ignem), Spirit/Anger (θυμὸν)</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that a lion rousing its cubs (or striking them with its tail) is a symbol for extreme anger that leads to fever. It relies on the ancient folklore that lion bones could produce sparks like flint when struck.</summary>

<keywords>lion, anger, fever, cubs, fire, animal symbolism, Horapollo, physiology</keywords>

### How they represent a musical old man. <note>original Greek: Πῶς γέροντα μουσικόν (*Pōs geronta mousikon*)</note>
When they wish to signify a **musical old man** <note>The term "musical" in this context refers to someone who is cultured, harmonious, or favored by the Muses, rather than just a performer of instruments.</note>, they paint a **swan**. For this bird sings the **sweetest melody** as it grows old. <note>This refers to the ancient "swan song" myth—the belief that the swan, normally silent, bursts into beautiful song just before its death.</note>

<margin>39</margin>

Wishing to demonstrate a musical old man, they paint a swan: because this bird, while aging, produces the **most delightful harmony**. <note>original Latin: *suauissimum concentum*. The Latin translator emphasizes the "concord" or "harmony" of the sound.</note>

<summary>This page explains the use of the swan as a hieroglyphic symbol for an elderly man of culture or music, drawing on the classical legend that swans sing most beautifully in their old age.</summary>

# HIEROGLYPHICA

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two crows or ravens in flight over a landscape. In the background, there is a rustic building with smoke rising from a chimney, set against a hilly terrain with trees.</image-desc>

->### How they signify a man joining with his own wife. <note>original Greek: πῶς ἄνδρα δηλῶσι συγγινόμενον τῇ ἑαυτοῦ γυναικί (*pōs andra dēlōsi synginomenon tē heautou gynaiki*)</note><-

Wishing to signify a **man joining with his own wife** in sexual union, they depict **two crows**. For these birds mate with one another in the same natural manner as a human being. <note>Ancient naturalists often believed that crows were unique among birds for their monogamy and for mating in a face-to-face position similar to humans—a belief that reinforced their status as symbols of lawful marriage.</note>

---

<margin>40</margin>
->### In what way they indicate a man coupling with his own wife. <note>original Latin: *Quo modo virum innuunt cum sua ipsius vxore congredientem*</note><-

If they wish to indicate a man who maintains **marital intimacy** <note>original Latin: *consuetudinem veneream*, literally "sexual habit" or "practice of Venus."</note> with his wife, they paint **twin crows**; because these naturally mate with each other in the same way that humans do.

<summary>This page describes the crow as a hieroglyphic symbol for marital union and fidelity, based on the ancient belief that crows mate in a manner similar to humans.</summary>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

->### What they signify by depicting a blind beetle. <note>original Greek: Τί δηλοῦσι κάνθαρον τυφλὸν γράφοντες (*Ti dēlousi kantharon typhlon graphontes*)</note><-

Wishing to signify a **man who has caught a fever from a solar ray** and consequently died, they depict a **blind beetle**; <note>The "beetle" here refers to the *scarabaeus sacer*, which was deeply associated with the sun god in Egyptian thought. The author implies a paradoxical relationship where the sun's power, usually life-giving, can also consume or blind.</note> for this creature, being blinded by the sun, dies.

To denote a man who has perished from a **fever contracted from the rays of the sun**, they paint a **blind Scarab**, <note>The Latin *scarabæus* translates the Greek *kantharos*. In antiquity, it was often believed that certain creatures had eyes so sensitive that the brilliance of the sun could overwhelm and destroy them.</note> as one who dies after being blinded by the brilliance of the sun.

139
# HIEROGLYPHICS
<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>HIEROGLYPHICA</header>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a mule standing in a landscape. The mule is harnessed and positioned in front of a thick, gnarled tree trunk. In the background, there are rolling hills and some foliage.</image-desc>

->### What they signify by depicting a mule. <note>original Greek: Τί δηλοῦσιν ἡμίονον γράφοντες (*Ti dēlousin hēmionon graphontes*)</note><-

When they wish to signify a **sterile woman**, they depict a **mule**; <note>In the ancient world, the mule—a hybrid of a horse and a donkey—was the most famous example of animal infertility.</note> for this creature is sterile because its **womb is not positioned straight**. <note>The Greek text uses the phrase *ep’ eutheias*, suggesting a belief that a "crooked" or tilted womb was the physical cause of the animal's inability to conceive.</note>

<margin>42</margin>
->### What the mule signifies. <note>original Latin: *Quid mulam*</note><-

Wishing to indicate a **sterile woman**, they paint a **mule**. For this animal is sterile for the reason that it does not possess a **straight womb**. <note>The Latin text mirrors the Greek, using *matrix* for womb. This anatomical explanation was common in early natural history to explain why hybrids could not reproduce.</note>

<term>mule</term> <note>original Greek: ἡμίονον (*hēmionon*); Latin: *mula*. A sterile hybrid animal.</note>
<term>sterile</term> <note>Latin: *sterilis*. Incapable of producing offspring.</note>
<term>woman</term> <note>Latin: *mulierem*. Referring here to the symbolic representation of human infertility.</note>
<term>hieroglyphics</term> <note>The study of symbolic signs, which the author interprets as allegories of nature.</note>

<summary>This page explains that the mule is used as a hieroglyphic symbol for a sterile woman, attributing the animal's well-known infertility to a perceived anatomical misalignment of its womb.</summary>
<keywords>mule, sterility, woman, anatomy, womb, animal symbolism, Horapollo</keywords>

140
# HORAPOLLO
<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO</header>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two bulls in a landscape. The bull in the foreground is facing right, while the bull in the background is facing left. There are three stars in the upper left corner of the frame and a small tree on the right horizon.</image-desc>

### How they signify a woman who has given birth to female infants. <note>original Greek: Πῶς δηλοῦσι γυναῖκα γεννήσασαν θήλεα βρέφη (*Pōs dēlousi gynaika gennēsasas thēlea brephē*)</note>

When they wish to signify a **woman who has first given birth to female infants**, they depict a **bull leaning toward the left**. But if they wish to signify **male offspring**, they again depict a **bull leaning toward the right**. <note>In ancient Greek and Roman thought, the "right side" was traditionally associated with masculinity and strength, while the "left side" was associated with femininity.</note> For when the bull dismounts from mating, if he should descend toward the **left**, it signifies that a **female** is being conceived; but if he should descend toward the **right** from mating, a **male** is born. <note>This reflects an ancient belief, also found in the writings of Aristotle and Pliny the Elder, that the physical movement or position of the male during or after copulation determined the sex of the child.</note>

<margin>43</margin>
->How they signify a woman who has brought forth female children. <note>original Latin: *Quo modo mulierem quæ puellos fœmineos pepererit*</note><-

Signifying a woman who has first brought forth **female infants**, they paint a **bull looking toward the left**. For **males**, however, they again paint a **bull looking toward the right**. For as he departs from the union, if he has turned toward the **left**, it indicates that a **female** has been produced; if toward the **right**, a **male**.

<term>bull</term> <note>original Greek: ταῦρον (*tauron*); Latin: *taurum*. Used here as a symbol of procreative force and sex determination.</note>
<term>left</term> <note>Latin: *sinistrorsum*. Symbolically linked to the female sex in ancient biological theories.</note>
<term>right</term> <note>Latin: *dextrorsum*. Symbolically linked to the male sex.</note>
<term>mating</term> <note>original Greek: ὀχείας (*ocheias*); Latin: *congressu*. Referring to the act of animal copulation observed for omens of gender.</note>

<summary>This page explains how the direction a bull leans or turns after mating serves as a hieroglyphic symbol for the sex of a woman's firstborn children. It reflects ancient biological theories that associated the right side with males and the left side with females.</summary>

<keywords>Horapollo, Hieroglyphica, bull, birth, gender, symbolism, sex determination, ancient biology</keywords>

141
# HIEROGLYPHICS
<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>HIEROGLYPHICS</header>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting the carcass of a horse lying on the ground in a landscape with a large tree on the left and mountains in the background. Swarming insects, intended to be wasps, are shown emerging from or hovering around the decaying animal.</image-desc>

### How they signify wasps. <note>original Greek: Πῶς δηλοῦσι σφῆκας (*Pōs dēlousi sphēkas*)</note>

When they wish to signify **wasps**, they depict a **dead horse**. For many wasps are born from it after it has died. <note>This reflects the ancient theory of spontaneous generation, specifically a belief sometimes called *hippogonia*. Ancient observers noticed insects swarming around carrion and concluded that the decaying flesh actually transformed into those insects.</note>

<margin>44</margin>
->How they signify wasps. <note>original Latin: *Quo modo vespas*</note><-

Signifying **wasps**, they paint the **carcass of a horse**. For from it many wasps are procreated. <note>Just as bees were famously believed to be born from the carcasses of oxen (a process called *bougonia*), wasps were linked to horses. This piece of "natural history" was widely accepted by classical authors such as Pliny the Elder and Ovid.</note>

<summary>This page explains that the hieroglyphic for wasps is a dead horse, rooted in the ancient biological belief that wasps spontaneously generate from equine carcasses.</summary>
<keywords>wasps, horse, spontaneous generation, hippogonia, Horapollo, symbolism</keywords>

142
# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO
<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO</header>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a horse (mare) standing over a wolf. The horse's hind leg is raised as if trampling or kicking the wolf, which is crouched on the ground beneath it. The scene is set in a sparse landscape with hills in the background.</image-desc>

->How they signify a woman miscarrying. <note>original Greek: Πῶς δηλοῦσι γυναῖκα ἐκτιτρώσκουσαν (*Pōs dēlousi gynaika ektitrōskousan*)</note><-

When they wish to signify a **woman miscarrying**, they depict a **horse trampling a wolf**. For not only does the **mare** <note>The Greek term *hippos* is used, but the context of pregnancy clearly refers to a female horse.</note> miscarry if she tramples the wolf, but even if she merely steps upon the **wolf’s track**, she miscarries immediately.

---

<margin>45</margin>
->How they signify a woman miscarrying. <note>original Latin: *Quo modo mulierem abortientem*</note><-

Signifying a **woman miscarrying**, they paint a **mare** that tramples a **wolf**. For not only does the mare miscarry if she has stepped upon a wolf, but even if she has stepped upon the **wolf’s footprint**, she miscarries at once. <note>This reflects an ancient belief in sympathetic magic and "antipathy" between species. Classical naturalists like Pliny the Elder recorded that the presence or scent of a wolf was so terrifying or toxic to a pregnant mare that it would cause an instant abortion.</note>

<vocab>Hieroglyphics, Horapollo, miscarriage, mare, wolf, horse, wolf, to miscarry</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that the hieroglyphic for a miscarrying woman is a mare trampling a wolf, based on the ancient folk belief that contact with a wolf or its tracks causes horses to lose their foals.</summary>
<keywords>miscarriage, pregnancy, mare, horse, wolf, animal symbolism, Horapollo, sympathetic magic</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS.
<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>HIEROGLYPHICS.</header>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a square frame depicting a bird (identified in the text as a wood-pigeon) in flight, carrying a laurel branch in its beak toward a large, gnarled tree on the right. The background shows a landscape with rolling hills and water.</image-desc>

### ->How they signify a man who has healed himself through an oracle. <note>original Greek: Πῶς ἄνθρωπον ἰαζόμενον ἑαυτὸν ἀχρήσμου (*Pōs anthrōpon iazomenon heauton achrēsmou*)</note><-

When they wish to symbolize a **man healing himself** by an oracle, they depict a **wood-pigeon** holding a **laurel leaf**; for when that bird is ill, she places a laurel leaf into her nest and becomes healthy. <note>The word "oracle" here refers to a divine response or prophecy. In antiquity, many sought medical cures from the gods, particularly Apollo.</note>

<margin>46</margin>
->How they signify a man who has cured himself by a response received from an oracle. <note>original Latin: *Quo modo hominem, qui sese responso ab oraculo accepto sanarit*</note><-

Wishing to represent a man who has restored himself to his former health based on an oracle, they paint a **wood-pigeon** <note>The Latin *palumbus* and Greek *phassa* both refer to the common wood-pigeon (*Columba palumbus*), which was believed by ancient naturalists to have self-medicating instincts.</note> holding a **laurel leaf**. For when this bird suffers from a disease, by bringing a laurel leaf into its nest, it recovers. <note>The laurel was sacred to Apollo, the god of both prophecy and medicine. This hieroglyph creates a symbolic bridge between the bird's natural "instinctive" cure and a human's "divine" cure received via an oracle.</note>

<vocab>Hieroglyphics, wood-pigeon, wood-pigeon, laurel, oracle, healing</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the symbolic representation of a person who has been cured of an illness following divine advice, using the image of a wood-pigeon with a laurel leaf. The laurel's connection to Apollo, the god of healing and prophecy, underscores the bird's role as a symbol for recovery through an oracle.</summary>
