144
# HORAPOLLO
<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>HORAPOLLO</header>

<margin>
[The] ant, endowed with wings; <note>Original Latin: *formica, alis praedita*. The text is partially cut off at the margin.</note> for <gloss>whoever</gloss> abuses riches, dignity, letters, talent, or [any] kind of fortune, nature, or free gifts in his own heart; hence it is commonly said: **the ant’s wings are its own undoing.** <note>A common Renaissance proverb: *formicae sua periere alae*. It suggests that when an ant grows wings, it is a sign of its approaching death or downfall, symbolizing those whose sudden rise or pride leads to their ruin.</note> This applies truly to those who [rise] from worms—that is, from men of [low birth], from children of lighter [character]—and do not fear to [do harm].
</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape with a river or body of water in the foreground. Numerous winged insects (gnats or mosquitoes) are swarming over the water and land. In the background, there are trees, hills, and a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

<insert>
And to those who are loathsome, to others by the power of favor... <note>original Latin: *Et nauseabundis, alijs fauoris potentatu*. This appears to be a fragmentary commentary on the nature of pests or perhaps social climbers.</note>
**How [to represent] many gnats.** <note>original Greek: Πῶς κώνωπας πολλούς (*Pōs kōnōpas pollous*)</note>
</insert>

> When they wish to depict **many gnats** swarming, they draw **worms**; for from these, gnats are born. <note>The author correctly observes the life cycle of mosquitoes or gnats, which emerge from aquatic larvae (the "worms" described here).</note>

### ->How they signify a swarm of gnats.<-

Wishing to express an increasing and arriving **swarm of gnats**, they paint **worms**. For indeed, from these, gnats are brought forth. <note>original Latin: *Quo modo culicum copiam*. The term *culices* refers generally to biting flies, gnats, or mosquitoes.</note>

<vocab>Horapollo, Hieroglyphica, gnats (culices), gnats (κώνωπας), worms (vermes), worms/larvae (σκώληκας)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes Chapter 47 of the Hieroglyphics, explaining that a swarm of gnats is represented by the image of worms, based on the observation of their larval state. The marginalia provides a moralizing commentary on the dangers of pride and sudden prosperity, using the metaphor of a winged ant.</summary>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>HIEROGLYPHICA.</header>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular frame depicting a natural landscape. In the foreground, a dove or pigeon is perched on a bare branch extending from the left. The background features a dense forest with tall trees and a small wooden fence or bridge to the right.</image-desc>

<margin>
145
Well-mannered men, while they are private citizens, [often] become ill-tempered and vicious once they attain leadership, etc. 
Hence the saying: **Office reveals the man.** <note>Original Latin: *Magistratus ostendunt hominem*. This is a classical proverb, often attributed to Bias of Priene, suggesting that power does not change a man so much as it exposes his true character.</note>
In this way, Saul was changed into another man. <note>A reference to the biblical account in 1 Samuel 10:6, where Saul is transformed upon being anointed king, though here the commentator uses it to illustrate how status alters personality.</note>
Just as bricks are soft before they are put into the furnace, but are hardened by the furnace; so men in private life are affable, but once established in high office, they become difficult.
48
</margin>

->How [to represent] a man who has no gall of his own, but receives it from another.<-
<note>original Greek: Πῶς ἄνδρα μὴ ἔχοντα χολὴν, ἀλλ’ ἀφ’ ἑτέρου δεχόμενον (*Pōs andra mē echonta cholēn, all’ aph’ heterou dechomenon*).</note>

When they wish to depict a man who is not naturally bitter or angry, but is provoked to anger by another, they paint a **dove** with its hindquarters raised; for it is there that the dove holds its gall. <note>In ancient natural history, it was widely believed that doves and pigeons lacked a gallbladder (the source of *chole* or "gall"), which accounted for their legendary gentleness. Horapollo here provides a "hieroglyphic" explanation, suggesting the bitterness is not internal to the bird's nature but displaced or external.</note>

->How [to represent] a man who, though not bitter by his own nature, is nonetheless moved to bitterness by another.<-
<note>original Latin: *Quomodo hominem qui cum ex se biliosus non sit, ab altero tamen bile commoueatur*.</note>

When marking a man who by his own nature lacks bitterness, but receives it from someone else, they paint a dove with its back held upright: for it is in that place that the bird possesses its gall.

<summary>This page describes Chapter 48 of the Hieroglyphica, explaining how a dove with its tail raised symbolizes a person who is naturally gentle but can be provoked to anger by others. The marginal notes reflect on how the pressures of public office can harden a person's character, much like a furnace hardens clay.</summary>

<keywords>dove, gall, anger, temperament, leadership, Saul, proverbs, symbolism, Hieroglyphica</keywords>

<header>THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO</header>

<margin>
A small creature, endowed with wings. This refers to those who stand out for their wealth, dignity, learning, or talent, <gloss>those who</gloss> possess a certain kind of fortune or nature, or who abuse the gifts given to them for their own selfish ends. From this, it is commonly said that they are an "ant-like evil" <note>original: *formicius malum*. This likely refers to a proverb about small things that cause great irritation or the transformation of small, base things into pests.</note>. This description truly applies to those who rise from worms—that is, from the most insignificant and lowly of men—and do not hesitate to become a nuisance to others once they have "taken wing."
</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape with a large tree on the left and a body of water or marshy ground in the foreground. Numerous insects, appearing to be gnats or mosquitoes, are swarming in the air and crawling on the ground near small worm-like creatures.</image-desc>

And to those who are loathsome, and to others by the power of favor.

### How [to represent] a multitude of gnats.

> When they wish to depict a multitude of **gnats** gathering together, they paint **worms**; for gnats are born from these. <note>original Greek: Πῶς κώνωπας πολλούς (*Pōs kōnōpas pollous*). The ancient author Horapollo explains that the Egyptians used the larval state of the insect to symbolize the adult swarm, reflecting an early understanding of metamorphosis.</note>

### In what way a multitude of gnats [is represented].

Desiring to express a growing and arriving multitude of **gnats**, they paint **worms**. For indeed, from these are gnats born. <note>original Latin: *Quo modo culicum copiam*. The term "culix" refers broadly to biting midges, mosquitoes, or gnats.</note>

<vocab>Horapollo, Hieroglyphics, gnats, worms, κώνωπας (kōnōpas), σκώληκας (skōlēkas)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the hieroglyphic convention of using worms to represent a swarm of gnats, based on the biological observation of their life cycle. The marginalia expands on this as a social metaphor for lowly individuals who become bothersome when they rise in status.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, gnats, mosquitoes, worms, metamorphosis, social hierarchy, symbolism, entomology</keywords>

Well-mannered men, when they are private citizens, [but] once they attain leadership, become ill-tempered, vicious, etc. 
From this [comes the proverb], **The magistracy reveals the man.** <note>original Latin: *Magistratus ostendunt hominem*. This is a classical Greek and Latin proverb (often attributed to Bias of Priene) suggesting that power does not necessarily change a person, but provides the opportunity for their true character—good or bad—to be seen.</note>
Hence [it is said that] **Saul** was changed into another man. <note>A reference to 1 Samuel 10:6, where the biblical King Saul is transformed upon being anointed; here, the commentator likely refers to Saul's later descent into jealousy and madness.</note>
Just as bricks are soft before they are put into the kiln but are hardened by the oven, so men in private life are affable, but once established in a position of dignity, they become difficult.
</margin>

->How [to represent] a man who does not have bile, but receives it from another.<-

When writing of a man who does not naturally possess bile, but receives it from someone else, they paint a **dove** <note>original Greek: περιστερὰν (*peristeran*)</note> with its tail feathers held upright; for in those parts, it holds its bile. <note>In ancient "humoral" medicine, bile—specifically yellow bile—was thought to be the source of anger and bitterness. Natural historians like Aristotle believed doves lacked a gallbladder (and thus bile), making them symbols of gentleness. This text suggests that any anger found in a "dove-like" person must come from an external source.</note>

->How [to represent] a man who, although not bitter by nature, is moved to bitterness by another.<-

Marking a man who lacks bitterness <term>bile<gloss>anger or irritability</gloss></term> by his own nature, but takes it up from another, they paint a **dove** with its back raised; for it is there that it holds its bile.

<summary>This page explores the symbolism of the dove as a creature naturally lacking bitterness (bile), using it to represent people who are inherently gentle but can be provoked by others. The marginal notes provide a cynical counterpoint, arguing that power and high office often "harden" a man's character, much like a kiln hardens a brick.</summary>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO
<meta>Header: ORI APOLLINIS (The Hieroglyphics of Horapollo)</meta>

<margin>
If it should be in your soul to dwell safely in the world, [bring] into the nest of your heart that which is **Jesus**—namely, the **Rock** <note>original Latin: *Petra*. A common biblical metaphor for Christ as a foundation.</note>—so that Christ may enter in, through faith, hope, and charity, through confession and communion. From the **sea of contrition** and the **earth of humility**. 

Likewise, this is to be understood regarding the **glorious Virgin**, whom Christ the Eagle [carried] into his nest in her ascension, and may he **cherish his chicks** <note>original Latin: *pullos foveat*. A metaphor for Christ protecting the faithful, likely referencing Deuteronomy 32:11.</note> so they are not in long danger, and he placed them there so that, in the absence of his bodily presence, they might be safer and firmer. 

**Psalm [27:1]:** *The Lord is my light and my salvation, whom shall I fear?* 
**Psalm [27:3]:** *Though an army should encamp against me, my heart shall not fear.* 
The Lord is with me like a strong warrior, and **if God is for us, who can be against us?** <note>A reference to Romans 8:31.</note> and many other such things, etc.
</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large eagle perched on a rocky cliffside. The eagle is shown in profile, leaning over its nest which contains small chicks. The eagle appears to be placing or holding a stone with its beak. The background shows a rugged, mountainous landscape with a sparse tree branch extending from the right.</image-desc>

### How [to represent] a man safely inhabiting a city.

To signify a man who lives safely in a city, they paint an **eagle** <note>original Greek: ἀετὸν (*aeton*)</note> carrying a **stone**; for that bird takes a stone from the sea or from the earth and places it in its own nest, so that it may remain secure.

### How [to represent] a man who dwells safely in a city.
<note>This is the Latin version of the heading above: *Quo modo hominem qui tutò vrbem incolat.*</note>

When they wish to represent a man who inhabits a city in safety, they paint an eagle carrying a stone. This bird brings a stone, taken from either the sea or the land, into its nest, so that it might be safer and more firm. <note>This refers to the *aetites* or "eaglestone," a mythical rock that eagles were believed to place in their nests to protect their young from predators or to prevent the eggs from rotting.</note>

<summary>This page explains the eagle as a symbol of safety and urban security, based on the belief that eagles use a special stone to protect their nests. The handwritten marginalia provide a Christian allegorical interpretation, equating the eagle's stone with Christ (the "Rock") protecting the soul or the Virgin Mary.</summary>

<keywords>eagle, eaglestone, nest, safety, security, Christ as Rock, Psalm 27, spiritual protection, Virgin Mary, Horapollo</keywords>

The **Otis** <note>original Latin: *Otis*. This refers to the Great Bustard, a large ground-nesting bird known for being wary and difficult to approach.</note> is a small bird similar to a partridge, which, exhausted by a short flight...

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large bird (likely an Otis or bustard) standing in a landscape with trees. To the left, a horse is grazing, head down. The scene is framed by a simple border.</image-desc>

<margin>
Likewise, the **Otus** <note>original: *Otus*. The Long-eared Owl.</note> is similar to a night-owl, endowed with tufts of feathers projecting around its ears, from which it received its name—as if you were to call it "the eared one." The people of the Nile call it a screech-owl. Others call it the *Asio* <note>A Latin term for a horned owl.</note>, about which **Dionysius Aelius** <note>An ancient scholar of the 2nd century known for his work on linguistics and birds.</note> says: "this bird," he says, "is praised and is captured while dancing" (just as we say regarding the Night-raven and the *Otus*).
</margin>

->How [to represent] a man who is weak and pursued by another.<-

<margin>
...namely, the **vain-glorious** <note>original Greek: *κενόδοξους* (kenodoxous). Those seeking empty praise.</note>. In this manner, you shall judge the powerful and the wealthy, as well as those eager for vain glory, who are captured by the teeth of flatterers. These birds are quickly exhausted by a short flight—that is, in the way of virtue; if they are attacked by pressure, they are very quickly worn out. At the sight of the **Horse**—that is, a swift conversion and a race toward God through repentance—they turn their backs; for **"they have turned their backs to me and not their faces,"** as written in **Jeremiah 2** <note>Referencing Jeremiah 2:27.</note>.
</margin>

When they wish to signify a man who is weak and pursued by one more powerful, they paint a **bustard** and a **horse**. For this bird flies away whenever it sees a horse.

->How [to represent] an inferior and weaker man, whom another pursues.<-

Wishing to designate a weaker man, whom a stronger and more powerful one pursues, they paint a bustard and a horse. For this bird, as soon as a horse is seen, immediately flies away.

The grace of the Holy Spirit knows no slow efforts. <note>original Latin: *nescit tarda molliumina [gratia] Spiritus Sancti*. A famous quote from St. Ambrose emphasizing that divine inspiration works instantly and without hesitation.</note>

<vocab>Hieroglyphica, Otis, Otus, Nycticorax, Dionysius Aelius, Equus, Jeremiah 2</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the hieroglyphic symbol of a bustard and a horse, used to represent a weak person fleeing from a more powerful pursuer. The marginalia provides a moralizing Christian interpretation, comparing the bird's short flight to the lack of endurance in those who seek vain glory rather than virtue.</summary>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO
<meta>Page 148. Catchword: noctua (the owl). This page features an entry on the betrayal of a patron.</meta>

<margin>
He presents an image of leaders or masters <note>original Latin: *duces vel dominos*</note> who, when people flee to them seeking protection from oppressors, instead crush them more severely. As the saying goes: *he falls into Scylla while trying to avoid Charybdis* <note>A famous classical metaphor for trying to avoid one danger only to fall into an even greater one; Scylla and Charybdis were two sea monsters in Homer's Odyssey situated on opposite sides of a narrow strait.</note>. Or: *fleeing the frying pan, he falls into the coals.* <note>original: *fugiens cartaginem, in prunas incidit*. This is an early variation of our modern idiom "out of the frying pan and into the fire."</note> Just as blood, corrupted by an infection from a poisoned arrow, flees toward the heart seeking a remedy; yet, as soon as it reaches the heart, it causes destruction—and where it sought life, it finds death. Thus it is for those who, pressed by misfortunes, flee to such men; seeking a remedy there, they find only death.
</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a hunter or fowler in a wooded landscape. The man, dressed in a tunic and hat, carries a long pole or snare. He is walking toward a large tree on the right where an owl is perched. Other smaller birds and shrubs are visible in the background.</image-desc>

### How they represent a man who flees to his own patron and is not helped.

When they wish to represent a man who flees to his own patron <note>A *patronus* in the ancient world was a person of high status who provided legal and financial protection to a "client" of lower status.</note> but receives no help from him, they depict a **sparrow** and an **owl**. For when the sparrow is being hunted, it flees to the owl [for safety], and is there crushed by her.

### How to represent him who, fleeing to his own patron, nevertheless feels no help from him.

Wishing to show a man who flees to his own patron and is not relieved by his help, they paint a **sparrow** <note>original Latin: *passerem*; original Greek: *στρουθὸν* (strouthon).</note> and an **owl** <note>original Latin: *noctuam*; original Greek: *γλαῦκα* (glauka).</note>. For while this bird is pursued by the hunting of fowlers, it runs to the owl, by whom it is soon destroyed.

<summary>This page describes a hieroglyph where a sparrow and an owl represent a "patron" who betrays someone seeking their protection. The marginal notes provide a grim commentary on how corrupt leaders act like poison reaching the heart, killing those who seek their aid.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, Hieroglyphics, patron, sparrow, owl, betrayal, Scylla and Charybdis, fowler, metaphors of power</keywords>

<margin>
In this way, there are many unlearned men who, lacking the wings of virtue, art, and literature, seek high honors, the spiritual care of souls, and the bishop’s miter <note>original Latin: *pontificalem infulam*. The *infula* was a headband or miter worn by Roman priests and later adopted as a symbol of episcopal dignity in the Church.</note>, yet they do not lift a finger to earn them. <note>original Latin: *illum non mouente lapidem*, literally "not moving a stone for it," an idiom for putting in no effort or lacking the necessary foundation.</note>
</margin>

->How they signify a weak and reckless man.<-

When they wish to symbolize a weak and reckless <note>original Greek: *propeteuomenon*, meaning someone who acts impulsively or falls forward into danger.</note> man, they paint a bat <note>original Greek: *nykteris*</note>. For that creature flies despite not having wings. <note>To the ancient eye, the bat’s leathery membranes were not considered "true" wings like the feathered wings of birds, making its flight seem unnatural or forced.</note>

<margin>
52
</margin>

->How they represent a man who, though lacking strength, nevertheless rashly undertakes a task and behaves too boldly.<-

When they want to show a weak man who is behaving wantonly and yet attempting something too daring, they paint a bat <note>original Latin: *vespertilio*</note>. For this creature, although it has no wings <note>original Latin: *alas*</note>, nevertheless strives to fly.

<vocab>Hieroglyphics, bat, nykteris, weak man, unlearned</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the bat as a hieroglyph for a person who attempts tasks far beyond their capabilities, specifically targeting unqualified men who seek high religious or social offices. The bat's flight without "true" (feathered) wings serves as a metaphor for ambition unsupported by virtue or education.</summary>
<keywords>bat, ambition, recklessness, ecclesiastical criticism, Horapollo, symbolism, nature as metaphor</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORUS APOLLO
Page 150

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a bird, likely a turtledove, perched on a branch of a large tree. In the background, a landscape with rolling hills and a small village or church spire is visible.</image-desc>

->How they represent a woman nursing and raising her child well.<-

When they wish to depict a woman nursing and nurturing her child well, they paint a **turtledove** <note>original Greek: *trygona*</note>. For this bird alone among all others is said to possess teeth and breasts. <note>This reflects an ancient belief in "crop milk"—a secretion produced by doves to feed their young—which led early naturalists to erroneously categorize the bird as having mammalian traits like breasts.</note>

---

<margin>53</margin> ->In what manner they signify a nursing woman, and one who nourishes most excellently.<-

When they wished to express a nursing and well-nourishing woman in a picture, they would paint a turtledove <note>original Latin: *turturem*</note>. For among all winged creatures, this one alone has teeth and breasts.

<vocab>**Horapollo**: The purported author of this treatise on hieroglyphs, often identified as one of the last priests of the Egyptian temples. **Turtledove**: In this context, a symbol of maternal devotion. **Lactantem mulierem**: Latin for "a nursing woman."</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the use of the turtledove as a hieroglyphic symbol for a mother who successfully nurses and raises her children, based on the ancient misconception that the bird possessed teeth and breasts.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, turtledove, nursing, motherhood, animal symbolism, hieroglyphics, ancient biology</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS
K iiij
<meta>catchword: mus</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting four men in a wooded landscape. Two men on the left are playing wind instruments (flutes or pipes), while two men on the right are seated, one holding a stringed instrument resembling a viol or cello. A bird, likely a turtledove, is perched in the branches of a tree in the center of the scene.</image-desc>

### How to represent a man charmed by dancing.
When they wish to signify a man who is charmed by dancing and flute-playing, they paint a **turtledove** <note>original Greek: *trygona*</note>; for this bird is captured by the sound of the flute and by dancing. <note>Ancient naturalists and hunters believed that certain birds, particularly the turtledove, were so entranced by rhythmic music and the movements of a dance that they would lose their natural caution, allowing hunters to approach and capture them.</note>

<margin>54</margin>
### In what manner they represent a man who is captured and charmed by dancing.
When noting a man who delights in dancing and the song of flutes, they paint a turtledove <note>original Latin: *turturem*</note>. For this bird is indeed captured by the flute <note>original Latin: *tibia*; a reed instrument similar to an oboe or flute</note> and by dancing.

**Hieroglyphics** <note>The "sacred carvings" of Egypt, interpreted here as a system of moral and philosophical symbols.</note>, **turtledove** <note>In this context, a symbol of one who is easily led or captivated by sensory pleasures like music.</note>, **flute** <note>original Latin: *tibia*; original Greek: *aulos*.</note>, **dancing** <note>original Latin: *saltatio*; original Greek: *orchesis*.</note>.

<summary>This page explains that the turtledove serves as a hieroglyph for a person easily enchanted by music and dance, stemming from the ancient belief that the bird could be lured and caught through these arts.</summary>
<keywords>turtledove, music, flute, dancing, animal symbolism, Horapollo, enchantment</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large, leafy tree in the foreground of a landscape. In the background, there are smaller trees, rolling hills, and a small building or chapel. A cicada is perched on the trunk of the central tree.</image-desc>

### How to represent a musical man.

When they wish to signify a **musical man**, and also a **sacred rite** <note>original Greek: *teletēn*; often referring to the secret religious "mysteries" of the ancient world</note>, they paint a **cicada** <note>original Greek: *tettiga*</note>; for this creature does not speak through its mouth, but by making a sound through its back, it sings a beautiful melody. <note>Ancient Greeks believed cicadas were "the mouthpieces of the Muses." The biological observation that their sound comes from the thorax rather than the head led to their use as symbols for those who possess an internal, divine harmony.</note>

<margin>55</margin>

### In what manner they represent a man initiated into the sacred rites.

When signifying one devoted to **sacred matters and ceremonies**, they paint a **cicada** <note>original Latin: *cicadam*</note>. For this insect produces a most beautiful and sweet harmony, resonating not from its mouth, but from its back.

**Horapollo** <note>The legendary author of this treatise on Egyptian symbols.</note>, **Hieroglyphics** <note>The "sacred carvings" of Egypt, interpreted here as a system of moral and philosophical symbols.</note>, **cicada** <note>A symbol of the soul and of those who are divinely inspired or initiated into higher knowledge.</note>, **musical** <note>Relating to the arts or the harmony of the soul.</note>, **initiated into the sacred** <note>One who has been formally admitted into religious mysteries.</note>

<summary>This page explains why the cicada is used as a symbol for a musical person and a religious initiate, citing the ancient belief that the insect's song originates from its back rather than its mouth.</summary>
<keywords>cicada, music, sacred rites, initiation, Horapollo, symbolism, ancient biology, Muses</keywords>

HIEROGLYPHICS.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular frame depicting an eagle perched on a rocky cliffside. The eagle has its wings partially spread and is looking down towards a nest or lower crag. The background shows a mountainous, desolate landscape with sparse, withered vegetation.</image-desc>

### How to represent a king who lives in seclusion and shows no mercy for failings.

When they wish to signify a **king who lives apart** <note>original Greek: *idiázonta*; suggesting one who is solitary or detached from the common crowd</note> and who is **unmerciful toward mistakes** <note>original Greek: *ptaismasi*; literally "stumbles" or "slips," referring to the errors of his subjects</note>, they paint an **eagle**. For this bird makes its nest in deserted places and flies higher than all other winged creatures. <note>The eagle's high flight represents a perspective far above the common person, while its solitary nest in the wilderness symbolizes a ruler who does not mingle with his people, maintaining a cold, objective distance that allows for no leniency.</note>

->nor grants pardon for the slips<-
->of his subjects.<-

If they wish to signify a **king** who delights in **solitude** and who does not forgive errors, they paint an **eagle** <note>original Latin: *aquilam*</note>. For this bird builds its nest in deserted places and flies more sublimely than the rest of the winged creatures.

ORI APOLLINIS

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a phoenix bird perched upon a rocky outcrop or nest. In the background, a detailed cityscape with towers and fortifications is visible under a sky with a sun or moon. The bird appears to be in a dynamic pose, possibly related to its rebirth or death as described in the text.</image-desc>

### How they represent a long-lasting restoration.
When they wish to signify a **long-lasting restoration** <note>original Greek: *apokatastasis*; a term used in ancient philosophy and astronomy to describe the return of celestial bodies to their original positions or a general "setting right" of the world</note>, they paint the bird called the **Phoenix**. For when that bird is born, a restoration of affairs takes place. It is born in this manner: when the Phoenix is about to reach the end of its life, it strikes itself violently against the ground, and a wound is created by the impact. From the **fluid** <note>original Greek: *ichōros*; in Greek mythology, "ichor" is the golden fluid that flows in the veins of the gods instead of blood</note> that flows out from the wound, another phoenix is born. As soon as its feathers have grown, this new bird travels with its father to the **City of the Sun** <note>Heliopolis, a major religious center in ancient Egypt dedicated to the sun god Ra</note> in Egypt. Arriving there at the rising of the sun, the father dies; after the father’s death, the young bird departs again for its own homeland. The Egyptian priests then bury the deceased Phoenix.

->How they represent a long-lasting<-
->restoration of things.<-
Moreover, a long-lasting restoration, and which <note>This fragment follows the Latin translation typically found in Renaissance editions of Horapollo, such as those by Filippo Fasanini.</note>

<summary>This page describes the Phoenix as a symbol for the "apokatastasis" or the cyclical restoration of the world, detailing its unique biological rebirth from its father's fluids and its ritual journey to Heliopolis.</summary>

It is born in this manner: when the Phoenix is about to die, it casts itself upon the ground with great force, from which it receives a wound. From the **gore** <note>original Latin: *sanie*; referring to the discharge or fluid from a wound, often associated in myth with the regenerative essence of the bird</note> that flows out, another is born. ->How they represent a lover of their father.<-

When they wish to signify a person who **loves their father** <note>original Greek: *philopatora*; a title often used by Hellenistic kings, but here used to describe the virtue of filial devotion</note>, they paint a **stork** <note>original Greek: *pelargos*; in antiquity, the stork was a famous symbol of "antipelargosis" or the reciprocal care of parents by their children</note>. For this bird, once it has been raised by its parents, does not separate from its own fathers, but remains with them until the very end of their old age, providing them with care.

<margin>58</margin>### How they represent a lover of their father.
When they wish to indicate a man **devoted to his father** <note>original Latin: *patris studiosum*</note>, they paint a **stork** <note>original Latin: *ciconia*</note>. For this bird, having been nourished by its parents, is never separated from them; instead, it remains with them until extreme old age, repaying them with **piety** <note>original Latin: *pietas*; a term describing the Roman virtue of duty and religious devotion to family and country</note> and diligent care.

### How they signify a man reviled by accusation and weakened.

When they wish to signify a man who has been reviled by an accusation and weakened by fear, they paint a **basilisk** <note>original Greek: *basiliskon*</note>; for that creature kills those who approach it with its very breath <note>original Greek: *physēmati*</note>.

Desiring to signify a man who, as a result of an accusation, was attacked with insults and fell into a state of illness, they paint a **basilisk**. For this creature destroys those who come near with its breath <note>original Latin: *afflatu*</note>.

### How [they represent] a man who stays at home.

When they wish to signify a man who stays at home <note>original Greek: *apethitēn anthrōpon*—this term refers to someone who does not go out or remains immobile</note>, they paint an **ant** and the **wings of a bat**; for if the wings are placed at the entrance of the ants’ nest, not one of them will come out <note>This reflects an ancient belief in natural "antipathies," where the presence of a predator's part (the bat) would paralyze or deter the smaller insects (the ants) through instinctive fear</note>.

---

<margin>64</margin>
->In what way [they represent] a man who<-
->keeps himself at home.<-

Signifying a man who does not go out of his house, they paint an **ant** <note>original Latin: *formicam*</note> and together with it the **wings of a bat** <note>original Latin: *pennas vespertilionis*</note>. For when these wings are brought near to the ants' nests, none of them will emerge.

<summary>This page explains how the combination of an ant and a bat's wing was used in Egyptian symbolism to represent a "homebody" or someone who does not leave their house, based on the observation that ants will not leave their nest if a bat's wing is placed at the entrance.</summary>

HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a landscape with a castle or fortified town in the background. In the foreground, several beavers are shown near a body of water and rocky terrain. One beaver appears to be biting or removing its own testicles, a classical myth regarding the beaver's escape from hunters.</image-desc>

### How [they represent] a man harmed by his own destruction.

When they wish to signify a man who is harmed through his own ruin <note>original Greek: *exōleias*—referring to utter destruction or ruin, or someone who is the architect of their own downfall</note>, they paint a **beaver**; for when that animal is pursued, it tears off its own testicles and throws them to the hunters <note>This reflects a widespread ancient belief—recorded by naturalists like Pliny the Elder—that beavers knew they were hunted for "castoreum," a substance found in their glands used for medicine, and would mutilate themselves to satisfy the hunters and save their lives</note>.

---

<margin>65</margin>
->How [they represent] a man who is himself the<-
->author of his own loss and ruin.<-

Wishing to signify him who is injured by a loss brought about and inflicted by himself, they paint a **beaver** <note>original Latin: *castorem*</note>. For when this animal sees itself targeted by the hunt, it casts its own torn-out testicles to the hunters <note>original Latin: *in prædam obiicit*—literally "throws them as the prey" to satisfy the pursuit</note>.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic use of the beaver to represent a person who inflicts self-harm or sacrifices a part of themselves to avoid total destruction, based on the ancient myth of the beaver's self-castration to escape hunters.</summary>

<keywords>beaver, castor, self-sacrifice, self-mutilation, survival, hunting myth, Horapollo, Hieroglyphica</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the symbolic sequence in Horapollo’s Hieroglyphica. ->HIEROGLYPHICA<-

L ij

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two monkeys in a wooded landscape. A larger monkey sits on the right, facing left, while a smaller monkey sits on the ground to the left. The scene includes trees and a distant horizon line.</image-desc>

### How [they represent] a man whose estate is inherited by a hated child.

When they wish to signify a man whose inheritance is received by a child he hated, they paint a **monkey** <note>original Greek: *pithēkon*—this refers generally to an ape or monkey, often associated in antiquity with imitation or distorted human behavior</note> holding another small monkey behind it. For that animal gives birth to two offspring; it loves one of them excessively, but hates the other. The one it loves, it carries in front [in its arms] and thus kills it <note>This refers to the ancient belief that the mother monkey, out of over-affection, would hug her favorite baby so tightly that she accidentally smothered it to death</note>; however, the one it hates, it carries on its back, and that one it successfully rears.

---

<margin>66</margin>
### How [they represent] a man who has left an inheritance to a son he disliked.

Pointing to a man whose goods have been succeeded by a hated son, they paint an **ape** <note>original Latin: *simium*</note>, and another small one behind. For since this animal gives birth to twins, it loves one of them beyond measure and hates the other. However, by constantly carrying the one it loves in front of itself, it suffocates it <note>original Latin: *suffocat*</note>. But the one it hates, it keeps behind its back, and subsequently raises it to adulthood.

<summary>This page describes the monkey as a symbol for a person whose preferred heir dies through over-protection, leaving the inheritance to a less-favored child who survived through neglect. It relies on a popular ancient fable regarding the maternal habits of apes.</summary>

<keywords>monkey, ape, inheritance, parenting, accidental death, favoritism, Hieroglyphica, allegory</keywords>

### How [they represent] a man hiding his own failings.

When they wish to signify a man hiding his own failings <note>original Greek: *elattōmata*—defects, shortcomings, or weaknesses</note>, they paint a **monkey** <note>original Greek: *pithēkon*</note> urinating; for the snub-nosed creature <note>original Greek: *simoutos*—a reference to the flat-nosed appearance of apes</note>, while urinating, hides its own urine.

<margin>67</margin>
### How [they represent] a man covering his errors.

When they wish to mark one who hides his crimes or errors, they paint an **ape** <note>original Latin: *simium*</note> urinating. For this creature, while urinating, conceals its urine <note>This reflects an ancient belief, also noted by Pliny the Elder, that certain animals hid their waste to prevent hunters from tracking them or because the substance was thought to have magical properties</note>.

->How [they represent] one who hears exceptionally well.<-

### HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a hyena standing over its prey or young in a wooded area. The animal is rendered with shaggy fur and a prominent mane, standing amidst gnarled tree trunks and roots.</image-desc>

->How [they represent] an unstable person.<-

When they wish to signify one who is unstable and does not remain in the same state, but is at one time strong and bold, and at another time weak and cowardly, they paint a **hyena** <note>original Greek: *hyainan*</note>; for this animal is at one time male, and at another time female. <note>The belief that the hyena changed its sex annually was a common myth in antiquity, found in the works of Aristotle, Pliny the Elder, and Ovid. It was frequently used as a metaphor for fickleness, deception, or a "double" nature.</note>

---

<margin>69</margin>
->In what way [they represent] the unstable.<-

Wishing to designate someone who is unstable, and not persisting in the same state, but who is meanwhile robust and daring, and yet at other times weak and fearful, they paint a **hyena** <note>original Latin: *hyænam*</note>. For this creature is sometimes male, and sometimes female.

<term>astaton</term> <note>Greek for "unstable" or "flickering."</note>, <term>hyaina</term> <note>The Greek word for hyena.</note>, <term>instabilem</term> <note>Latin for "unstable."</note>, <term>hyænam</term> <note>The Latin word for hyena.</note>, Horapollo, hieroglyphics

<summary>This page explains why the hyena is used as a hieroglyphic symbol for instability or an inconsistent character. The symbolism relies on the ancient biological misconception that the hyena periodically changed its gender.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Hieroglyphica of Horapollo. While the previous page discussed the hyena as a symbol of instability due to its supposed gender-shifting, this page introduces a new symbolic meaning: the hyena as a dominant force over its natural enemies, even after death.</meta>

HIEROGLYPHICS

L iiij

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two animal skins (pelts) hanging from a horizontal wooden pole supported by two tree trunks. The skin on the right is spotted, representing a leopard or panther (pardus), while the skin on the left represents a hyena.</image-desc>

### How [they signify] a man defeated by those inferior to him.

When they wish to signify a man being defeated by those of lesser status, they paint two skins: one of a **hyena** <note>original Greek: *hyaines*</note> and the other of a **leopard** <note>original Greek: *pardaleos*</note>. For if these two skins are placed together, the skin of the leopard loses its hair, while the other does not. <note>This allegorical "battle of the skins" was a popular belief in the Renaissance, suggesting that the "natural antipathy" between two animals continues even in their hides.</note>

---

->In what way [they represent] one who has been conquered by inferiors.<-

When they wish to suggest one who has yielded to a lower and weaker person, they paint a pair of skins, one of a **hyena** <note>original Latin: *hyænæ*</note> and the other of a **leopard** <note>original Latin: *pardi*</note>. For if these are joined together, the skin of the leopard casts off its hair, but the other does not. <note>The hyena was often considered a "baser" or "inferior" animal compared to the noble leopard, yet in this specific lore, the hyena's hide possesses a magical potency that destroys the leopard's fur.</note>

<term>Hieroglyphica</term> <note>The title of the work, referring to the "sacred carvings" or symbols of Egypt.</note>, <term>hyena</term> <note>A scavenger animal often used in emblems to represent transition or dominance.</note>, <term>pardus</term> <note>A leopard or panther; in heraldry and ancient biology, the "pard" was noted for its spots and swiftness.</note>, <term>antipathy</term> <note>A natural or occult opposition between two species that exists even after death.</note>

<summary>This page describes how the skins of a hyena and a leopard are used to symbolize a superior person being overcome by someone of lower status. The symbolism is based on the myth that a hyena's pelt will cause a leopard's fur to fall out if they touch.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, hyena, leopard, skins, dominance, natural antipathy, animal symbolism, hair loss</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Hieroglyphica of Horapollo. While the previous page (167) discussed the "battle of the skins" where the hyena's hide was said to destroy the leopard's fur, this page transitions to the behavior of the living animal as a symbol for victory or defeat in conflict.</meta>

THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a hyena standing in a landscape with trees. The animal is shown in profile, looking back over its shoulder toward the right.</image-desc>

### How [they signify] a man overcoming his own enemies.

To signify a man overcoming his own enemies, they paint a **hyena** <note>original Greek: *hyainan*</note> turning toward the right side. But if [they wish to signify] one being defeated, they conversely paint her turning toward the left. For if this animal, while being pursued, turns toward the right, she destroys the pursuer; but if she turns toward the left, she is destroyed by the pursuer. <note>In ancient symbolism, the right side (*dexter*) was almost universally associated with power and success, while the left (*sinister*) was associated with weakness or misfortune.</note>

---

<margin>71</margin>
->In what way [they represent] a man superior to an enemy.<-

When showing one who has emerged superior to an enemy, they paint a **hyena** <note>original Latin: *hyænam*</note> turned toward the right; conversely, [they paint] the same animal turned toward the left to indicate one who is conquered. For when she is attacked by an adversary, if she turns to the right, she kills the pursuer; if to the left, she is killed by the same. <note>This Latin passage is a parallel translation of the Greek text above, reflecting how Renaissance scholars studied these "Egyptian" secrets through both classical languages.</note>

<term>Horapollo</term> <note>The purported author of the *Hieroglyphica*, though the work likely dates to the 5th century AD and blends genuine Egyptian tradition with Greek philosophical interpretation.</note>

<summary>This page describes the hyena as a symbol of victory or defeat based on the direction the animal turns when pursued by an enemy.</summary>
<keywords>hyena, victory, defeat, right and left symbolism, Horapollo, Hieroglyphica, pursuer, enemies</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hyena as a symbol in the Hieroglyphica. Following the previous page's focus on the hyena's physical movement (turning right or left) as an omen of victory or defeat, this section explores the symbolic and magical properties attributed to the animal's hide as an emblem of protection and endurance.</meta>

HIEROGLYPHICS.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a hyena's pelt hanging from the branch of a gnarled tree. The landscape in the background shows rolling hills, clouds, and a small structure or ruin in the distance.</image-desc>

->How [they represent] a man who has fearlessly passed through the misfortunes brought upon him.<-

To represent a man who has fearlessly endured the misfortunes brought upon him until death, they paint the **skin of a hyena** <note>original Greek: *derma hyainēs*</note>. For if someone wraps themselves in this skin and passes through their enemies, they will not be harmed by anyone, but will pass through without fear. <note>This reflects a common belief in "sympathetic magic," where the properties of an animal—in this case, the hyena's supposed invulnerability or ability to ward off predators—are transferred to the person wearing its skin.</note>

<margin>72</margin>
->How [they represent] one who has intrepidly endured the calamities brought upon him.<-

To indicate one who has overcome and endured with a steady and constant mind all the calamities he encountered, even until the very end of his life, they paint the **skin of a hyena** <note>original Latin: *pellem hyenæ*</note>. For if one is clothed in this, even if they are carried through the midst of enemies, they will pass by fearless and unharmed. <note>This Latin passage mirrors the Greek text above, a standard feature in Renaissance editions of Horapollo where scholars provided parallel translations to assist in the study of classical languages and "Egyptian" wisdom.</note>

**Vocabulary:** <term>Hieroglyphics</term>, <term>hyena</term>, <term>hyena skin</term>, <term>calamities</term>, <term>fearless</term>.

<summary>This page describes the hyena's skin as a symbol for a person who survives hardships and enemies without fear. It highlights the belief that the pelt possessed protective qualities, allowing a wearer to pass through danger unharmed.</summary>
<keywords>hyena, skin, pelt, protection, misfortune, resilience, symbolism, Horapollo, endurance</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the symbolism of the hyena's hide as a charm of invulnerability to the symbolism of the wolf. While the previous page focused on enduring calamities through magical protection, this page examines the strategy of escaping enemies through calculated sacrifice, using the wolf's supposed self-mutilation as a metaphor.</meta>

HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a hunting scene in a forest. In the foreground, a man in a tunic raises a club or staff over his head, while another man behind him holds a spear. A hunting dog is seen at their feet. To the right, a wolf or similar beast is shown fleeing or being cornered near a large tree.</image-desc>

->How [they represent] a man crushed by his own enemies.<-

When they wish to signify a man who has been crushed by his own enemies, and yet escaped with only a small loss, they paint a **wolf** <note>original Greek: *lykon*</note> that has lost the tip of its tail. For when this animal is about to be hunted, it sheds its hair and the tip of its tail. <note>Ancient lore suggested that the tip of a wolf's tail contained a tuft of hair with magical or aphrodisiac properties. The belief held that the wolf would bite off this part of its body when pursued so that the "charm" would not fall into the hands of the hunters.</note>

---

<margin>73</margin> ->How [to represent] a man oppressed by enemies.<-

Wishing to indicate one who has been surrounded and oppressed by enemies, and who has rescued himself from a great danger and loss with only minimal harm, they paint a **wolf** <note>original Latin: *lupum*</note> with the end of its tail missing. For this creature, when it is being tracked by hunters, shears off its hair and the end of its tail. <note>This Latin translation, common in Renaissance editions, mirrors the Greek text above but emphasizes the "minimal harm" (*minimo damno*) of the escape, illustrating the concept of "cutting one's losses."</note>

**Vocabulary:** <term>Horapollo</term> <note>the supposed Egyptian author of this treatise on hieroglyphs</note>, <term>Hieroglyphica</term> <note>a work interpreting Egyptian symbols through the lens of Greek philosophy and natural history</note>, <term>wolf</term>, <term>lupus</term>, hunting, symbolism.

<summary>This page explains the wolf as a symbol for a man who escapes his enemies by making a small, calculated sacrifice, based on the folk belief that a wolf bites off its tail-tip to evade hunters.</summary>
<keywords>wolf, escape, sacrifice, enemies, hunting lore, Horapollo, hieroglyphics</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS.

<margin>
V6.
Seeing the raging males in a poured-out line, Pan says: "Who now blows my horns?" <note>original Latin: "Effuso cernens furentes agmine mas, Quis mea nunc flat cornua? Panus ait." This refers to the god Pan, the source of "panic" or sudden terror.</note>
Sudden fears come upon armed armies in the midst of their panics, making the entire multitude tremble, even when no manifest cause is present, unless [it is of the god Pan].
</margin>

->**How [to represent] a man fearing things that happen to him from the unseen.**<-

<margin>
These are the terrors which some call **Panics**. <note>original Latin: "Quos quidem S qui Panicos dic."</note>
Because Pan was said to blow his horns, he would strike sudden and unexpected terrors into the minds of men. The name Pan <note>original Latin: "παν."</note> in Greek signifies "all" <note>original Latin: "o" for "omne"</note>; he is a powerful god who strikes terror into shepherds, causing them to flee in a rush, neither following a path nor knowing where they go, as if in a dream.
</margin>

Wishing to signify a man fearing things that happen to him from the unseen, they paint a **wolf** and a **stone**. For this animal fears neither iron [weapons] nor a wooden rod, but only a stone. Indeed, if someone throws a stone at it, one finds it to be terrified; and wherever the wolf is struck by the stone, worms emerge from the wound. <note>This curious piece of ancient natural history suggests that the wolf—otherwise a bold predator—has a supernatural vulnerability to stones, which causes its flesh to rot or breed worms instantly upon impact.</note>

### How [to represent] a man fearing those things which happen to him unexpectedly and by some hidden reason.

When they wished to show one who fears dangers presented to him unexpectedly and from an unseen source, they paint a **wolf** and a **stone**. For this animal [does not fear] iron...

<margin>
The **wolf** <note>original Latin: *Lupus*</note> signifies the sinner, who fears nothing else except the **stone** <note>original Latin: *lapis*</note> of the one falling and rising, or **Christ**, who is the "cornerstone." For as Christ says: "Upon whom the stone falls, it will crush him, and he who falls upon the stone will be broken." Matthew 21. <note>The commentator here moves from nature to theology, interpreting the wolf's fear of stones as a metaphor for the sinner's fear of divine judgment and the "Stone which the builders rejected" (Christ).</note>
</margin>

->**How [to represent] a man suffering from fever, who has cured himself.**<-

When they wish to represent a man suffering from a fever who has cured himself, they paint a **lion** eating an **ape** <note>In ancient lore, particularly in the works of Pliny the Elder, it was believed that the lion—the king of beasts—could only be cured of the "tertiary fever" by consuming the flesh of a monkey.</note>; for that animal, if it should suffer from fever, recovers its health by eating an ape.

<margin>76</margin>
->**How a feverish man heals himself.**<-

Showing one who, laboring under a fever, cures himself, they paint a lion devouring a monkey. For this animal, once seized by fever, if it devours a monkey, it immediately recovers its strength.

<insert>
If you wish to be made healthy from lust <note>original Latin: "concupiscentia"</note> or the **fever of sin**, imitate Christ or the saints in their virtues and through penance; for the **ape** signifies **imitation**. <note>The writer of this addition is using a pun on the monkey's natural behavior: just as a lion eats a monkey to heal, a sinner "consumes" or takes in the "imitation" of holy figures to heal their soul.</note>

<margin>
From those things which he suffered, he learned. <note>original Latin: "Ex his quæ passus est, didicit." A reference to Hebrews 5:8, suggesting that wisdom comes through endurance.</note>

My Savior’s beauty, he says, is like that of a first-born bull. The horns of the Rhinoceros <note>The "rhinoceros" or "monoceros" was often used in older Latin Bibles to describe a powerful, one-horned creature, frequently translated as "wild ox" in modern versions.</note> are the horns of **APOLLO**; with them he shall toss the nations even to the ends of the earth. ### 77 How a man, warned and corrected by earlier losses and calamities [is represented].

If they wish to indicate one who has later become more moderate after the calamities into which he fell shortly before, they paint a **bull** tied back to a **wild fig tree**. For if he grows fierce, he grows gentle once bound to the wild fig.

<margin>
All things [are explained] by likeness...
</margin>

In ancient times, God was called terrible, an avenger, and the like; but after he was bound by the bond of sonship and the punishment of our sins—tied to the **fig tree**, that is, to the **Virgin Mary**—he was made most gentle.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the bull as a symbolic animal. While the previous page focused on the bull being tamed by the "wild fig tree" (an allegory for Christ’s incarnation), this page explores the bull as a specific hieroglyphic symbol for "Sophrosyne"—the Greek virtue of temperance, self-control, and moral restraint.</meta>

### HIEROGLYPHICS.

<margin>
Where.
Endure and abstain. <note>original: "Ἀνέχου καὶ ἀπέχου" (Greek) and "sustine et abstine" (Latin). This was the famous motto of the Stoic philosopher Epictetus, emphasizing endurance of hardship and restraint from worldly desires.</note>

A strong bull, powerful in its reproductive urges, yet serving a much weaker man and abstaining from a pregnant cow, is a most noble example of patience and self-control.

Just as in the preceding section, the bull—previously fierce—is said to have been tamed by being bound to the right joint; that is, in the humility of the **hypostatic union**. <note>term: <term>hypostatic union</term>—the Christian theological doctrine describing how the divine and human natures of Jesus Christ are united in one person.</note> Paul, in Titus: *kindness appeared*. <note>original: "Paul. tit. apparuit benignitas," referring to Titus 3:4: "But when the kindness and love of God our Savior toward man appeared..."</note>
</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a bull standing in a landscape. A rope or binding is visible around its front right leg (knee area), tethering it to a small stump or bundle on the ground.</image-desc>

-> How [to represent] a man possessing unchangeable temperance. <-

When they wish to signify a man who possesses an unchangeable and not fragile **temperance**, <note>term: <term>sophrosyne</term> (σωφροσύνη)—an Ancient Greek concept of excellence of character and soundness of mind, characterized by self-control and moderation.</note> they paint a bull with its right knee bound. For if you bind this animal by the right knee, you will find it follows the binding of the limb. Furthermore, the bull is always adopted as a symbol for temperance because it never mounts the female after she has conceived.

-> How [to represent] a man not yet endowed with certain and stable temperance. 78 <-

<margin>
immovable
</margin>
When they wish to show a man of doubtful and insufficiently stable temperance, they paint a bull tied at the right knee. For if you bind it by the right knee, you will find it follows the joint of the foot. Furthermore, the bull is always used as an example and sign of temperance, for the reason that it never goes into the female after conception.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of symbolic animals in Horapollo's *Hieroglyphics*. While the previous page used the tethered bull as a symbol of self-control (Sophrosyne), this page shifts to a warning against the destruction of one’s household and livelihood, using the image of livestock and a specific toxic plant.</meta>

### THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<margin>
Fleabane <note>original: "Conyza"</note> is a drying herb, of which Dioscorides <note>Dioscorides was a famous Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD, author of *De Materia Medica*.</note> writes in Book 3, Chapter 130. Its root is useless and its flower is sparse, dissolving into tufts. This herb, when tied to the hand, draws to itself all the worms of the house. It is a bitter herb, yet four-legged animals graze on it gladly; however, held by thirst, they perish. So it is here: the Root represents greed <note>original: "cupiditas"</note> and the flower is useless and very harmful. The Root is like a dream; the flower represents the bitterness in acquiring, in preserving, and in leaving behind wealth. Finally, in death, these things dissolve into tufts; the men sleep and find nothing in their hands. Gathered riches abound, yet the reality is bitter for beast-like men. This herb, when placed over hot coals, puts pests to flight; so should riches be given to the poor: "Redeem your sins with alms." <note>original: "Peccata tua Eleemos[yna]"—a reference to Daniel 4:27, common in medieval moralizing texts.</note>
</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a pastoral scene with several sheep and goats grazing or resting in a landscape with trees. One animal in the foreground appears to be lying down or dead, while others graze on the sparse vegetation.</image-desc>

-> How [to represent] a man who destroys his household's sheep and goats. <-
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄνθρωπον προβάτων & αἰγῶν φθορόοικον."</note>

When they wish to signify a man who brings ruin to his household of sheep and goats, they paint these animals eating **fleabane** <note>term: <term>fleabane</term> (κόνυζα/conyza)—a plant traditionally used to repel insects, but here described as fatal to livestock due to the intense thirst it causes.</note>; for these animals, after eating fleabane, perish when they are seized by thirst.

-> How [to represent] a domestic slaughterer of sheep and goats. <-
<note>original Latin: "Quo modo domesticum ouium & caprarum mactatorem."</note>

When they wish to signify one who exhausts and destroys the home by slaughtering sheep and goats, they paint the animals themselves feeding on fleabane. For when they have grazed on this herb, they perish, gripped by thirst.

<margin>
They draw to themselves worms, that is, sins... and the moth, that is, thieves, both domestic and foreign, in the process of acquiring and possessing. Yet four-legged animals gather it most gladly. Its uses: for when scattered over the ground and over coals, it puts to flight wild beasts and bedbugs, and kills fleas. Thus, [charity] puts to flight the burning coals of consumed charity; redeem [your sins]. See Dioscorides, as cited above.
</margin>

<vocab>Fleabane, Dioscorides, sheep, goats, greed, alms, slaughterer</vocab>

<summary>This page describes a hieroglyph for a "household destroyer," using the image of livestock eating fleabane (conyza), a plant that causes fatal thirst. The marginalia provide a moral allegory, equating the toxic plant to the "bitter" nature of greed and the fleeting value of earthly riches.</summary>

<keywords>Horapollo, hieroglyphics, fleabane, conyza, sheep and goats, greed, allegory, Dioscorides, almsgiving, moral instruction</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of animal symbolism from the *Hieroglyphics* of Horapollo. # HIEROGLYPHICA.

<margin>
Saffron <note>original: "Crocus"</note> softens hard swellings <note>original: "apostemata dura"</note>; so by its own nature and passion: Christ <note>represented by the "x" abbreviation in the original Latin</note> softens the hard hearts of the wealthy toward the poor.
</margin>

-> Honey causes bloating, so too do riches; Solomon [says]: do not eat much [honey]. <note>This refers to Proverbs 25:27: "It is not good to eat much honey."</note> <-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a crocodile on a riverbank. The crocodile is depicted with its mouth open, showing sharp teeth. In the background, there are classical architectural ruins including an archway and a broken column, with some foliage and trees.</image-desc>

<margin>
By the Crocodile, he understands the greedy rich man, who seeks the "honey" of wealth for pleasure <note>original: "voluptate"</note>. In whatever way [he gains it], the Crocodile flees Christ’s <note>original: "x"</note> teaching regarding the contempt for riches. Saffron <note>original: "Crocus"</note> gladdens the heart; so through this emblem, it gladdens God and the angels: "There is joy in heaven over one sinner who repents." <note>A reference to Luke 15:7.</note> From the smallest, he becomes the greatest.
</margin>

<insert>
Leviticus 11: The crocodile is an unclean animal, etc. <note>The crocodile was listed among the "unclean" swarming things in the Old Testament law.</note>
</insert>

### How they represent a person who is eating.

**When they wish to signify a person who is eating, they paint a crocodile with its mouth open. For this [animal]** <gloss>is then like the wealthy acquiring riches without limit, as we see everywhere. It hibernates in the winter and does not eat; clearly because of the "coldness" of his charity, the greedy man cannot "taste" or help the poor. It has no tongue, because God does not hear the greedy man’s prayer. He is a loathsome rich man who will be tormented in the pains of hell; here he has no tongue to confess his sins. Dung: that is, his wealth makes his face "beautiful" [to the world], for the wealthy are held in reverence and esteem in this world, though they are otherwise deformed in soul.</gloss>

### How they intimate a person who is eating.

<margin>
Saffron from the herb.
</margin>

To signify a person feeding <note>original: "Vescentem"</note>, they paint a crocodile with an open mouth. For it is so named because it flees saffron <note>original: "Crocum fugit"—this is a folk etymology, claiming the word *crocodilus* comes from *crocus* (saffron) and *deilo* (to fear/flee)</note>, because those who produce honey in Egypt strew saffron so that crocodiles do not consume the honey, since they are wonderfully delighted by it. This creature, above all others, grows from the smallest size to the largest; from an egg, it grows up to twenty-two cubits <note>About 33 feet or 10 meters.</note>. For four winter months it does not eat, and it is the only beast that has no tongue <note>Ancient naturalists like Aristotle and Pliny believed the crocodile lacked a tongue because it is attached to the lower jaw and does not move.</note>. Concerning this, Horace <note>A Roman lyric poet of the 1st century BC.</note> says: "Smeared with the dung of a crocodile." They believe it is called *Crocodilon* because it "fears saffron" <note>original: "κρόκον δείλει"</note>, and recoils from its scent. It is of an amphibious genus, for it lives on land and in water. Its dung is a medicine for the face; they call it *Crocodilēam*, as Pliny <note>Roman author of *Natural History*.</note> and Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire.</note> testify.

<vocab>Hieroglyphica, Crocodile, Saffron, Horace, Pliny, Galen, Avarice, Egypt</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the crocodile as a hieroglyphic symbol for gluttony and greed, emphasizing its open mouth, its lack of a tongue as a sign of spiritual silence, and the use of its dung in cosmetics as a metaphor for the superficial beauty of wealth.</summary>
<keywords>crocodile, saffron, gluttony, greed, Egyptian symbolism, Horace, Pliny, Galen, folk etymology, cosmetics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of animal symbolism from the *Hieroglyphics* of Horapollo. While the previous page focused on the crocodile's mouth as a symbol of gluttony and its relation to wealth, this page examines the crocodile's paralysis when touched by an ibis feather as a metaphor for the spiritual immobility of the greedy.</meta>

<margin>
Look for the properties of the Ibis there; it is a bird of Egypt, which, as Jerome <note>Saint Jerome, a 4th-century scholar known for his Latin translation of the Bible</note> writes, is of two kinds: one is entirely black, not unlike a fierce stork. This bird, representing a "trinity in unity" through its own nature, first washes away the burdens it is accustomed to carry. It feeds on snakes. Cicero, in Book 2 of *On the Nature of the Gods* <note>original: "de n. deor."</note>, writes: "The Ibises kill and consume a great multitude of snakes brought in from the deserts of Libya by the African wind, so that they do not harm people with their venomous bite or the stench of their carcasses."
</margin>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a crocodile lying on its belly. A bird, likely an ibis, is perched or hovering near its head, possibly placing a feather upon it. The scene is set in a rocky or natural landscape.</image-desc>

<margin>
By the lion's head, he understands [humanity] bowing through the night. I dedicate this to Minerva <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and strategic warfare</note>. I understand Wisdom; through the lion, I understand human power, which ought to be overcome by Wisdom. Likewise, the Lion represents Rapacity, of which Pythagoras <note>The Greek philosopher</note> said: "A lion is not to be kept in the state, nor are animals with blood to be sacrificed." See Plutarch <note>A Greek-Roman historian and essayist</note>, page 517.
</margin>

How they represent a rapacious and inactive man. <note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄρπαγα ἄνθρωπον καὶ ἀνενέργητον σημαίνουσιν"</note>

When they wish to signify a rapacious and inactive man, they paint a crocodile with an ibis feather on its head. For if you touch this [creature] with an ibis feather, you will find it becomes motionless.

### How they signify one who is greedy and sluggish.

They signify a man who is both greedy and lazy or inactive by painting a crocodile that has an ibis feather on its head. Indeed, if you touch this creature with an ibis feather, you will find it remains unmoved.

<margin>
According to this meaning, the Ibis signifies penance <note>original: "peniam," likely "poenitentiam"</note> which consumes the "snakes"—that is, the sins <note>original: "pta," likely "peccata"</note> of the Egyptians, meaning the darkness of the sinner. These [sins] are carried by birds, that is, the demons of the world and the flesh, by the wind of temptation. This [feather] makes the crocodile immobile; for if you place it on the head of the crocodile—that is, the wealthy man—he will remain [still]. For such people are fierce toward Jesus; no one saw them move from their place [to help the needy].
</margin>

According to a hidden reasoning, he will remain motionless; for men of this sort are unmoved to compassion for the harshness of poverty. In Egypt, during the three days <note>original: "9 die," possibly a scribal error for the three days of darkness in Exodus</note> of darkness, no one saw his brother because of the darkness—meaning they lacked compassion and did not perform works of charity, etc.

***

->In what way they represent a woman who has given birth once. 82<- <note>original Latin: Quo modo mulierem semel enixam. The number 82 refers to the chapter number in this edition of Horapollo.</note>

They represent a woman who has given birth once by painting a lioness. For she does not carry in her womb a second time. <note>This reflects an ancient belief, mentioned by Herodotus and others, that a lioness's cub destroys her womb with its claws during birth, making her unable to bear more children. <margin>Saint Epiphanius <note>A 4th-century bishop and Church Father known for his "Panarion," a massive refutation of heresies</note>, Bishop, in *Against Heresies*, Book 3, against the Antidicomarianites <note>A sect that opposed the doctrine of the perpetual virginity of Mary</note>, Heresy 78, Volume 2, near the middle.</margin>

<margin>
See the properties of the Ibis in this place <note>original: "ibi"</note>. It is a bird of Egypt, which as Saint Jerome <note>A 4th-century scholar and translator of the Bible</note> writes, is of two types: one is entirely black, not unlike a wild stork, while the other is [white]. It is said to cleanse itself with its own beak <note>A reference to the ancient belief that the ibis administered enemas to itself, which led to it being associated with cleanliness and medicine</note>. It feeds on serpents, as Cicero writes in Book 2 of *On the Nature of the Gods*: "The Ibises destroy a great multitude of serpents. Since they are tall, with stiff legs and a long, hooked beak, they kill and consume winged snakes brought in by the winds from Libya; thus they ensure these snakes do not harm the living with their bite, nor the dead with their stench."
</margin>

# HORAPOLLO <note>original Latin: ORI APOLLINIS</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a crocodile on the ground with an ibis bird perched upon its head or neck. The crocodile appears stationary or subdued. The background shows a rocky or natural landscape.</image-desc>

<margin>
Consider the head of the lion as leaning toward [wisdom]. Through the owl of Minerva <note>Roman goddess of wisdom</note>, understand wisdom; through the lion, understand human power, which ought to be subject to wisdom. Likewise, the lion represents rapacity, of which Plutarch <note>A Greek-Roman philosopher and biographer</note> writes... in the Republic...
</margin>

-> To signify a rapacious and inactive man. <- <note>original Greek: Ἅρπαγα ἄνθρωπον καὶ ἀνενέργητον σημαίνουσιν.</note>

-> When they wish to represent a rapacious and inactive man, they paint a crocodile having an ibis feather upon its head; for if you touch a crocodile with the feather of an ibis, you will find it rendered motionless. <- <note>original Greek: Ἅρπαγα ἄνθρωπον καὶ ἀνενέργητον βουλόμενοι σημῆναι, κροκόδειλον ἔχοντα ἴβεως πτερὸν ἐπὶ τῆς κεφαλῆς ζωγραφοῦσι...</note>

<margin>
According to this sentiment, the Egypt of darkness and the flesh of temptation... if you place [the feather] upon the head of the crocodile (meaning the rich man), he will remain motionless. For as in the biblical plague of darkness, no one could see his brother, nor did anyone move from his place.
</margin>

### In what way they represent the rapacious and the inert.

They signify a man who is simultaneously greedy and sluggish or inactive by painting a crocodile that has an ibis feather on its head. For if you touch this beast with an ibis feather, you will find it immediately paralyzed.

According to the reasoning <unclear>passed down</unclear>, such men are "immobile" when it comes to compassion; like those in Egypt during the nine days of darkness, they do not see their brothers <note>A reference to Exodus 10:23</note> and do not move toward works of charity.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of animal symbolism in Horapollo's *Hieroglyphica*. While the previous page (179) discussed the lioness as a symbol for a woman who has given birth only once, this page moves to the bear to symbolize the concept of gradual formation or education, transitioning from the biological to the metaphorical.</meta>

# OF HORAPOLLO <note>original: "ORI APOLLINIS"</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a bear in a wooded setting, hunched over and licking its cub. The scene illustrates the ancient belief that bear cubs are born as unformed lumps of flesh and are "licked into shape" by their mothers.</image-desc>

### How to represent a human being born unformed at the beginning.

When they wish to signify a person born unformed at the beginning, but who later receives a proper shape, they paint a pregnant bear. For she gives birth to a mass of congealed and hardened blood; later, this is shaped by being warmed between her own thighs, and is brought to completion by being licked with her tongue. <note>original Greek: "Ἄνθρωπον ἄμορφον γεννηθέντα... τῇ γλώττῃ λειχόμενον τελειοῦται." This reflects a common ancient myth in natural history—shared by writers like Aristotle and Pliny—that bear cubs are born as "mola" (unformed masses) and must be physically sculpted by the mother.</note>

### 83. In what manner a human is first brought forth rough and unformed.

They indicate a person who was indeed born unformed at the start, but afterwards received a shape, by a depicted pregnant bear. For she first gives birth to thickened and hardened blood: this she afterwards forms by warming it with her own thighs, and perfects it by licking it with her tongue. <note>original Latin: "Hominem qui initio quidem informis natus sit... linguáque lambens perficit." This is the source of the English idiom "to lick into shape."</note>

<insert>
God left man in the power of his own counsel; he fashions himself by free will, whether toward punishment or toward life, while divine predestination remains firm. A teacher performs this same service for his pupils. <note>original Latin: "Reliquit deus hominem in manu consilij sui... hoc idem præstat discipulis magister." This handwritten or added note provides a moral and pedagogical application of the bear symbol: just as the bear shapes its cub, a teacher "shapes" a student, and a soul shapes its own destiny through free choice.</note>
</insert>

<summary>This page describes the bear as a hieroglyphic symbol for a person who undergoes a transformation from a "rough" state to a "formed" state, based on the ancient myth that bears lick their unformed cubs into shape. A later addition connects this concept to the theological idea of free will and the role of a teacher.</summary>

<keywords>Horapollo, Bear, Licking into shape, Embryology, Symbolism, Free Will, Education, Predestination</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS <note>original: "HIEROGLYPHICA"</note>
M iij

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of an elephant standing in a landscape with sparse vegetation and a cloudy sky. The elephant is depicted with detailed skin texture, large tusks, and a long trunk curved towards the ground.</image-desc>

->How to represent a strong man, and one who scents out what is beneficial.<-

When they wish to signify a strong man, and one who scents out what is beneficial, they paint an elephant having its trunk; for it scents with this, and it grasps things that fall in its way. <note>original Greek: "Ἄνθρωπον ἰσχυρὸν... τῇ προβοσκίδα... ὀσφραίνεται." The Greek text emphasizes the elephant's trunk (proboscis) as an organ of both perception and action.</note>

<margin>84</margin>
->In what way a robust man, and one who tracks down things for his own interest.<-

When they wish to signify someone who excels in both strength and intellect for discerning what is useful, they paint an elephant with its trunk. For with this it smells and pursues what it encounters. <note>original Latin: "Eum qui simul & viribus & ingenio valeat... elephantum pingunt." The Latin translation adds "ingenio" (intellect/talent), suggesting that the elephant represents a blend of brawn and brains.</note>

<insert>
A worthy image for a prudent and provident man, whether regarding worldly matters or eternal and heavenly things. Providing for himself—<unclear>Luke</unclear>. "Who among you, wishing to build a tower, does not first sit down and calculate the cost?" <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke 14:28, connecting the elephant's foresight to Christian prudence.</note> etc. Here is the caution against the sins of hell; from here, one learns to place little value on hardships, having been offered, that is, the hope of eternal happiness, and many other things of that kind.
</insert>

<vocab>Hieroglyphics <note>sacred carvings</note>, elephant <note>symbol of strength</note>, trunk <note>organ of discernment</note>, robust man, prudent, provident</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the elephant as a symbol for a person who possesses both physical strength and the keen foresight or intuition to recognize what is beneficial. A later handwritten addition connects this symbolism to the Christian virtue of prudence and the Gospel of Luke.</summary>

<keywords>elephant, strength, foresight, trunk, prudence, providence, Horapollo, animal symbolism, discernment</keywords>
