# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO <note>original: "ORI APOLLINIS"</note>
182

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting an elephant in a landscape with trees and a distant city. To the right of the elephant, a ram (aries) is shown running away. The elephant appears to be reacting to the presence of the ram.</image-desc>

<margin>
Abraham offered up his son to be sacrificed and yet he was not killed, but a ram in his place. Isaac is interpreted as "Laughter" <note>The Hebrew name Isaac (Yitzhak) means "he will laugh," often interpreted by theologians as the spiritual joy of the faithful.</note>, which remains in the mind of God; but carnal joy, which is symbolized by the ram, perishes in sinners, for carnal joy passes away most swiftly. The joy of a hypocrite is like a single point in time. Wisdom says: "They spend their days in good things, but in a moment they descend to hell" <note>A paraphrase of Job 21:13, often cited in warnings against fleeting worldly pleasures.</note>. There are many such passages in the divine scriptures. For this reason, the aforementioned prudence of the elephant is such that it flees [the ram], because eternal punishments are prepared for those who seek brief carnal delight. It is said that the elephant, upon seeing blood, burns for the fight; likewise the best of men—contemplating the death and the blood of Christ shed for our sins, and the carnal pleasures of Adam and his successors—burns against vice, through fasting, discipline, and prayer, etc.
</margin>

->How to represent a king who flees from folly and senselessness.<-

When they wish to signify a king who flees from folly and senselessness <note>original Greek: "μωρίαν καὶ ἀφροσύνην." Both terms denote a lack of wisdom or a state of being "senseless."</note>, they paint an elephant and a ram; for the elephant, upon seeing the ram, flees.

->In what way they represent a king recoiling from foolishness and imprudence.<-

In this way, they signify a King who avoids foolishness and imprudence with all his zeal, by painting an elephant and a ram together. For that animal [the elephant], having seen the ram, flees. <note>The Latin translation uses "imprudentiam" for the Greek "aphrosyne," emphasizing a lack of foresight or practical wisdom. The belief that elephants fear rams or pigs was a common trope in ancient natural history, such as in the works of Pliny the Elder.</note>

<summary>This page describes a hieroglyphic for a wise king who avoids folly, using the elephant's supposed fear of rams as a metaphor. The extensive marginalia provides a Christian allegorical interpretation, linking the ram to fleeting carnal joy and the elephant's "prudence" to the avoidance of sin.</summary>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO <note>original Latin: "ORI APOLLINIS"</note>
184

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a stag in a landscape. The stag has large antlers and is turning its head back in a moment of alarm to look at a coiled snake (a viper) on the ground. Trees and a small body of water are visible in the background.</image-desc>

->How to represent a man who is quick in movement, but moves thoughtlessly and foolishly.<-
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄνθρωπον ὀξὺ μὲν κατὰ τὴν κίνησιν, ἀσκέπτως δὲ καὶ ἀνοήτως κινύμενον"</note>

When they wish to signify a man who is quick in his movements but moves without reflection or understanding, they paint a **stag** and a **viper** <note>original Greek: "ἔλαφον καὶ ἔχιδναν" (elaphon kai echidnan)</note>; for the stag, upon seeing the viper, flees in haste.

### 87 How to represent a man who moves quickly, but unadvisedly and inconsiderately.
<note>This is the Latin translation of the Greek heading above, provided by the 16th-century editor.</note>

To signify one who is agile and swift, but who is carried along without judgment or reason, they depict a **stag** and a **viper**. Indeed, once the viper is seen, the stag turns to flight in a rash and headlong manner.

<insert>
Nay, the stag designates the good Christian who, having seen the viper of temptation—which kills the soul with the deadly poison of sin—rejects it with great speed and without delay, flees, and shakes it off, etc. This is just as it is told of Paul in the Acts of the Apostles on the island of Mytilene <note>The scribe likely refers to Acts 28, which actually takes place on the island of Malta (Melita). Paul was bitten by a viper but shook it off into the fire unharmed.</note>. "Resist the beginnings" <note>original Latin: "Principiis obsta," a famous maxim from the poet Ovid, suggesting that it is easier to stop a vice or an illness at the very start than when it has taken root.</note>. Smash the head of the serpent to save your skin <note>original Latin: "caput serpenti p cute" (pro cute) — a proverbial expression meaning one should protect the head (symbolizing the soul or faith) even at the cost of the body/skin.</note>, that is, break the beginning of temptation and <unclear>etc</unclear>
</insert>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

->How to represent a man who has lived a full life.<-
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄνθρωπον ζήσαντα τέλειον βίον."</note>

When they wish to represent a man who has lived a full life, they paint a **dead crow**; for this bird lives one hundred years according to the Egyptians. An Egyptian year consists of four common years. <note>In this symbolic system, a "year" is sometimes equated to a four-year Sothic cycle or Olympiad, meaning the crow was believed to live 400 solar years.</note>

### 89 How to represent a man who has lived a full and proper age.
<note>This is the Latin version of the chapter heading: "Quo modo hominem qui plenam iustámque ætatem vixerit."</note>

Wishing to show one who has lived a man’s proper and lawful age, they paint a dead crow. For this bird lives one hundred years calculated according to the Egyptian custom. It is established, however, that an Egyptian year is equal to four usual and common years.

<margin>
This can also be referred to the remembrance of death. For since this bird is so long-lived, it eventually dies, as divine history shows; for in Genesis it is written: Adam lived 930 years <note>The OCR text reads "900," though the Bible traditionally states 930.</note> and he died; Methuselah lived 969 years <note>The OCR text reads "963."</note> and at last he died, etc.
</margin>

<vocab>Horapollo, Hieroglyphica, crow, Egyptian year, Adam, Methuselah</vocab>

The marginal commentary connects this longevity to the biblical patriarchs, emphasizing that even the longest lives must eventually end in death.</summary>

In ancient and Renaissance bestiaries, the leopard was often characterized by its spotted coat, which was seen as a metaphor for a "spotted" or inconsistent soul, and its ability to hide in wait.</term>; for this animal hunts other creatures by stealth, never allowing its predatory drive to be seen while it stalks its prey.

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO
188

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large stag standing in a forest. To the right, a figure is seated at the base of a tree, playing a flute or pipe. The stag appears to be listening to the music, its posture relaxed and attentive to the sound.</image-desc>

->How to represent a man deceived by flattery.<-
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄνθρωπον ἐξαπατώμενον διὰ κολακείας."</note>

When they wish to signify a man who is being deceived by flattery, they paint a **stag** <term>stag: in Greek, *elaphos*. Ancient natural history often claimed that stags were uniquely susceptible to music, becoming so entranced by the sound of a pipe or flute that they would forget their safety.</term> with a man playing a flute; for this animal is captured while listening to the melodic tunes of those playing the pipes, as it is utterly charmed by the pleasure of the sound.

---

### 91 ->How to represent a man deceived by adulation.<-
<note>original Latin: "Quomodo hominem adulatione deceptum."</note>

To indicate one who is captured by sycophancy <term>sycophancy: in Latin, *assentatio*, referring to the practice of insincere praise used to gain advantage.</term>, they paint a stag with a flute player. For this creature, while listening to the sweetest melody of the players, is soothed as if by a great pleasure, and thus falls easily into the hands of hunters.

<insert>
÷ Thus it is with princes, high-ranking clergy, and almost all other nobles who are led astray by the flattery of sycophants. ÷ Thus it is with the foolish and incautious sinner, lured by the sweetness of sins; as the proverb says, "The flute sings sweetly while the fowler deceives the bird." <note>The Latin quote "fistula dulce canit, dū voluc. decip. auceps" is from the *Distichs of Cato*, a popular medieval and Renaissance textbook on morality.</note>
</insert>

<note>The following list highlights the key terms used in this entry.</note>
**Vocabulary:**
**Horapollo**, the purported author of this work; **Hieroglyphica**, the study of sacred carvings; **Adulatione**, meaning flattery; **Assentatio**, meaning sycophancy or "yes-man" behavior; **Ceruum**, the Latin word for a stag; **Tibicine**, a flute player or piper; **κολακείας** (*kolakeias*), the Greek term for flattery; **ἔλαφον** (*elaphos*), the Greek term for a stag.

<summary>This page uses the stag's legendary love of music as a metaphor for how humans, especially those in power, are easily led to ruin by the "sweet music" of flattery and sycophancy.</summary>
<keywords>stag, flute player, flattery, deception, moral lesson, princes, hunters, music, Horapollo</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICA
189

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a hoopoe bird perched atop a trellis in a vineyard. The vines are laden with clusters of grapes. In the background, rolling hills and a cloudy sky are visible.</image-desc>

->How to represent a prediction of a plentiful wine harvest.<-
<note>original Greek: "πῶς πρόγνωσιν εὐκαρπίας οἴνου."</note>

When they wish to signify a foreknowledge of a plentiful harvest of wine, they paint a **hoopoe** <term>hoopoe: in Greek, *epops*. A bird known for its striking crown of feathers and its distinctive "oop-oop-oop" call, which ancient observers linked to the timing of the agricultural seasons.</term>; for if that bird calls out before the season of the vines <note>referring to the time before the vines begin to bud or flower</note>, it signifies an abundance of wine.

->How to represent a prophecy of a copious vintage.<-
<note>original Latin: "Quo modo præsagium copiosæ vindemiæ."</note>

To indicate a prediction of an abundance of wine, they paint a hoopoe. If this bird sings before the season of the vines, it foretells an extraordinary quality of wine as well as a great quantity.

---

<margin>The hoopoe, a foul-smelling bird <note>original Latin: "Upupa avis fœtida." The hoopoe was often described as "foul" in medieval bestiaries because of its nesting habits and the odor it produces to deter predators.</note>, represents the sinner. If he sings before the time of the vintage—that is, the time of death—delighting in the vices to which he has surrendered his flesh, he signifies an abundance of sins by which men are intoxicated. In this state, they are without sight of their own condition, without hearing the threat of eternal damnation, without the tongue of confession, and without any impulse toward good works or repentance; as the Psalm says: *They were shaken like a drunken man, and all their wisdom was consumed.* <note>A reference to Psalm 107:27.</note></margin>

<vocab>Hieroglyphics, hoopoe, vintage, vice, sinner, drunkard</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the hoopoe bird as a prophetic symbol for a successful grape harvest in the Greek and Latin traditions, while the marginal note provides a stark Christian moralization, likening the bird to a sinner intoxicated by vice before death.</summary>

<keywords>hoopoe, wine harvest, vineyard, prophecy, moral allegory, vice, repentance, Horapollo</keywords>

### How to represent a man harmed by a grape.
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄνθρωπον, ὑπὸ σταφυλῆς βλαβέντα."</note>

A fern known for its ability to shed water, which ancient writers found symbolic of purity.</term>; for this bird, having been harmed by eating grapes, recovers its health by placing maidenhair in its own mouth.

### How to represent a man whom the eating of grapes has harmed.
<note>original Latin: "Quomodo hominem, cui uvæ esus nocuerit."</note>

<margin>
93
</margin>

<margin>
Concerning one to whom Drunkenness brought offense through the grape <note>original Latin: "staph[yle]"</note>, he brings **maidenhair** <note>original Latin: "adiantum"</note>—that is, **confession**—to the mouth; for maidenhair grows in waters and yet is not made wet by water. In the same way, confession, which proceeds from virtues, ought to be pure, whole, true, humble, and lowly, without sin. Maidenhair (commonly called *culantrillo* <note>a Spanish common name for the maidenhair fern</note>) is effective for many things: it strengthens the stomach (that is, the conscience) and the brain (that is, reason) before God; it purges bile and phlegm—meaning all vices. See Dioscorides <term>Dioscorides: a 1st-century Greek physician and botanist whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary source for herbal knowledge in the Renaissance.</term>, book 4, chapters 137 and 138. To indicate one harmed by the eating of grapes and curing himself, they paint a hoopoe and the maidenhair herb. For if this bird is injured by consumed grapes, by placing maidenhair in its mouth, it regains its former health after a certain space of time has passed.

### How to represent pederasty.
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς παιδεραστίαν."</note>

When they wish to signify **pederasty** <term>pederasty: from the Greek *paiderastia*, historically referring to same-sex relations between men; here used by the author to categorize specific behaviors in the animal kingdom applied to human morality.</term>, they paint **two partridges**; for those birds, whenever they are widowed <note>meaning they are without a female mate</note>, they consort with one another.

---

<margin>95</margin>

### In what way to represent the obscene love of boys.
<note>original Latin: "Quo modo obscœnum puerorum amorem."</note>

To designate **pederasty** <note>original Latin: "Pædicationem."</note>, they paint twin partridges. When these birds are widowed, they abuse each other in turn. <note>Ancient naturalists, including Aristotle and Pliny, claimed that male partridges were so lecherous that they would engage in sexual acts with each other if females were unavailable; Horapollo uses this lore to create a hieroglyphic symbol.</note>

### How to represent an old man dying of hunger.
<note>original Greek: "πῶς γέροντα ὑπὸ λιμοῦ ἀποθανόντα."</note>

When they wish to signify an **old man dying of hunger**, they paint an **eagle** with a **curved beak**. For as that bird grows old, its beak becomes curved, and it dies of famine. <note>This entry reflects an ancient belief, also found in the writings of Aristotle and Pliny the Elder, that an eagle's upper beak continues to grow and curve as it ages until it eventually prevents the bird from opening its mouth to eat.</note>

When they wish to show an old man who has perished from hunger, they paint an eagle with a hooked beak. For as this bird ages, its beak becomes hooked, and thus it perishes from lack of food. <term>inedia: a Latin term for fasting or starvation, often used in medical or natural history contexts to describe death by hunger.</term>

# HORUS APOLLO
<note>original: "ORI APOLLINIS"</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large, leafy tree with a bird's nest nestled in its upper branches. A crow is shown in flight above the nest, appearing to approach or hover over its young.</image-desc>

->### How to represent a man living in constant motion and agitation.<-
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄνθρωπον ἐν κινήσει καὶ θυμῷ διάγοντα."</note>

When they wish to signify a **man who is always in motion and living in a state of agitation**, who does not even rest while he is being nourished, they paint the **fledglings of a crow**. For this bird feeds its young even while it is in flight. <note>The Greek term "θυμῷ" (thymō) suggests a restless spirit or intense energy, while the image of feeding on the wing serves as a metaphor for a person so driven by activity that they cannot pause for basic necessities.</note>

<margin>97</margin> ->In what way to represent a man living in perpetual motion and agitation of mind.<-
<note>original Latin: "Quo modo hominem in perpetuo motu atque animi agitatione degentem."</note>

To signify one who is occupied in such continuous motion and intensity of mind that he does not rest even while eating, they paint the **chicks of a crow**. For this bird feeds its chicks even while flying. <term>cornix: the Latin term for a crow or raven, birds which in ancient lore were often associated with restlessness or prophetic vigilance.</term>

<vocab>Horus Apollo, Hieroglyphics, crow, cornicis, perpetual motion, agitation, θυμῷ</vocab>

### How to represent a man who knows the things on high.
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄνθρωπον εἰδότα μετέωρα."</note>

When they wish to signify a **man who knows things on high** <note>The Greek "μετέωρα" (meteōra) refers to atmospheric or celestial phenomena—the origin of our word "meteorology."</note>, they paint a **flying crane**. For that bird flies very high indeed so that it may observe the clouds, seeing if perhaps a storm is brewing, in order that it might remain in tranquility.

---

<margin>98</margin>
->How to represent a man skilled in sublime matters.<-
<note>original Latin: "Quo modo hominem sublimium rerum peritum."</note>

When they signify one who is knowledgeable in **sidereal science** <note>original Latin: "sideralis scientiæ," referring to the study of the stars and constellations.</note>, they paint a **flying crane**. For it flies quite high so that it may see the clouds from close by, and not be tossed about by the storm, and thus it may enjoy a deep peace. <term>grus: the Latin name for the crane, a bird celebrated in antiquity for its high-altitude migration and perceived foresight regarding the weather.</term>

<vocab>Hieroglyphica, crane (Greek: γέρανος, Latin: grus), high things/meteorology (μετέωρα), sidereal science (sideralis scientia), skilled in sublime matters (sublimium rerum peritum)</vocab>

->How to represent a man living in movement and in passion.<-
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄνθρωπον ἐν κινήσει καὶ θυμῷ διάγοντα." The word "θυμῷ" (thymō) can mean anger, but in this context, it often refers to a spirited, restless, or passionate disposition.</note>

### How to represent a man who knows the things on high.
<note>original Greek: "Πῶς ἄνθρωπον εἰδότα μετέωρα." The term "μετέωρα" (meteōra) refers to things raised above the earth, including both astronomical bodies and weather phenomena.</note>

When they wish to signify a **man who knows things on high**, they paint a **flying crane**. For that bird flies very high indeed, so that it may see the clouds from above and avoid being caught in a storm, thus ensuring it remains in a state of tranquility.

---

<margin>98</margin>
->How to represent a man skilled in sublime matters.<-
<note>original Latin: "Quo modo hominem sublimium rerum peritum."</note>

When they signify one who is knowledgeable in **astronomical science** <note>original Latin: "Sideralis scientiæ," literally "the science of the stars."</note>, they paint a **flying crane**. For it flies quite high so that it may observe the clouds from nearby, and thus it is not shaken by a tempest, but maintains a deep peace. <term>grus: the Latin name for the crane, which ancient naturalists like Pliny believed flew above the clouds to avoid foul weather, making it a natural symbol for foresight and the contemplative life.</term>

<vocab>Hieroglyphica, γέρανος (geranos), grus, crane, astronomy, sublimium rerum, μετέωρα (meteōra)</vocab>

<summary>This page identifies the flying crane as a symbol for an astronomer or a person with celestial foresight, based on the bird's ability to fly above storm clouds to find peace. It highlights the crane's role as a metaphor for rising above worldly disturbances through higher knowledge.</summary>

# HORUS APOLLO.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large bird of prey, likely a hawk or eagle, perched in a rocky nest. The bird is shown tending to a single chick while other eggs or debris lie scattered in the nest. The background features rugged, mountainous terrain.</image-desc>

->How to represent a man who has given up his own children due to poverty.<-

To signify a **man who has given up his own children due to poverty**, they paint a **pregnant hawk** <note>original: ἱέραξ (*hierax*). In Egyptian iconography, the hawk is ubiquitous, often associated with Horus and the sun, but here Horapollo focuses on a specific bit of ancient folk-biology regarding its nesting habits.</note>. For that bird, when laying three eggs, selects and nourishes only one, and breaks the other two. It does this because, during that time, it loses its talons, and is consequently unable to provide food for all three fledglings <note>original: βρέφη (*brephē*), literally "infants," here referring to the chicks</note>.

<margin>99</margin> ->How a man, pressed by want, sends away his own children.<-
<note>original Latin: "Quo modo hominem proprios à se dimittentem liberos ob paupertatem."</note>

When they wish to denote a **man who, forced by want, has bidden his children farewell**, they paint a **pregnant hawk**. For though it may lay three eggs, it chooses only one to nourish, while the other two are broken. It does this because during that season it loses its claws <note>original Latin: *vngues*, referring to the bird's talons</note>, and therefore is not able to raise three chicks at the same time. <term>paupertas: the Latin term for poverty or "want." In the context of this emblem, it reflects the ancient view of nature as a harsh system where survival requires difficult sacrifices.</term>

<vocab>Horapollo, Hieroglyphica, hawk, falcon, poverty, abandonment, survival</vocab>

<summary>This entry explains that a hawk represents a father forced by poverty to abandon his children, based on the belief that the bird destroys two of its three eggs because it cannot hunt well while molting its talons.</summary>
<keywords>hawk, ἱέραξ, accipiter, poverty, parenting, sacrifice, Egyptian symbols, talons</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS.
N iij

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a camel facing right, carrying a heavy pack consisting of three large rounded bundles secured with ropes. The camel is depicted in a rocky landscape with sparse vegetation.</image-desc>

->How to represent a man who is hesitant to move his feet.<-

When they wish to signify a **man who is hesitant to make movement with his feet**, they draw a **camel**; for it alone among all other animals bends its thigh, which is why it is called *kamēros* <note>original Greek: κάμηρος (*kamēros*). This is a folk etymology connecting the word for camel (*kamēlos*) with the words *kamptein* (to bend) and *mēros* (thigh), referring to the animal's distinct way of kneeling or its gait.</note>.

<margin>Others read this as *kammēros* <note>original Latin: "Alii κάμμηρος legunt."</note></margin>

->How to represent a man who is hesitant and slow in moving his feet.<-

When they wish to show a **man who is slow and sluggish in the movement of his feet**, they paint a **camel**. For this animal alone among all living creatures bends its leg while walking, from which it also received its name: since in Greek, **camel** <note>original Latin: *camelus*; original Greek: κάμηλος (*kamēlos*)</note> is thought to be called *kamēros*, as it were, from the bending of the legs <note>original Latin: *crurum inflexione*. This repeats the linguistic wordplay found in the Greek text above, suggesting the camel’s name describes its physical anatomy.</note>.

<vocab>Hieroglyphics, camel, kamēlos, kamēros, etymology, symbolism</vocab>

<summary>This page explains how the camel is used in hieroglyphics to symbolize a person who is slow or reluctant to walk, based on an ancient linguistic pun linking the animal's name to the bending of its thighs.</summary>
<keywords>camel, sluggishness, walking, leg bending, folk etymology, Greek language, animal symbolism</keywords>

THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular frame depicting a frog sitting in a marshy area surrounded by tall reeds or cattails.</image-desc>

### How to represent an impudent man who also has sharp vision.

When they wish to signify an **impudent man** who has **sharp vision**, they draw a **frog**. For this creature has no blood except in its eyes alone. They call those who have blood in that place "impudent" <note>This reflects an ancient physiological belief that "blood-shot" or red eyes were a physical manifestation of shamelessness or a lack of modesty.</note>. For this reason, the poet <note>The "poet" refers to Homer, the most revered author of the Greek world.</note> also says: *You drunkard, with the eyes of a dog but the heart of a deer.* <note>original Greek: οἰνοβαρὲς, κυνὸς ὄμματ’ ἔχων, κραδίην δ’ ἐλάφοιο (*oinobarès, kynòs ómmat’ échōn, kradíēn d’ eláphoio*). This is a quote from the Iliad (1.225), where Achilles insults Agamemnon, suggesting he has the bold, staring eyes of a dog but the cowardly spirit of a deer.</note>

### How to represent a shameless man of sharp and swift sight.

When designating an **immodest man** who is **quick of sight**, they paint a **frog**. This creature has no blood anywhere else but in its eyes. Furthermore, those who have eyes flecked with blood are called impudent. Whence also the poet: *You drunkard, shameless with the eyes of a dog, but with the heart of a stag.* <note>original Latin: *Ebrie, luminibus canis effrons, pectore cerui*. This is a Latin translation of the same Homeric line quoted in the Greek section above, reinforcing the connection between the eyes and character traits.</note>

<vocab>Horapollo, Hieroglyphics, frog, impudence, vision, Homeric quote</vocab>

<summary>This page describes how the frog is used in hieroglyphics to represent a shameless or impudent person, based on the ancient belief that frogs only possess blood in their eyes. It supports this association with a famous insult from Homer's Iliad regarding "dog-like" eyes.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, hieroglyphics, frog symbolism, impudence, Homer, Iliad, ancient physiology, vision</keywords>

### How to represent a man who was unable to move.

When they wish to signify a **man who for a long time was unable to move**, but who later regained the use of his feet, they paint a **frog having its hind legs**. For this creature is born without feet <note>The author is referring to the tadpole stage of a frog's life cycle, which lacks limbs.</note>, but as it grows, it later acquires its hind legs.

---

<margin>102</margin>
->How to represent a man who cannot move himself.<-

When showing a **man who**, though he could not move for a long time, **eventually moves with his feet**, they paint a **frog** having hind legs. For this creature is first born without feet, but afterwards, as it grows, it takes on hind legs. <note>original Latin: *Hominem qui cum diu ſeſe mouere non potuerit, poſteà tandem pedibus moueatur...* The Latin text mirrors the Greek above, emphasizing the biological transformation of the frog—from a legless tadpole to a mobile adult—as a metaphor for recovering from paralysis or a long period of inactivity.</note>

### How to represent a man who is an enemy to everyone.

When they wish to signify a **man who is an enemy of everyone** and isolated <note>original Greek: *apeschoiniismenon*, meaning separated, shunned, or literally "roped off" from others</note>, they paint an **eel**; for this creature is not found in the company of any other fish.

---

<margin>103</margin>
->**How to show a man who is hostile and hated by all.**<-

To represent someone who is **hostile to everyone** and who **shuns the company of others**, they paint an **eel**. For it is observed that this creature has no dealings with any other fish. <note>original Latin: *Omnibus infestum, & qui cæterorū consortium fugiat... anguillam pingunt.* The Latin translation of the Greek emphasizes that the eel's solitary nature makes it a perfect symbol for a misanthrope or a person who avoids social interaction.</note>

<term>anguillam</term> <note>Latin for "eel." In ancient natural history, eels were often thought to be unique among fish for their lack of scales and their perceived solitary lifestyle.</note>

### How to represent a man who saves many at sea.

When they wish to signify a **man who saves many at sea**, they paint the **torpedo fish** <note>original Greek: *narkē*. This is the electric ray, known in antiquity for its ability to numb its prey or enemies. The name shares a root with "narcotic."</note>. For when this fish sees many other fish that are unable to swim, it gathers them to itself and rescues them. <note>This reflects a curious piece of ancient folklore; while the electric ray actually uses its shock to hunt, ancient observers interpreted the way fish clustered around or were "stilled" by it as an act of protection or stabilization during storms.</note>

---

### In what way to show a man who preserves many in the sea.

Wishing to indicate a **man who preserves many in the sea**, they paint the **torpedo fish**. For when this creature sees many fish that are unable to swim, it draws them toward itself and keeps them safe. <note>original Latin: *torpedinem piscem*. The Latin term *torpedo* refers to the numbness or "torpor" caused by the fish's sting.</note>

<term>torpedo</term> <note>The electric ray. In the context of this "hieroglyph," the creature's numbing power is viewed metaphorically as a way to "hold" or "catch" those who are floundering in the water, preventing them from being swept away by the current.</note>

<summary>This page describes the torpedo fish (electric ray) as a hieroglyphic symbol for a person who rescues others at sea, based on the ancient belief that the fish protects smaller, struggling fish.</summary>

THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large octopus (polypus) in the water near a rocky shore. In the background, there is a coastal landscape with buildings, including a tower and classical architecture, under a cloudy sky with a sun or moon partially visible.</image-desc>

### How to represent a man who has wastefully consumed both useful and useless things.

When they wish to signify a **man who has wastefully consumed both useful and useless things**, they paint an **octopus** <note>original Greek: *polypoda*. Literally "many-footed," this refers to the octopus.</note>. For that creature, eating much and in a profligate manner, stores its food in its dens <note>original Greek: *thalamas*, meaning chambers or crevices in the rocks.</note>; and when it has consumed the useful parts, it then casts out the useless parts as well. <note>Naturalists in antiquity, including Aristotle, observed that octopuses leave "middens" or piles of shells and debris outside their burrows. This behavior was interpreted here as a metaphor for someone who consumes everything greedily and leaves a mess of waste behind.</note>

---

<margin>105</margin>
### In what way to show a man who has badly consumed both useful and useless things alike.

To indicate a **man who has consumed both useful and useless things badly**, they paint an **octopus**. For since it devours many things and does so immoderately, it gathers and stores its food in caverns; and when it has consumed the useful parts, it then throws away the remaining useless parts. <note>original Latin: *polypum*. The Latin text mirrors the Greek, emphasizing the "intemperate" or gluttonous nature of the animal’s eating habits.</note>

<term>polypus</term> <note>The octopus. In the context of these "hieroglyphs," the animal's natural habit of discarding shells outside its home is used to symbolize a person who lacks the wisdom to save or the restraint to consume moderately.</note>

<summary>This page describes how the octopus is used as a symbol for a man who consumes resources wastefully, based on the animal's habit of leaving food remains outside its den. It provides both the Greek and Latin versions of this symbolic interpretation.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, Hieroglyphics, octopus, polypus, gluttony, wastefulness, animal symbolism, Greek, Latin</keywords>

### How to represent a man who has gained power over those of his own kind.

When they wish to signify a **man who has gained power over those of his own kind** <note>original Greek: *homophylon*, meaning of the same race, tribe, or species.</note>, they paint a **crayfish** <note>original Greek: *karabon*. In ancient biological texts, this usually refers to the spiny lobster or a large crustacean.</note> and an **octopus** <note>original Greek: *polypoda*.</note>; for this creature [the crayfish] holds power over the octopuses, and carries off the first prize. <note>Ancient naturalists believed there was a natural hierarchy or enmity between certain sea creatures; here, the lobster is seen as the master of the octopus, despite them both being "sea-dwellers" or of a similar category.</note>

<margin>106</margin>
->How one who commands the people of his own nation.<-

When they wish to denote one who commands men of his own nation and kind, they paint a crayfish and an octopus. For that one [the crayfish] commands the octopuses, and holds the chief rank among them. <note>The Latin text—*Suæ nationis & generis hominibus imperantem*—closely follows the Greek, reinforcing the idea of a leader emerging from within a specific group to rule over his peers.</note>

THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a coastal scene. In the foreground, large oyster shells or mollusks are clustered on the shore. ### How to represent a man joined to a woman.

When they wish to signify a **man joined to a woman** from the very first age in which he was born, they paint **pregnant pinna-mussels** <note>original Greek: *pinnas enkyous*. The *pinna* is a large Mediterranean fan-mussel known for its silk-like fibers.</note>. For these, being born within the shell, are joined to one another inside that shell after only a short time. <note>Ancient naturalists believed that certain shellfish were born in pairs or mated immediately upon birth within their shared valves, making them a perfect symbol for a lifelong, "cradle-to-grave" union.</note>

---

<margin>107</margin>
->How to represent a man joined to a woman.<-

Signifying a man joined to a woman from a tender age, even from his **earliest childhood** <note>original Latin: *primis vnguiculis*, literally "from his first fingernails," an idiom meaning from one's earliest infancy or "from the cradle."</note>, they paint **pregnant oysters** <note>The Latin translation uses *ostrea* (oysters) whereas the Greek uses *pinnas* (fan-mussels).</note>. For since these are born in a shell, a little while after they are joined to one another within that same shell. <margin>☌</margin>

# HIEROGLYPHICS

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a coastal scene. In the foreground, a large crab is shown interacting with a bivalve mollusk (pinna) on the seabed. In the background, there is a turbulent sea with a ship, and a rocky coastline featuring several buildings or small houses nestled against the cliffs under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

->How to represent a man not looking out for himself.<-

When they wish to signify a **father**, or a **man who does not look out for himself** but is looked out for by his own kin, they paint a **pinna-mussel** and a **crab** <note>original Greek: *pinnan kai karkinon*. The *pinna nobilis* is a large Mediterranean fan-mussel known for its silk-like fibers.</note>. For this crab remains attached to the flesh of the pinna, and is accordingly called the **pinna-guardian** <term>pinnophylax: a small crab, often a pea crab, that lives commensally inside the shell of a pinna-mussel, alerting the mollusk to prey or danger.</term>. The pinna remains completely open in its shell when it is hungry; then, whenever a little fish enters while it is gapping open, the pinna-guardian bites the pinna with its claw. Feeling this, the pinna closes its shell, and in this way, it hunts the little fish.

->In what way to represent a man who does not take care or account of himself.<-

<margin>108</margin>

For this crab remains as if glued to the flesh of the oyster:

## OF HORAPOLLO

...from which also, by the logic of its name, it is called the **pinna-guardian** <note>original Greek: *pinnophylax*</note>, as if it were the guardian of the oyster. The oyster, when hungry, opens its shell wide. If, while it is open, some small fish should slip inside, the crab bites the oyster with its claw; feeling this, the oyster closes its shell, and in this way it hunts the little fish.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a coastal landscape with rocky cliffs and a winding river or inlet. In the foreground, a large fish (identified in the text as a scarus) is shown in the water, appearing to consume smaller fish.</image-desc>

->How to represent a man possessed by gluttony.<-

<margin>gluttony <note>original Greek: *laimeian*</note></margin>
When they wish to signify a man possessed by **gluttony** <note>original Greek: *lameian*</note>, they paint a **scarus-fish** <term>scarus: likely the parrotfish (Sparisoma cretense), which ancient naturalists like Aristotle and Pliny believed was the only fish to "ruminate" or chew its cud like a cow.</term>; for this is the only fish that **chews the cud**, and it eats every small fish it encounters.

<margin>109</margin>
How a man suffering from gluttony [is depicted].

Wishing to designate a man devoted to the **gullet** <note>original Latin: *gulae deditum*, meaning gluttonous</note>, they paint a **scarus**. For this is the only fish that **ruminates** <note>original Latin: *ruminat*</note> and devours all the little fish that cross its path.

# HIEROGLYPHICA.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a coastal landscape. In the foreground, an otter-like creature (the "aquatic cat") is crouched on a rocky shore near the water's edge. The background features a winding river or coastline, rocky cliffs with sparse vegetation including a palm tree, and a distant view of a town or settlement with small figures and a boat.</image-desc>

### How to represent a man vomiting his own food.

When they wish to signify a man who vomits his own food and then greedily eats it once more, they paint a **water-weasel** <term>water-weasel: The Greek *enydros galeos* likely refers to an otter or a species of shark. Ancient naturalists often conflated the two due to perceived similarities in their predatory nature.</term>. For this creature gives birth through its mouth, and while swimming, it swallows its offspring <note>original Greek: *ton gonon*. This reflects an ancient biological myth that certain animals, particularly sharks or vipers, protected their young by swallowing them or gave birth through the mouth.</note>.

### In what way to represent a man vomiting food.

Signifying one who, after he has vomited the food he consumed, greedily devours it again in such a way that he cannot be satisfied, they paint an **aquatic cat** <note>original Latin: *felem aquaticum*</note>. For this creature gives birth by the mouth, and while swimming, it absorbs its young.

<summary>This page explains how the "water-weasel" or "aquatic cat" (likely an otter or shark) represents a man who vomits and then re-consumes his food, based on the ancient myth that the animal swallows its own young.</summary>
<keywords>gluttony, vomiting, water-weasel, aquatic cat, otter, Horapollo, animal symbolism, biological myths</keywords>

### How to represent a man who associates with those of another race.

When they wish to signify a man who engages in relations with people of another race <note>original Greek: *allophylon*, meaning those of another tribe, nation, or species</note>, they paint a **muraena** <term>muraena: The moray eel. Ancient naturalists like Pliny the Elder famously claimed that the moray eel would come onto land to mate with terrestrial snakes, a myth used here to symbolize "mingling" outside of one's own kind.</term> fish. For this creature, coming up out of the sea, mingles with vipers <note>original Greek: *echesi*, referring to adders or vipers</note>, and then slips back again into the sea.

<margin>Chapter 111</margin>
->How to represent a man who has dealings with foreigners.<-

Indicating a man who delights in the company of foreigners <note>original Latin: *alienigenarum*</note>, they paint a muraena fish. For this creature, ascending from the sea into the rivers, joins with vipers, and immediately hurries back into the sea.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic use of the muraena (moray eel) to represent a person who seeks out "foreign" associations, drawing on the ancient biological myth that eels leave the water to mate with land snakes.</summary>

### How to represent a man punished for murder.

When they wish to signify a man who has been punished for a murder <note>original Greek: *phono*</note> and has repented, they paint a **turtledove** <term>turtledove: Called *trygon* in Greek. While usually a symbol of fidelity, here it is used for its legendary behavior when trapped.</term> entangled in a snare by a hook. For this bird, when it is captured, casts away the thorn that it has in its tail <note>This refers to an ancient bit of folk biology; it was believed that the turtledove had a stinging "thorn" or spike in its tail which it would shed or break off once caught, symbolizing the abandonment of its "weapon" or its former nature.</note>.

Wishing to designate a man who has paid the penalty for a murder committed and who has repented, they paint a turtledove entangled in a snare. For this creature, once captured, sheds the thorn which it carries in its tail.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two fishermen in a small boat on a turbulent sea. They are pulling in a large net. In the foreground, a cuttlefish (sepia) is visible in the water, releasing a dark cloud of ink to obscure itself from the hunters.</image-desc>

->**How to represent a man who has strived for something good.**<-

When they wish to signify a man who has strived for something good, and instead has fallen into misfortune <note>original Greek: *kakō*, literally "evil" or "badness," here implying a bad situation or danger resulting from one's actions.</note>, they paint a **cuttlefish** <term>cuttlefish: Known as *sepia* in both Greek and Latin, this cephalopod is famous for its defensive ink, which it uses to create a "smoke screen" to evade predators.</term>. For if this creature sees someone wanting to hunt it, it discharges a black liquid from its belly into the water, so that it can no longer be seen, and in this way, it escapes.

<margin>114</margin>
->**How to represent him who burns with the love of honorable things.**<-

Wishing to indicate one who devotes himself with his whole heart to most excellent pursuits <note>original Latin: *res pulcherrimas*, literally "the most beautiful things."</note>, and thereby exposes himself to dangers, they paint a cuttlefish. For if it perceives that snares are being set for it by anyone, it pours out a very black blood <note>original Latin: *nigerrimum effundit sanguinem*. While the Greek refers to it simply as "the black" [ink], the Latin translator describes the ink as "blackest blood."</note> from its belly into the water: by which it happens that it is no longer visible, and thus it escapes.

Wishing to signify a fertile man, they paint a **house sparrow** <note>original Greek: *strouthion pyrgiten*. ### How to represent a man who is fertile and devoted to sexual love.

Wishing to designate a man who abounds in seed, they paint a **little house sparrow** <term>house sparrow: The text uses the Latin *passerculum pyrgiten*, identifying the bird by its habit of living near human towers or dwellings.</term>. For this bird, driven by both immoderate passion <note>original Latin: *ira*, literally "anger." In early natural history, the aggressive or "angry" temperament was often linked to high sexual drive.</note> and a great abundance of seed, enters the female seven times in an hour, pouring out a copious amount of seed in quick succession.

### How to represent a man who formerly did not rule his own thoughts, but later came to possess his own wisdom.

When they wish to signify a man who previously was not the master of his own thoughts, but who later came to possess his own prudence <note>original Greek: *phronēseōs*, referring to practical wisdom or the ability to govern oneself.</note> and brought order to his own life, they draw a **syrinx** <note>original: *syringas*, a musical instrument consisting of several pipes of increasing length bound together; a pan flute.</note>. For this instrument is soothing and calls to mind the things done by him according to the soul's passion, while producing a particularly orderly sound.

->How to represent a man who distributes justice equally to all.<-

When they wish to signify a man who distributes justice equally to all, they draw an **ostrich feather** <note>original Greek: *strouthokamēlou pteron*</note>. For this animal, unlike all others, has feathers that are equal on all sides <note>In most birds, the vanes of the feathers are asymmetrical (one side wider than the other), but the ostrich feather is famously symmetrical, making it an ancient symbol for balance and impartiality.</note>.

Wishing to signify him who imparts justice equally to all, they paint an **ostrich feather**. For this animal, above all others, possesses wing feathers that are equal in every part.

# HIEROGLYPHICS

### TO THE READER

To the *Hieroglyphics* of Horapollo <note>original Latin: *Ori Apollinis*</note> we have added certain other excerpts collected from various sources; because these tend toward the same purpose, we anticipated that they would not be unwelcome to you, kind reader.

---

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a large wooden Tau cross (shaped like a capital T) standing on a hill. In the background, there is a landscape with mountains, a body of water, and a small town or cluster of buildings. Clouds are visible in the sky above the cross.</image-desc>

->How they represent the life to come or salvation.<-

To indicate the future life or salvation <note>original Latin: *salutem*, which can mean health, safety, or religious salvation</note>, they would draw two lines, one of which was laid across the other drawn in a perpendicular fashion <note>This describes the "Tau cross," a T-shaped symbol which many Renaissance scholars identified with the Egyptian *Ankh* or "Key of Life"</note>. And they had no other explanation for this practice, except that it was a signifier of a certain divine mystery <note>original: *mysterij*</note>.

<summary>This page serves as a preface to an appendix of additional symbols following the main work of Horapollo. It features the Tau cross as a symbol for the afterlife and divine mystery.</summary>
<keywords>salvation, afterlife, Tau cross, divine mystery, appendix, symbolism</keywords>

# THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a tall, ornate scepter standing upright in a landscape. At the very top of the scepter is a single human eye. The background features a rolling landscape with trees, a distant city or castle on a hill, and large, stylized clouds in the sky.</image-desc>

### How they represent the two principal virtues of a king.

They signified the two primary virtues of a king by depicting a scepter with an eye above it: by the scepter, they suggested the man’s exceptional power; by the eye, they indicated his providence <note>original Latin: *prouidentiam*, meaning foresight or the protective care of a ruler</note>, through which it is fitting for him to look upon his people.

<term>king</term>, <term>virtues</term>, <term>scepter</term>, <term>eye</term>, <term>power</term>, <term>providence</term>, <term>Horapollo</term>

<summary>This page explains the symbolic meaning of a scepter topped with an eye, representing the dual royal qualities of power and watchful providence. It illustrates how ancient symbols were interpreted as moral lessons for leadership.</summary>
<keywords>kingship, scepter, providence, power, Horapollo, virtues, governance</keywords>

HIEROGLYPHICS.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular frame. It depicts an ornate table with a carved base featuring a grotesque mask or face. On top of the table lies a spindle (fusus) with thread, and a small piece of cut thread lies nearby. The table is set against a background showing a window and interior architectural details.</image-desc>

->How death or the end of a person<-
->is signified.<-

When indicating the end of a human life, they would depict a <term>spindle<note>original: *fusus*; a tool used for twisting fibers into thread</note></term> and the cut end of a thread, as if it had been torn away from the <term>distaff<note>original: *colus*; a staff used to hold unspun wool or flax</note></term>. This imagery is used because the poets imagine that the <term>Fates<note>original: *Parcæ*; the three goddesses of destiny in Roman and Greek mythology</note></term> spin the life of every person: <term>Clotho</term> carries the distaff; <term>Lachesis</term> (whose name is explained as **Lot** or **Fate**) does the spinning; and <term>Atropos</term> (which is translated into Latin as **unchanging** or **inexorable**) breaks the thread.

<note>This passage draws on the classical "Moirai" or Fates—the sisters who determined the length and nature of a mortal's life. The author connects Egyptian "hieroglyphic" symbols with these Greco-Roman mythological archetypes to explain how the end of life was visually represented.</note>

<summary>This page explains the symbolic representation of death through the tools of spinning, linking the imagery of a spindle and cut thread to the three Fates of classical mythology.</summary>

<keywords>death, Fates, spindle, thread of life, Clotho, Lachesis, Atropos, symbolism, mythology</keywords>

THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a lamp in the shape of a mythical creature or dolphin resting upon a stone pedestal or altar. ### How they signify life.

When indicating life, they would depict a burning <term>lamp<note>original: *lampada*; the lamp was a common ancient metaphor for the vital spark or soul within the vessel of the body</note></term>: for as long as a lamp is lit, it gives off light, but once it is extinguished, darkness spreads everywhere. In the same way, once the **soul** is released from the body, we are deprived of both sight and light.

<note>The author draws a direct parallel between the physical flame of a lamp and the "anima" (soul or breath). In classical thought, the presence of the soul was what allowed the body to perceive the world; its departure resulted in the "darkness" of death, mirroring the previous page's discussion on the end of life's thread.</note>

<summary>This page explains how a burning lamp was used to symbolize life, comparing the light of a flame to the presence of the soul within a living body.</summary>

<keywords>life, lamp, soul, light, darkness, vital spark, symbolism, Horapollo</keywords>

# HIEROGLYPHICS.

<margin>
In ancient Rome, houses displayed the heads of oxen, suspending clusters of berries and garlands from their horns; hence, from the head of the ox <note>This refers to the *bucranium*, a common decorative motif in Roman architecture representing sacrificial piety and prosperity.</note>
</margin>

<image-desc>A central woodcut or drawing of a bovine skull (bucranium) with large horns, set against a background of stone blocks or ruins and some sparse vegetation/foliage.</image-desc>

<margin>
Indeed, by the garlands and the berry-clusters, the fruits flowing from labors are signified. This is why Ezekiel in chapter 1, when he contemplated each [living creature] at the divine command...
</margin>

<margin>
Solomon intended to signify this when, in the [lavers] of the Temple, he placed the ox; for in leaders and prelates, [the virtue of labor] ought to shine forth. And in Ezekiel, the face of the ox shows what the divine prophet contemplated: the [endurance] and the most extreme labors to be undergone.
</margin>

->How they signify labor.<-

They would draw an ox head stripped of its flesh when they wished to represent labor in a hidden way. They used the ox head because all plowing is completed through the service of oxen; but it was stripped of flesh because hardworking men are most often lean <note>The "lean" ox head symbolizes the physical toll of hard work and the idea that true labor consumes the body's excess for the sake of productivity.</note>.

<margin>
÷ From this comes the adage: **make haste slowly** <note>original: *festina lentè*; a famous classical motto suggesting that activities should be balanced between urgency and diligence.</note>
</margin>

Wishing to signify this, the ancient philosophers painted an ox endowed with a human face with a **dolphin** upon its head. Through the dolphin, they signify speed; through the ox, slowness. For in all things, first comes counsel with deliberation, then the deed with speed, and finally a happy outcome is expected. This quality especially ought to be present in princes; for just as **Saturn**, who holds the highest place among the wandering stars <note>In the Ptolemaic system, Saturn was the furthest known planet and thus moved the slowest through the zodiac.</note>, is whirled around most quickly by the swift motion [of the heavens], yet moves most slowly by his own proper motion and according to his nature; so a prince or any <term>prelate<note>original: *prælatus*; here referring to any high-ranking official or church leader</note></term>, though he must run through all things with the most diligent care, must nevertheless be as far as possible from haste, and govern with great **prudence** and deliberation.

<summary>This page explains the ox (and specifically the ox skull or bucranium) as a symbol of labor and endurance. It also discusses the virtue of balancing speed with deliberation, using the metaphor of a dolphin and an ox, and the astrological movement of Saturn.</summary>

<keywords>labor, ox, bucranium, festina lente, Saturn, prudence, leadership, Solomon's Temple, Ezekiel, symbolism</keywords>

<header>HORAPOLLO <note>original: "Ori Apollinis"; this refers to Horapollo, the legendary author of a treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphs which was rediscovered in the 15th century.</note></header>

Clouds and horizontal lines representing the sky or sea fill the background.</image-desc>

### How they signify God.

By means of a painted eye, they understood God <note>original: "Deum"; the author uses the Latin singular to align the Egyptian concept with the Renaissance understanding of a singular divine presence.</note>, because just as the eye gazes upon whatever is placed before it, so God perceives and sees all things.

<vocab>Horapollo, God, eye, hieroglyphics</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the symbolic use of the eye to represent the omniscience of God, suggesting that just as the eye observes its surroundings, the divine sees and knows everything.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, God, Eye of Providence, omniscience, hieroglyphics, divine vision, Renaissance symbolism</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 222, which described the "All-Seeing Eye" as a symbol for the omniscience of God, this page explores the inverse: how the absence of eyes represents those who dwell in the darkness of the afterlife.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>223</page-num>
<header>HIEROGLYPHICS.</header>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a square border. It depicts a large, eyeless mask or face with an open mouth, floating in front of a cloud. Above the mask, two disembodied eyes appear within the cloud. Below, there is a landscape of rolling hills or mountains under a horizon line.</image-desc>

### How they represent the Spirits of the Dead.

They signified the Manes <note>original: "Manes"; in Roman mythology, these were the souls of deceased loved ones, often revered as "benevolent" spirits or underworld deities.</note>, that is, the gods of the Underworld <note>original: "Infernos deos"</note>, by depicting a face without eyes, and above it, a pair of eyes. By the eyes, as has already been stated, they understood the gods; however, by the face without eyes, they signified those who dwell in the Underworld and in darkness <note>original: "in tenebris"</note>.

<vocab>Spirits of the dead, Underworld gods, eyes, darkness, Hieroglyphics</vocab>

<summary>This page explains how the Egyptian hieroglyphic for the spirits of the deceased (the Manes) combines a pair of eyes representing divinity with an eyeless face representing the darkness of the tomb or the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>Manes, Underworld, spirits of the dead, afterlife, blindness, divine sight, Egyptian symbolism, Horapollo</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the work of Horus Apollo, focusing on the etymology of the author's name and the symbolic nature of Egyptian hieroglyphs.</meta>

# OBSERVATIONS ON
## Horus Apollo.

**Horus Apollo:** The Sun is called **Horus** by the Egyptians. As can be seen later in this author’s work, this is "because he rules over the seasons" <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ τοῦ τῶν ὡρῶν κρατεῖν</note>. By his motion—since he is the ruler not only of time and the earth (as Pliny writes in Book 2, Chapter 6) but even of the stars themselves and the heavens—the intervals of hours and seasons are distinguished. Consider, then, whether perhaps this author's name should more correctly be written with a "rough breathing" <note>A Greek grammatical mark indicating an "h" sound, potentially linking the name Horus more closely to the Greek word for seasons, *horai*</note>. However, this matter is of little importance.

**Hieroglyphics:** By this name are understood certain enigmatic carvings in sacred matters, and certain sacred monuments or characters and signs used among the Egyptians. For they—before the method of letters and writing was in common use, or perhaps so that sacred mysteries would not easily be profaned by the common people—designated these things not with letters, but with carved figures of objects and animals. Among their characters, one seems especially worthy of mention to us—though omitted by our author—which is that they signified "future life" with the sign of a cross, carving this on the breast of a figure of Serapis <note>A Graeco-Egyptian deity associated with healing and the afterlife</note>. And I do not know whether the Egyptians took that custom from the Hebrews (as their languages are neighbors); among whom, as Jerome <note>The 4th-century scholar and Bible translator</note> testifies in his commentary on the ninth chapter of Ezekiel, in the ancient letters (which were like those of the Samaritans at that time), the letter **taf** <note>The final letter of the Hebrew alphabet</note> bears a resemblance to a cross. But these things are perhaps secondary matters.

**How it signifies an eternity:** In the Venetian copy, the singular "it signifies" <note>original Greek: σημαίνει</note> is also read. But see whether it might more correctly be read as the plural "they signify" <note>original Greek: σημαίνουσι</note>, unless you prefer to refer "it signifies" back to the word "Hieroglyphics" itself. However, in the following sections, you will always find the plural verb: "they signify," "they show," "they manifest" <note>original: σημαίνουσιν, δηλοῦσιν, ἐμφαίνουσιν</note>, so that the subject clearly ought to refer to the Egyptians themselves. And I would read it that way more willingly because these titles placed above the chapters are not by the author himself—as is clear from the manuscript codex in which these are noted in the margin—but were added later by a diligent reader, as has happened in many other cases.

**Because of the eternal things:** *Aiōn* and *aeuum* <note>Greek and Latin terms for eternity or an age</note>—that is, "the constitution of the world" <note>original Greek: τὴν τοῦ κόσμου σύστασιν</note>.

<summary>This page features a scholarly commentary on the origins of the name Horus and the symbolic function of hieroglyphics as a secretive, sacred script. It highlights the cross symbol found on Egyptian statues and draws a parallel to the Hebrew letter 'taf,' suggesting ancient cultural exchanges.</summary>

<keywords>Horus Apollo, Hieroglyphics, Serapis, Pliny the Elder, St. Jerome, Hebrew alphabet, taf, eternity, ancient Egyptian religion, textual criticism</keywords>

# COMMENTARY ON THE ORACLES OF APOLLO
## Page 216

**According to the extension of time** <note>original Greek: κατὰ χρόνου ἔκτασιν</note>: This denotes the constitution of the world and the duration of time for as long as the world shall stand, as well as eternity; it is exactly the same as *Olam* <note>original Hebrew: HOLEM; a term meaning "eternity" or "world-age"</note> among the Hebrews. Therefore, I translate "aeon" <note>Greek: αἰῶνα</note> as "eternal things" <note>Greek: αἰῶα; likely a transcription error for αἰώνια</note>, or things producing a continuous flow of time. Furthermore, the foolishness of antiquity believed that the nature of the celestial bodies was eternal.

**Oubaion** <note>original Greek: οὐβαῖον</note>: Previously, this was read as *ouraion* <note>Greek: οὐραῖον; relating to the tail</note>, but we have restored it thus from a handwritten manuscript. This is an Egyptian word, as one may learn from our author, signifying a "basilisk." I suspect it may come from the Hebrew word *Ob* <note>original Hebrew: OB</note>, which they usually translate as a "spirit of divination" <note>The Latin here refers to a *pythonem*, a spirit or serpent of prophecy</note>. Moreover, what is related here concerning the basilisk is confirmed by Pliny, who adds that it kills other serpents with its scent, and kills a man even if it only touches him slightly; it kills not only by contact but also by its breath, which withers shrubs. On the various kinds of serpents, see the same author in Book 8, Chapter 23.

**Eating its own tail** <note>original Greek: τὴν ἑαυτῦ ἐσθίοντα οὐράν</note>: From which even the Latins call it the "turning year," because the year always rolls back into itself along its own tracks.

**Since they say at its rising it is of fifteen parts** <note>original Greek: ἐπειδὴ φασὶν ἐν τῇ ἀνατολῇ πεντεκαίδεκα μοιρῶν ὑπάρχουσαν</note>: Previously, it was read as "parts are" <note>Greek: μοίρας ὑπάρχουσι</note>. But this reading is much more correct, as it clearly teaches the course of the moon. Furthermore, by "fifteen parts of the moon," understand fifteen days. For later, where he speaks of its setting, he says "having fulfilled the number of thirty days."

**And wanting to say a year [with] a fourth [day] of disorder** <note>original Greek: βουλόμενοί τε ἔτος ἀταξí τι ταρτον λέγουσιν</note>: The year was previously so disordered by the fault of the priests—as described in Suetonius—that neither the harvest festivals matched the summer, nor the vintage festivals the autumn. Caesar, by the work of Sosigenes <note>The astronomer who helped Julius Caesar reform the Roman calendar</note>, arranged it into such an order that, since the year consists of 365 days and a quarter (that is, six hours), every fourth year one day would be intercalated in the month of February. This is why it is usually called a <term>bissextile</term> <note>A leap year; from the Latin "twice sixth," referring to the doubling of the sixth day before the Kalends of March</note>. Our own Celsus <note>A Roman legal scholar</note>, under the title "On the Meaning of Words," explains the reasoning behind this name in that chapter of the Law which begins, "When it is bissextile."

**To add** <note>original Greek: προστίθεαζ</note>: Previously, it was read as "to set before" <note>Greek: προτίθεαζ; likely a typo in the original for προτίθεσθαι</note>.

**Or victory** <note>original Greek: ἢ νίκην</note>: In the edition by Aldus <note>Aldus Manutius, the renowned Renaissance printer</note>, these words are inserted: "or Mars, or Venus" <note>Greek: ἢ ἄρεα, ἢ ἀφροδίτην</note>, the reasoning for which is handed down in the following chapters. We have also taken care to attach these. Furthermore, I preferred to translate "hawk" <note>Greek: ἱέρακα</note> as a falcon <note>Latin: accipitrem</note> rather than as an eagle, which others had translated, even though most of the things said about the nature of "hawks" <note>Greek: τῶν ἱεράκων</note> also apply to eagles. Regarding the various kinds of hawks, see Pliny, Book 10, Chapter 8.

**By the hawk-weed** <note>original Greek: τῇ ἱερακíᾳ βοτάνῃ</note>: This is called "goat's lettuce" by Pliny, and by Apuleius...

# OBSERVATIONS
## Page 227

**Wild lettuce** <note>original Latin: *lactuca syluatica*</note>. It is also called *Sonchites*. Pliny reports the same plant is called *hieracia* (hawkweed) by some (commonly called *hieraceum*), because hawks <note>original Latin: *accipitres*</note> are said to scratch it and drench their eyes with its juice to dispel any dimness they feel in their vision. For its medicinal properties, consult Pliny himself in Book 20, Chapter 7, as well as the physicians.

**But a lowering** <note>original Greek: ταπείνωσις δὲ; referring to a "depression" or "lowering" of status or spirit</note>. This was the reading in the *Aldine* edition, but we have corrected it to **a lowering** <note>original Greek: ταπείνωσιν; changing the grammatical case for consistency</note>, so that it corresponds with the preceding and following words. Additionally, an old manuscript supports our reading.

**The sympathy toward the soul** <note>original Greek: τὸ πρὸς τὴν ψυχὴν συμπαθές</note>. This was the reading in the old edition, but the true reading is found in the handwritten codex: **because of the sympathy toward the soul** <note>original Greek: διὰ τὸ πρὸς τὴν ψυχὴν συμπαθεῖν</note>, which we have restored. To philosophers and physicians, "sympathy" <note>original Greek: συμπάθεια</note> is that "consensus of nature" <note>original Latin: *naturæ consensus*</note> when we are affected or moved by the same things.

**As the soul is also nourished** <note>original Greek: ὡς καὶ ἡ ψυχὴ τρέφεται</note>. For the blood is the **vehicle of the soul** <note>original Greek: ὄχημα τῆς ψυχῆς</note>. Furthermore, in the *Aldine* edition, it reads **as** <note>original Greek: ὡς</note>, but I think it should be read as **by which** <note>original Greek: ᾧ</note>. Nevertheless, the former reading should not be rejected, as we found it in the manuscript.

**Two crows** <note>original Greek: δύο κορώνας</note>. For the nature of crows, see Pliny, Book 10, Chapter 12.

**Their concord** <note>original Greek: ἀυτῶν ὁμονοίας</note>. From this, the "concord of crows" became a celebrated proverb, concerning which there is a very elegant poem in Alciati's *Emblems*:
> The concord of life between crows is a marvel,
> and their mutual faith is never defiled. etc.

**At weddings, *ekkori kori*** <note>original Greek: ἐν τοῖς γάμοις ἐκκορὶ κορὶ</note>. We have translated these words *ekkori kori* such that *ekkori* is the word that was traditionally shouted by the Greeks during the celebration of weddings, while the author says *kori* in that name signifies a "crow" <note>original Greek: κορώνην</note>. Furthermore, one should not inquire too deeply into what else this word *ekkori* signifies, especially since the author says the Greeks use the word **without knowing its meaning** <note>original Greek: ἀγνοοῦντας</note>. Some join these two words, *ekkori kori*, and say they can be translated into English as "Adorn the crow"—meaning, adorn chastity. Among the Greeks, *ekkoro* <note>original Greek: ἐκκορῶ</note> sometimes means "I adorn," which would be *ekkorei* <note>original Greek: ἐκκόρει</note> in the imperative. I simply believe *ekkori* is a word by which the Greeks wished perpetual harmony for the couple at the wedding, though they were ignorant of the name's origin. Furthermore, in the manuscript, instead of "crow" <note>original Greek: κορώνην; in the accusative case</note>, it reads "O crow" <note>original Greek: κορώνη; in the nominative/vocative case</note>, suggesting those words were shouted at weddings: "**Be gone, be gone, O crow!**" <note>The author is debating whether the chant was an invitation to the bird of concord or a ritual to ward off the crow as a bird of solitude/bad luck</note>.

**They paint a beetle** <note>original Greek: κάνθαρον ζωγραφῶσι</note>. Regarding the scarab beetle, see Pliny, Book 11, Chapter 28. Erasmus treats this most extensively in the proverb, "The beetle seeks the eagle."

<meta>This page continues the philological and scientific commentary on a Greek text, likely the Hieroglyphics of Horapollo. The previous page discussed the medicinal properties of wild lettuce and addressed grammatical corrections regarding "sympathy" and the soul's nourishment.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>

->**IN THE COMMENTARY ON HORAPOLLO**<-

**When the male** <note>original Greek: ἐπειδὰν ὁ ἄρσεν</note>. This is how it is read in the Venetian manuscript. However, it should be read as we have restored it: **the male** <note>original Greek: ὁ ἄρσην</note>. For is there anyone who has even slightly greeted the Greek Muses <note>a metaphor for having a basic education in Greek literature</note> who does not see this?

**For it is carried from the east wind toward the southwest** <note>original Greek: αὐτὸς γὰρ ἀπὸ τοῦ ἀπηλιώτου εἰς λίβα φέρεται</note>. The old translator did not translate this passage faithfully enough, as he understood this sentence to refer to the sun—though no explicit mention of the sun was made. Both the logic of the context and the truth itself, drawn from the sources of the Astrologers, clearly demonstrate that the pronoun **it** <note>original Greek: αὐτὸς</note> must refer to **the world** <note>original Latin: mundum</note> or universe. For who among the astrologers does not know that the heaven, which they call the <term>Primum Mobile: "the first moved," the outermost sphere in the ancient model of the universe</term>, is whirled most rapidly from east to west every single day? Meanwhile, the Sun and the other stars are carried by an opposing motion from west to east. Ovid elegantly taught this in *Metamorphoses, Book 2*, with these verses:

> Add that the sky is snatched along by a constant whirling,
> And it pulls the high stars and twists them in a swift roll.
> I push against the opposing force, nor does the momentum
> That conquers all else overcome me, as I am carried in a direction contrary to the swift world.

For what has been said by this author—"from the east wind to the southwest" and "from the southwest to the east wind"—is the same as what he said shortly before: "from the rising to the setting" and likewise "from the setting to the rising." Indeed, the **East Wind** <note>original Greek: Ἀπηλιώτης / Apheliotes</note>, which is called *subsolanus* in Latin, blows from the spring sunrise, as Aristotle teaches in his *Meteorology* and Gellius in Book 2, Chapter 22. The **Southwest Wind** <note>original Greek: Λὶψ / Lips</note>, called *Africus* in Latin, blows from the winter sunset. It is opposed to the *Vulturnus* wind, which blows from the winter sunrise. See also Pliny, who discusses the nature of the winds beautifully in Book 18, Chapters 33 and 34, and Book 2, Chapters 46 and 47.

**And on the ninth and eighth** <note>original Greek: τῇ ἐνάτῃ δὲ καὶ ὀγδόῃ</note>. It is clear enough from what precedes that this reading is faulty, and **twenty-eighth** <note>original Greek: εἰκοστῇ</note> should be substituted for "eighth."

**For on this day** <note>original Greek: ταύτῃ γὰρ τὴν ἡμέραν</note>. This is also the reading in the Venetian copy. But you can mend this passage if you add a single letter: **for on this day** <note>original Greek: ταύτῃ γὰρ τῇ ἡμέρᾳ</note>. Or rather—which in my judgment fits better with what follows—**for on this day**, etc., so that the word **conjunction** <note>original Greek: συνόδου / synodou</note> is a noun. The sense would then be: For on that day, he considers there to be a conjunction of the Moon and the Sun, and moreover the "generation of the world," which is the origin and production of the things created in the world.

**But births** <note>original Greek: γενέσεις δὲ</note>. I read this as **birth** <note>original Greek: γένεσιν</note>, even though the Venetian manuscript also argues against it.

# OBSERVATIONS.

P iij

*And always... three kinds of beetles* <note>original Greek: ἀεὶ δὲ κ, κανθάρων ἰδέαι τρεῖς</note>. See Pliny, who seems to establish certain other kinds of these as well.

*But it becomes round-shaped* <note>original Greek: στρογγυλοειδὴς δὲ γίνεται</note>. It seems it should be read as *but they become round-shaped* <note>original Greek: στρογγυλοειδεῖς δὲ γίνονται</note>, unless one wishes to allow a sudden change in grammatical number <note>switching from singular to plural</note>, which is more common in Hebrew than in Greek speech. It is also read this way in the Venetian edition. But now there is no doubt that this reading must be changed based on the reliability of the handwritten manuscript.

*In Heliopolis* <note>original Greek: ἐν ἡλίϰ πόλει</note>. Heliopolis, or written separately as "City of the Sun" <note>original Greek: ἡλίου πόλις</note>, is a city in Egypt. As Diodorus Siculus <note>a Greek historian of the 1st century BCE</note> testifies, it was called by this name by the Egyptians, but by the Greeks it was called **Thebes**. This city was famous for its hundred gates. Hence Juvenal <note>a Roman satirist</note> says:

> And ancient Thebes lies buried with its hundred gates.

And Homer in the *Iliad*, Book 9:

> ...nor all the wealth that lies stored in the houses of
> Egyptian Thebes, where the greatest possessions are kept,
> Which is a city of a hundred gates, and through each one
> Two hundred men with horses and chariots go forth.

Hessus <note>Helius Eobanus Hessus, a German Latin poet</note> rendered it thus:

> Or even the ancient Egyptian walls of Thebes,
> A city famous for wealth, which a hundred thresholds
> Of gates enclose, through any one of which
> Columns of men, most beautiful in war, often lead,
> Equipped with rapid chariots and equestrian arms.

Where the interpreter of Homer, explaining "Egyptian Thebes," says it is "the city now called Diospolis." They believe this is the city which in Holy Scripture is called sometimes **On**, sometimes **Aven** (Genesis 41 & 46; also Ezekiel 30). For the city called **Beth Shemesh** <note>original Hebrew: "House of the Sun"</note> by the Hebrews, although it bears the name of the Sun, is nevertheless quite different: since, as Josephus witnesses, it is called **Scythopolis** by the Greeks. This was in the tribe of Judah, as written in Joshua 15, and there was a city of the same name in the tribes of Naphtali and Issachar, as the history in Joshua chapter 19 teaches. Although Jeremiah (chapter 43) also mentions a city of this name in Egypt. And they believe this is the one named **Ir Ha-heres** <note>original Hebrew: "City of the Sun" or "City of Destruction" (Isaiah 19)</note> in Isaiah 19.

*The second kind is the two-colored* <note>original Greek: δευτέρα δὲ γενεὰ ἡ δίκροως; likely referring to the forked or two-horned variety</note>. In a certain large kind of them, Pliny says, there are very long horns, toothed with two-forked pincers at the tip, which meet for a bite when they please, etc. Furthermore, besides those...
