<meta>This page continues a commentary on Horapollo’s Hieroglyphica, specifically focusing on the symbolic and natural history of beetles, birds, and celestial alignments as understood by the Egyptians and classical authors like Pliny the Elder.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON HORAPOLLO

Concerning the information our author <note>Referring to Horapollo, the purported author of the *Hieroglyphica*.</note> has provided in this place regarding the wondrous nature of Beetles, add also what follows from Pliny: that the beetle is born from the dead body of asses, just as wasps and hornets come from horses, and bees from bullocks, as nature transforms some things from one form into another (Book 11, Chapter 20). He also notes that a great part of Egypt worships the beetle among the gods because it possesses a certain similarity to the workings of the sun—as can also be gathered from our author Horapollo (Book 30, Chapter 11). It is even said that seeing a beetle can sharpen the eyesight (Book 29, last chapter). But this is enough on the matter. For the diligent reader will be able to investigate the rest through constant reading.

**Just as also the ibis** <note>original: "καθὰ καὶ ἶβις"</note>) The Egyptians also held this bird among their deities. Pliny is a witness to this in Book 10, Chapter 38.

**Or the celestial one** <note>original: "ἢ οὐρανίαν" (Uranian)</note>) I am not unaware that Urania is one of the nine Muses, and that this was also an epithet of Venus among various nations. However, in this passage, I interpret "the celestial one" in a new way, so that it signifies nothing more than "the heaven" <note>original: "οὐρανόν"</note>. For he mentions this further below: namely, that the Egyptians represent "the heaven" without malice and in the masculine. Therefore, he used the feminine form "celestial" <note>original: "οὐρανίαν"</note> here according to the Egyptian custom rather than the masculine "heaven" <note>original: "οὐρανὸν"</note>. You should observe, however, that the explanation of this matter is not given later in the same order in which it was proposed. For he does that in the second to last place. Consider, therefore, whether "or the celestial" would be more correctly placed after "or Juno" <note>original: "ἢ ἥραν"</note>.

**But their birth** <note>original: "ἡ δὲ γένεσις αὐτῶν"</note>) Concerning this, Pliny writes in Book 10, Chapter 6 in this manner: "No one has touched their nests. Therefore, there were even those who thought they flew here from the opposite side of the world, but this is false. For they nest in the highest cliffs."

**Drawing toward the wind** <note>original: "πρὸς βορεὰν ἄνελκον"</note>) Do not let this phrasing trouble you, as it once troubled me. For based on the manuscript, it should be read as "wind" <note>original: "ἄνεμον"</note>, just as we have restored it. You will also observe in the old copy, where a little later "doing" <note>original: "ποιοῦσα"</note> is read, it should be read as "desiring" <note>original: "ποθοῦσα"</note>, which seems to fit better with the preceding word "teeming" or "swelling with lust" <note>original: "ὀργύση" or "ὀργώση"</note>.

**Which are conceived by the wind** <note>original: "αἳ ὑπὸ ἀνέμου συλλαμβάνονται"</note>) This passage is corrupt and was read differently by the old translator than it is read by us. That translator read it with a negation: "which are *not* conceived by the wind." But we think no negation should be inserted here, but rather later where it is said, "of vultures, however, not wind-eggs," etc., so that the meaning of this passage is this: there is a certain kind of vulture that indeed conceives from the wind, but the eggs are useful only for eating, not for offspring; these are called "wind-eggs" <note>original: "ὑπηνέμια"</note> by the Greeks, or <term>subventanea</term> <note>Latin term for "wind-eggs," which are eggs produced without fertilization by a male.</note> in Latin. Regarding these vultures—

<summary>This page contains a scholarly commentary on Horapollo's Egyptian lore, using Pliny the Elder's natural history to explain the symbolism of beetles, the ibis, and the celestial heavens, while also correcting manuscript errors regarding the reproduction of vultures.</summary>
<keywords>Horapollo, Pliny the Elder, beetles, ibis, vultures, Urania, Egyptian mythology, natural history, manuscript corrections, wind-eggs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the natural history and symbolic meanings of birds in Egyptian lore, specifically moving from the ibis to the reproductive habits and symbolic life cycle of the vulture.</meta>

### OBSERVATIONS

...who use not only <term>wind-conception</term> <note>original Latin: *subuentaneo*; referring to the ancient belief that some animals could conceive through the wind without a mate</note>, and in which nothing at all is conceived from the wind, to lay eggs of this sort which are able <term>to be brought to life</term> <note>original Greek: *ζωογονεῖσθαι* (zoogoneisthai)</note>, that is, to be vivified and produce a fetus. However, if you prefer to follow the reading of the old translator, you are free to do so; but the meaning will be entirely different: namely, that there is another kind of vulture which by no means conceives from the wind, and that the eggs of these are suitable only for eating, and not for reproduction. But he argues that all those vultures which are compressed and conceive by the wind bring forth eggs most suitable for producing offspring. Furthermore, our reading and opinion seem to be confirmed by what is written by Erasmus in the proverb "She lays wind-eggs" <note>original: *ὑπηνέμια τίκτει* (hypenemia tiktei)</note>. He notes that this adage is used for light and frivolous inventions, and was transferred from birds that conceive eggs through the "imagination of Venus" <note>desire or lust</note>, but these eggs are empty and no offspring is produced from them; the Greeks called these both *hypenemia* <note>windy</note> and *zephyria* <note>produced by the west wind</note>. From this also comes the phrase in Homer’s *Iliad*, book 4: "you speak words as empty as wind" <note>original: *ἀνεμώλια βάζεις* (anemolia bazeis)</note>, meaning you speak vain and frivolous things. There, the commentator on Homer explains *anemolia* as "spoiled by the wind and like the winds, that is, vanishing." And in the books of epigrams, in that poem by Palladas:

> O wind-blown race of men, angry for your bread,
> knowing nothing until the end of life.
> <note>original: *ὦ γένος ἀνθρώπων ἀνεμώλιον ἀρτοχόλωτον / μέχρι τέλους βιότου μηδὲν ἐπιστάμενον*</note>

They are also called *anemodia* and *anemophthoreta* <note>wind-corrupted</note>. However, there is a slightly different reading in the manuscript which, truly, I do not know whether it should be accepted. For instead of "there are also other kinds of vultures" <note>original: *ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἄλλα γένη γυπῶν*</note>, it reads there "kinds of lambs" <note>original: *ἀρνέων γένη*</note>, and then the negation should not be added to the following sentence. The sense would then be that there are indeed other kinds of birds <note>referring to the "lambs" variant</note> which conceive from the wind, but whose eggs are suitable only for eating, not for reproduction. But the eggs of vultures, even if they conceive from the wind, are capable of generating life.

<margin>Seven days before <note>original: *πρὸ ἡμερῶν ἑπτά*</note></margin> Pliny writes that vultures fly to where corpses will be three or two days in advance. From this comes the proverbial saying: "If you are a vulture, wait for the corpse." See Erasmus on the proverb *The shadow of a vulture*.

<margin>For she remains pregnant for one hundred and twenty days. <note>original: *ἑκατὸν γὰρ εἴκοσιν ἡμέρας ἔγγυος μένει*</note></margin> If you compare our version of this passage with the old translator, you will find that he either read this place differently or did not understand it at all. For while the Greek context says the vulture distributes the three hundred and sixty-five days of the year into three parts—that is, into three sets of one hundred and twenty days—it says she remains <term>pregnant</term> <note>original: *ἔγγυος* or *ἔγκυος* (enkyos)</note> for one hundred and twenty days, nourishes the chicks for just as many, and cares for herself for the same number; in the remaining five days, she conceives from the wind. The old translator, however, says one hundred and twenty...

<meta>catchword: ...remaining (reliquis)</meta>

The author compares various classical sources, including Homer, Pliny, and Erasmus, to explain the vulture's symbolic life cycle and linguistic associations with the wind.</summary>

->ON HORAPOLLO<-

...assigns days to the first two parts, and finally two hundred and forty, by which means the 360 days of the year are completed, though they are not distributed in this way in the Greek context. Furthermore, for <term>terrestrial</term> <note>original Greek: *ἔγγαιος* (engaios)</note> I read **pregnant** <note>original Greek: *ἔγκυος* (enkyos)</note>, which seems to me more consistent with the nature of the vulture, and indeed was read this way in the manuscript. Truly, this is worthy of notice: where it is read in the Greek context, **for the remaining hundred**, a smaller number—**twenty** <note>original Greek: *εἴκοσι* (eikosi)</note>—is missing, which is both often omitted and is left out below where he says, **in the hundred days in which she rears her own young**. But in this passage, so that the number of days in the year might be absolute in every part, I have judged that it must necessarily be added. Otherwise, in the manuscript it is read in both places as **one hundred and twenty**, which is how we have restored it.

**which is the work of a female**) This ought to be referred to the word itself, **origin** <note>original Greek: *γένεσις* (genesis)</note>, which is a feminine noun.

**I reveal the word.** <margin>the vultures</margin> The verb **they show** <note>original Greek: *δηλοῦσιν* (delousin)</note> or something similar is missing in this gap; the old translator also noticed this.

**for she is the mother of a female animal**) It can be understood as we have translated: that the mother is of the female sex. It can also be taken regarding the vulture, meaning she is the mother of a female offspring. For a vulture—which is itself always female <note>referring to the ancient belief that all vultures were female and conceived by the wind</note>—never procreates anything from itself except a female.

**and two drachmae**) For the sake of <term>consistency</term> <note>original Greek: *συνέπειαν* (synepeian)</note> with the preceding words, I read **and two drachmae** <note>changing the grammatical case from nominative *drachmai* to accusative *drachmas* to fit the sentence structure</note>.

**nor to males**) Something seems to be missing here. However, a sense can be drawn out, even if some supply the passage thus: **nor are males necessary**. But let us follow the reading of the manuscript copy, where it is read as follows: **from both a male and female existing**.

**hermaphrodites**) <note>original Greek: *ἀρσενοθήλεας* (arsenotheleas); literally "male-females"</note> This is well enough known from the fables of the poets, who establish the god **Vulcan** as soft and effeminate, and **Pallas** <note>Athena</note> as a virile and warlike goddess.

**and fate**) I read **fate** <note>original Greek: *εἱμαρμένην* (heimarmenen)</note> in the accusative case, so that it responds to the preceding and following words.

**only five of them moving**) Why not rather all seven planets? Unless perhaps from this number they wished to exclude **Saturn** and **Mars** as hostile and heavy stars.

**they depict a dog-headed baboon**) Regarding the <term>cynocephalus</term> <note>literally "dog-head"; a species of baboon sacred to Thoth in Egypt</note>, see Pliny, book 8, chapter 54. From which it may be gathered that the animal is a Cynocephalus, not far removed from a human...

## OBSERVATIONS

...[similar to] a monster, and to be counted as such, just as satyrs, sphinxes, and similar types of portents celebrated among writers are regarded.

**seventy-two regions**) <note>original Greek: ἑβδομήκοντα δύο χώρας</note> These they call **climates** <note>lat: *climata*; these are zones of the earth defined by their latitude</note>, based on the inclination and elevation of the celestial pole.

**and obtaining care**) <note>original Greek: καὶ ἐπιμελείας τυγχάνοντες</note> This passage is otherwise corrupt regarding those two words, **obtaining** <note>original Greek: τυγχάνοντες</note> and **these** <note>original Greek: τούτοις</note>. You should emend it, as the very structure of the sentence suggests, reading **obtaining** <note>original Greek: τυγχάνοντας</note> and **these** <note>original Greek: τούτους</note> in the accusative case.

**dying every day**) <note>original Greek: καθ’ ἑκάστην ἡμέραν νεσόμενον</note> We were previously in doubt as to how this passage should be restored, and we substituted **being eaten** <note>original Greek: ἐσθιόμενον</note>, but the passage will now be clearly restored if you read **dying** <note>original Greek: νεκρούμενον</note> from the old manuscript.

**and a reed pen**) <note>original Greek: καὶ σχοινίον</note> By this name he refers to a writing stylus fashioned from rushes; for a **schoinos** <note>original Greek: σχοῖνος</note> is a rush or reed.

**and he records**) <note>original Greek: καὶ ἐγγράφει</note> This can refer either to the **cynocephalus** <note>a dog-headed baboon, sacred to Thoth/Hermes</note>, as we have translated it, or to the priest. The sense would be that when the priest perceives a cynocephalus of that kind—one that is naturally inclined toward letters—he enters it among the sacred animals and initiates it into the sacred order. For the verb **to record** <note>original Greek: ἐγγράφειν</note> often carries that meaning of "enrolling" or "registering."

**it was entrusted to Hermes, who participates in all letters**) <note>original Greek: ἐπὶ ἑρμῇ ἐνεμύθη τῷ πάντων μετέχοντι γραμμάτων</note> For Mercury <note>the Greek Hermes</note> is the presiding deity of speech and eloquence. Plato even suggests that he was called *Hermes* as if he were **the speaker** <note>original Greek: εἰρέμην</note>—that is, the god who devised the method of speaking. Thus, among the Greeks, there was once a **Mercury of the Marketplace** <note>original Greek: ἑρμῆς ἀγοραῖος</note> in the public squares.

**but not even if he feeds on fish is he idle**) <note>original Greek: ἀλλ’ οὐδὲ ἰχθυώμενος ἀργὸν</note> I emend it thus, or rather to **idle** <note>original Greek: ἀργὸς</note>, meaning that even if he is nourished by fish (which is otherwise contrary to his nature), he is not for that reason lazy or given over to idleness and bodily ease, like foreign priests. For the most eminent authors have recorded that the Egyptian priests cultivated the study of wisdom and the finest arts with great diligence—among whom is Aristotle, near the beginning of his **Metaphysics** <note>original Greek: τῶν μετὰ τὰ φυσικά</note>. In the old manuscript, it is read as **feeding on fish, being idle** <note>original Greek: ἰχθυώμενον ἀργὸν</note>, as if derived from the verb *ichthyao* ("to fish").

**gaps**) <note>original Greek: χήματα (chēmata)</note> This word is also written in the Venice copy, but it stands further apart from the rest, which shows that something is missing in this place. However, in the manuscript, it is omitted entirely.

**how two equinoxes**) <note>original Greek: πῶς ἰσημερίας δύο</note> The Greeks took account of the days, while the Latins took account of the nights. From this, it happened that what the Greeks called **equal-days** <note>original Greek: ἰσημερίας (isēmerias)</note>, the Latins called **equinoxes** <note>equal-nights</note>, although the Greek word literally sounds like "equidia" <note>equal days</note>. These occur when the Sun...

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Horapollo’s Hieroglyphica. The previous page discussed the symbolic behavior of the cynocephalus (dog-headed baboon) and the linguistic roots of Egyptian terms, specifically regarding the sacred scribe and the reed pen.</meta>

# ON THE WRITINGS OF HORAPOLLO

...passing through the circle they call the equator, under the signs of Aries and Libra. For twice in a year the nights are equal to the days: in the spring during the month of March, and in the autumn during the month of September.

**twelve times a day, it urines every hour**) <note>original Greek: δωδεκάκις τῆς ἡμέρας καθ' ἑκάστην ὥραν οὐρεῖ</note> And from this, the origin of the twelve hours is believed to have come. For at a certain time, Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Egyptian god Thoth</note>, while he was in Egypt, noticed a certain sacred animal dedicated to Serapis <note>a Greco-Egyptian deity</note>—the **cynocephalus**—which urinated twelve times throughout the day at always equal intervals. From this, he deduced that the day should be divided into twelve hours, and since then this number of hours has been maintained. Others say the twelve hours were named from the "squaring of the heavens," which according to musical theory is said to be divided into twelve parts. Our ancestors caught water flowing through a very thin opening <note>a clepsydra or water-clock</note> for a whole day and divided this into twelve parts; from these parts they gave names to the hours. The distinguished rhetorician Victorinus mentions this in those brilliant commentaries he published on Cicero's *Rhetoric*. It seemed appropriate to transfer these words here verbatim because they help in understanding what follows. By "sacred animal," he means our **cynocephalus** <note>a baboon often depicted in Egyptian art associated with timekeeping</note>.

**so that there might not be a wide opening**) <note>original Greek: ἵνα δὲ μὴ εὐρύτορον</note> This passage is corrupt and should be restored, in my judgment, by deleting the word **water** <note>original Greek: τὸ ὕδωρ</note> which you find in the first instance, and reading the singular **structure** <note>original Greek: κατασκεύασμα</note> instead of the plural **structures** <note>original Greek: κατασκευάσματα</note>, like so: *so that there might not be a wide-passaged structure through which the water...* and so on. Furthermore, where we read **showing through** <note>original Greek: διαφάντες</note>, in the old manuscript I suspect it is more correctly read as **piercing through** <note>original Greek: διέραντες</note>, as if passing through with a pin or buckle.

**for it has a large head**) <note>original Greek: κεφαλὴν γὰρ ἔχει μεγάλην</note> The lion has a very large and very strong head, if we believe Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist</note>, Book 8, Chapter 16. Moreover, he speaks extensively on the nature of lions in that same book and chapter.

**and when sleeping, it has these eyes open**) <note>original Greek: κοιμώμενος δὲ ἀνεῳγότας τούτους ἔχει</note> It sleeps with open eyes, which the Greeks call by the single word **korybantian** <note>original Greek: κορυβαντιᾶν; to sleep like a Corybant, or with eyes half-open</note>, as Pliny also testifies in Book 11, Chapter 37.

**in Egyptian, Neilo; when translated, it means "new"**) <note>original Greek: αἰγυπτιστὶ νειλῶ, ἑρμηνευθὲν δὲ σημαίνει νέον</note> And according to Servius <note>a 4th-century grammarian</note>, the Greeks also use the name **Neilos** <note>original Greek: Νεῖλος</note> because it **brings new silt** <note>original Greek: παρὰ τὸ νέαν ἰλὺν ἕλκειν</note>, since it carries new mud and sediment with it.

**three water-jars**) <note>original Greek: τρεῖς ὑδρίας</note> Here he uses **hydria** <note>original Greek: ὑδρίαν</note> in the feminine gender, and elsewhere **hydreion** <note>original Greek: ὑδρεῖον</note> in the neuter. Both names denote a water vessel.

**the sun being in Leo**) <note>original Greek: ὁ ἥλιος εἰς λέοντα γενόμενος</note> Pliny testifies to the same in Book 5, Chapter 18, with these words: *The Nile, there acting in the place of a farmer, begins to overflow, so that...*

<summary>This page explains the Egyptian origins of the twelve-hour day through the observation of the cynocephalus baboon and provides philological corrections to Greek descriptions of lions and the Nile's name.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological notes on the Hieroglyphica of Horapollo. The previous page discussed the symbolic role of the dog-headed baboon (cynocephalus) in Egyptian timekeeping and its biological or mythological associations.</meta>

# OBSERVATIONS

...closest to the summer solstice or the new moon, and at first slowly, then more violently as long as the sun is in the sign of Leo. Soon it slows down, having crossed into Virgo, and then it settles in Libra, and so forth.

**the water-gutters**) <note>original Greek: τὰς χολέδρας</note> I would more gladly read <term>clepsydras<note>water-clocks used in antiquity to measure time by the flow of water</note></term>, or certainly, with a slight change to the text, *chohydras*: by which name water channels are suggested, <note>original Greek: παρὰ τὸ ἐν αὐτοῖς ὡσανεὶ χολᾶν τὸ ὕδωρ</note> derived from the idea that in them the water seems to sprout and bubble forth.

**according to a prayer for an abundance of moisture**) <note>original Greek: κατ’ εὐχὴν πλεονασμοῦ ὑγρότητος</note> This passage can be completed if you add "they use the same sign." They use the same sign—I mean Leo, the Lion—when they offer prayers because of an immoderate flooding of the Nile. For the Nile overflows beyond measure if it exceeds 16 cubits. As Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman naturalist</note> says in that same work, if the water does not exceed 12 cubits, famine is certain, and it is no less certain if it exceeds 16.

**the one making a likeness**) <note>original Greek: τὸ ὁμοιοῦντες</note> I think it should be read as "likening the young one" <note>original Greek: τὸ νέον ὁμοιοῦντες</note>. For he said earlier that this was the name for the Nile among the Egyptians, unless you prefer to read it as "the Nile" <note>original Greek: τὸν νεῖλον</note>. For the things that follow are applied to the Nile. But in the ancient version, it is read as *the Mydreion*.

**and to be the mother**) <note>original Greek: γενέτειραν <unclear>τ’</unclear> εἶναι</note> Perhaps because in this [the tongue] is the sense of taste, without which Aristotle <note>the Greek philosopher</note> claims no animal can exist; yet Pliny writes in Book 11, Chapter 37, that the sea turtle lacks a tongue.

**thirdly, by the rains**) <note>original Greek: τρίτον δὲ ὑπὸ τῶν ὄμβρων</note> See the writings of Pomponius Mela <note>a Roman geographer</note> on this matter.

**donkey-headed**) <note>original Greek: ὀνοκέφαλον</note> A certain kind of animal, named for its donkey-like head. Others explain it simply as the head of a donkey.

**how it is a protective charm**) <note>original Greek: πῶς φυλακτήριον</note> The Latins call this an *amuletum* <note>amulet</note>, which refers to anything effective for warding off poison and danger; the Greeks also call it an <term>alexipharmakon<note>literally "warding off poison," a protective charm or antidote</note></term>.

**and without letters**) <note>original Greek: καὶ χωρὶς γραμμάτων</note> Almost all these sacred Egyptian symbols and engravings are of this kind <note>meaning they communicate meaning through images rather than an alphabet</note>.

**an unformed human**) <note>original Greek: ἄπλασον δὲ ἄνθρωπον</note> They call a fetus that is still imperfect and still in the womb by the single name <term>embryo<note>original Greek: ἔμβρυον</note></term>; a commentator on Homer’s *Iliad* (Book 23) thinks he calls this a *brephos* <note>a babe or newborn</note>.

**with its other part like a frog**) <note>original Greek: τῷ μ’ ἑτέρῳ μέρει αὐτ’ βατράχῳ</note> Ovid elegantly declares this in the first book of his *Metamorphoses*:
->...and in the same body often
one part lives, while the other part is crude earth.<-
<note>Ovid is describing creatures spontaneously generating from the mud of the Nile</note>

**the upper parts of this by the eyes**) <note>original Greek: τὰ δ’ ὑπερᾶα δὲ ταύτης τοῖς ὀφθαλμοῖς</note> And not without reason: since in no part, as Pliny writes in Book 11, Chapter 37, are there greater indications of the soul, especially in man. Now, voices are <note>original Greek: τῶν ἐν τῇ ψυχῇ πα-</note> <note>The text breaks off, likely referring to the "passions of the soul"</note>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the Hieroglyphica of Horapollo. Following the discussion on the Nilotic measurements and the symbolism of the lion, the author now turns to the duration of the year, the lifecycle of the Phoenix, and the anatomy of the ibis.</meta>

# ON THE ORATION OF HORAPOLLO

**symbols of the gods**) <note>original Greek: θεῶν σύμβολα</note> as Aristotle writes in the book *On Interpretation* <note>original Latin: Aristot. in lib. περὶ ἑρμηνείας</note>.

**of three hundred and sixty-five days of the year being**) <note>original Greek: ἐκ τριακοσίων ἑξηκονταπέντε ἡμερῶν τ' ἔτους ὑπάρχοντος</note> The year is composed of 365 natural days (as stated previously), with a <term>quadrant<note>a quarter of a day</note></term>, which is to say six hours. These hours, collected every year, constitute a full day in the fourth year, as Ovid gracefully expressed in that <term>distich<note>a pair of poetic lines</note></term> in the third book of his *Fasti*:

> He joins three hundred and five with ten times six days,
> And adds the fourth period from a full day.
<note>original Latin: Is decies senos tercentum & quinque diebus / Iungit, & è pleno tempora quarta die.</note>

**it is signified as being hindered**) <note>original Greek: σημαίνεται ὡς παραπεποδισμένον</note> This is an easy reading from the manuscript. For what was previously read as "as it followed remaining" <note>original Greek: ὡς παρείπετο διαμένον</note> could be understood only with difficulty. It was an easy mistake for an ignorant or drowsy scribe to turn "hindered" <note>original Greek: παραπεποδισμένον</note> into "followed remaining."

**the sixteen [years], pleasure**) <note>original Greek: τὰ δέκα ἕξ ἡδονὴν</note> Previously this was read as "the children from pleasures" <note>original Greek: τὰ τέκνα ἐκ ἡδονῶν</note>; however, since this did not seem to have been mentioned before, while it was indeed stated that the sixteenth year is the beginning of the intimacy and delight of men with women, we preferred to restore this reading from the ancient source.

**for to mount all things**) <note>original Greek: πάντων γὰρ ἐπιβαίνειν</note> I read this as "he mounts" <note>original Greek: ἐπιβαίνει</note>.

**he will be named great**) <note>original Greek: πολὺς ὀνομασθήσεται</note> This was also the reading in the Venetian manuscript, but who does not see that it should be read as "he shall be named"? You will note here that the future tense is used for the past, perhaps in the Hebrew fashion, just as below "the word will be given" <note>original Greek: ὁ λόγος ἀποδοθήσεται</note>, unless you prefer to substitute the <term>aorist<note>a Greek past tense used for completed actions</note></term> forms "was named" and "was given." Furthermore, in this place between "great" and "he will be named," a certain space is left in the manuscript, as if a word is missing.

**through five hundred years**) <note>original Greek: διὰ πεντακοσίων ἐτῶν</note> Pliny <note>the Roman naturalist Pliny the Elder</note> reports in Book 10, Chapter 2, that the Phoenix lives for six hundred and sixty years, where he describes its truly wondrous birth. He says that as it grows old, it builds a nest from twigs of cassia and frankincense, fills it with perfumes, and dies upon it. From its bones and marrow, there is first born a sort of small worm, which then becomes a chick; at the beginning of its life, it pays the funeral rites to its predecessor and carries the entire nest to the City of the Sun <note>Heliopolis</note> near Panchaia, and places it there upon the altar.

**to rejoice in the sun by the Egyptians**) <note>original Greek: ἡλίῳ χαίρειν ὑπ' αἰγυπτίων</note> I would more gladly read "among the Egyptians" <note>original Greek: ἐπ' αἰγυπτίων</note>. Pliny also writes in the same place that the bird is sacred to the Sun in Arabia.

**the ibis is itself in its very self similar to a heart**) <note>original Greek: ἡ ἶβις αὐτὸ καθ' αὑτὸ τῇ καρδίᾳ ἐστὶν ἐμφερής</note> This means that its heart is so large that nearly the whole bird seems to be nothing else but a heart. Instead of "similar" <note>original Greek: ἐμφερής</note>, the manuscript reads "indivisible" <note>original Greek: ἀμερὴς</note>, as if the heart of the ibis cannot be divided, which does not please me as much.

<keywords>Horapollo, Phoenix, Ibis, Leap Year, Ovid, Pliny the Elder, Aristotle, Calendars, Egyptian Mythology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Horapollo's Hieroglyphica. The author is transitioning from the symbolic meaning of numbers and years to specific textual corrections and cultural parallels found in Roman authors like Juvenal and Pliny the Elder.</meta>

# OBSERVATIONS

***or an end***) <note>original Greek: ἢ πέρας</note> Some explain this as a line or a point, but I prefer to translate it as "end," since the reasoning that is added afterward fits this sense best.

***everyone who has food will learn letters, and the rest***) <note>original Greek: πᾶς ὁ ἔχων τὴν τροφὴν, μαθήσεται τὰ γράμματα, καὶ τὰ λοιπὰ</note> Whence Juvenal writes:
> They do not easily rise, whose virtues are hindered
> by narrow circumstances at home.
<note>original Latin: Haud facilè emergunt, quorum virtutibus obstat / Res angusta domi. From Juvenal's Satires, suggesting that poverty prevents talent from being recognized.</note>

And that emblem of Alciato:
> The right hand holds a stone, the other hand supports wings.
> As the feather lifts me, etc.
<note>original Latin: Dextra tenet lapidem, manus altera sustinet alas. / Vt me pluma leuat, &c. This refers to Andrea Alciato's famous emblem book; the image symbolizes genius ("wings") being held back by poverty ("the stone").</note>

***ambres***) <note>original Greek: ἀμβρὴς</note> This is an Egyptian name, as far as can be conjectured.

***having learned letters into a harbor***) <note>original Greek: μαθὼν γράμματα εἰς ὅρμον</note> For according to the proverbial <term>senarius<note>a line of verse consisting of six feet</note></term>: "Those who have learned letters see double." And: "Education is the staff of life." And in the *Book of Epigrams*:
> Virtue is something stable and unchanging, on which alone
> life courageously sails across the waves.
<note>original Greek: ἡ δ' ἀρετὴ σταθερόν τι καὶ ἄτροπον, ἧς ἐπὶ μόνης / κύματα θαρσαλέως ποντοπορεῖ βιότος.</note>

***they place beside the dog also a royal robe lying nearby or a naked word***) <note>original Greek: προστιθέασι τῷ κυνὶ καὶ βασιλικὴν στολὴν παρακειμένην ἢ ῥῆμα γυμνόν</note> Nor were there lacking those who—according to Pliny, Book 6, Chapter 30—held a dog as a king, divining its commands from its movements. Moreover, where it reads "or a naked word" <note>original Greek: ἢ ῥῆμα γυμνὸν</note>, I would prefer to read it in the <term>dative case<note>the grammatical case used to indicate the indirect object</note></term>, "with a naked word" <note>original Greek: ῥήματι γυμνῷ</note>, unless you prefer to say that this <term>accusative<note>the case used for the direct object</note></term> depends on the participle "lying nearby" <note>original Greek: παρακειμένην</note> by the force of the preposition, as often happens. Or certainly, punctuate after "lying nearby," so that "a naked figure" <note>original Greek: σχῆμα γυμνὸν</note> may be understood by itself.

***how they show ignorance***) <note>original Greek: πῶς δηλοῦσιν ἀγνοίαν</note> I wonder that the ancient translator did not notice that "purity" <note>original Greek: ἁγνείαν</note> should be read, which is also how it appears in the manuscript; it should not be "ignorance" <note>original Greek: ἄγνοιαν</note> as he translated it, which is not consistent with the reasoning that follows. This was perhaps the reason that drove him to translate "corruption" for the word <term>katharmos<note>original Greek: καθαρμὸς</note></term>, which actually signifies purification and lustration.

***they paint a fish***) <note>original Greek: ἰχθυὸν ζωγραφῶσι</note> Instead of "of fishes" <note>original Greek: ἰχθυῶν</note>, although the Venetian copy opposes it, I read "a fish" <note>original Greek: ἰχθῦ</note>. The old <term>codex<note>an ancient manuscript in book form</note></term> also confirms this reading.

***for every fish is a doer of evil***) <note>original Greek: κακοποιὸν γὰρ ἰχθῦς πᾶς</note> There are some among fishes who devour the hook, or bite and rob the bait. On the craftiness of fish generally, see Pliny in various places in Book 9.

***and eating one another***) <note>original Greek: καὶ ἀλληλοφάγον</note> Regarding the hostility of fish among themselves, see the same [Pliny], Book 9, final chapter.

***once into the female nature according to its own...***) <note>original Greek: ἅπαξ εἰς θήλειαν φύσιν καθὰς τὸ ἑαυτ</note> In bulls...

<summary>This page contains philological notes on Horapollo’s text, correcting errors made by previous translators regarding Greek terms like "purity" vs "ignorance." The author uses classical citations from Juvenal, Pliny, and Alciato to explain the cultural context of Egyptian symbols.</summary>

--- Page 282 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Horapollo's Hieroglyphica. The author is moving through specific animal symbols, comparing Horapollo’s descriptions with the natural histories of Roman authors like Pliny the Elder to correct potential errors in the Greek manuscript.</meta>

### ON THE ORATIONS OF HORAPOLLO

...that conception is completed in a single encounter, as Pliny is the authority in Book 8, Chapter 45. If by chance it misses, the female seeks the male again after 20 days. The same author testifies that these animals mate no more than twice a day.

***they depict an oryx*** <note>original Greek: ὄρυγα ζωγραφοῦσι</note>
Pliny records that the *oryx* is a unicorn and cloven-hoofed animal in Book 11, Chapter 46. Some say it is covered with hair growing in the opposite direction, turned toward the head (Book 8, Chapter 53). Concerning the same animal, while explaining the power of the dog star in Book 2, he writes: "Egypt calls a wild beast the *oryx*, which he reports stands against the star at its rising and watches it, and as if adoring it, when it sneezes." However, from a manuscript codex, we have restored the reading **quail** <note>original Greek: ὄρτυγα; Latin: coturnicem</note> instead of "oryx," because he attributes wings to this creature. Nevertheless, it seems to cause some difficulty that he mentions "front legs," unless you understand the front part of the legs or feet in a quail. It also causes trouble that he says ancient kings sat upon this creature, and that he mentions lips.

***he paints the pupils for himself*** <note>original Greek: ζωγραφεῖ ἑαυτῷ τὰς κόρας</note>
He depicts the **pupils of the eyes** <note>original Latin: Pupillas oculorum</note> in this way, and dyes them with such a color, that he is seen with the appearance of one who is indignant or sad; or certainly, fixing his eyes on the ground, he seems to paint them by pressing them into the dust.

***as if of certain gnomons*** <note>original Greek: ὡς τινων γνωμόνων</note>
A <term>gnomon<note>the part of a sundial that casts a shadow</note></term> is usually translated as a "square" or "rule" by Valla; it is the stylus from whose shadow the indication of the hours is taken.

***as if the only one of the birds*** <note>original Greek: οἷον μόνον τῶν πτηνῶν</note>
This could not be applied to the *oryx*; therefore an old copy conveniently appeared, which removed this whole scrap of doubt for us. Furthermore, previously after "since" <note>original Greek: ἐπειδὴ</note> one word was missing, which we inserted into the context from the old manuscript: **contention** <note>original Greek: ἀντιδικίαν</note>.

***the mouse having chosen the purest of them*** <note>original Greek: ὁ μῦς τὸν καθαρώτατον αὐτῶν ἐκλεξάμενος</note>
Pliny records the same thing in Book 8, Chapter 37 regarding Pontic mice, whose palate, he says, is most discerning in taste; I wonder how the authors understood how they knew this.

***they depict an ant*** <note>original Greek: μύρμηκα ζωγραφοῦσιν</note>
Regarding the wonderful industry and natural talent of ants, see Pliny, Book 11, Chapter 30.

***an Egyptian goose*** <note>original Greek: χηναλώπεκα</note>
The *chenalopex* (which you might call "fox-goose" in English), Pliny counts among the genus of geese in Book 10, Chapter 22. What was read here as "to join" <note>original Greek: συμβάλλειν</note> has been correctly changed from an old manuscript to **to revere** <note>original Greek: σεβάζειν</note>.

***writing "gratitude," they depict...*** <note>original Greek: εὐχαρισίαν γράφοντες, κεκυφαὶ ζωγραφοῦσι</note>
It is clear enough that this name <note>the term "kekuphai"</note> is foreign and barbarous. However, I believe this is the bird that the Greeks call a *pelargos* and the Latins a **stork** <note>Latin: ciconia</note>. Storks, says Pliny in Book 10, Chapter 22, return to the same nests; the mother...

<summary>This page features a philological analysis of Horapollo's work, using Pliny the Elder's Natural History to identify and correct animal names such as the oryx, quail, and stork in the context of Egyptian symbolism. The author focuses on resolving manuscript discrepancies regarding animal anatomy and behavior.</summary>

--- Page 283 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Horapollo's Hieroglyphica, focusing on animal symbolism and the moral lessons derived from them. The previous page discussed the oryx (or quail) and the symbolism of eyes; this page transitions into the stork and hippopotamus as symbols of piety and injustice.</meta>

# OBSERVATIONS

...since they nurture old age in turn. From this comes the word *antipelargein* <note>original Greek: ἀντιπελαργεῖν; literally "to stork-act back"</note>, used for returning a favor to those who deserve it in a timely manner. Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia.</note> explains the whole reasoning for this matter carefully in these words, which I have decided to write down here because through them the natures of both the stork and the hippopotamus (which is discussed in the next chapter) are clearly and elegantly expressed: "To return stork-care to the aged <note>original Greek: Ἀντιπελαργεῖν τὴν γραῦν</note> means to return favors. For it is said that storks feed their aged parents. Aristotle says the story about storks is true. He says the heavy-footed animals <note>original Greek: ἀδρόποδας; likely referring to hippopotamuses in this context</note> do similarly to them. Therefore, on scepters they shape a stork at the top, but a hippopotamus below, showing that violence is subject to righteous action. For storks, being practitioners of justice, carry their aged parents on their wings; but hippopotamuses are the most unjust of animals." There is an emblem by Alciatus <note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), a legal scholar famous for his "Emblemata," which paired images with moralizing Latin verses.</note> on this subject:

->The stork, distinguished by its piety in its airy nest, etc.<-

<margin>claws of the hippopotamus <note>original Greek: ἱπποποτάμου ὄνυχας</note></margin> Pliny describes the hippopotamus in Book 8, Chapter 25.

<margin>they paint a city to see <note>original Greek: πόλις ὁρᾶν ζωγραφουσι</note></margin> Concerning doves, Pliny, Book 10, Chapter 34.

<margin>and the worst king <note>original Greek: βασιλέα δὲ κάκισον</note></margin> Before I came across the Venetian copy, because "worst" <note>original: κάκισον</note> was read in our copy, I believed it should read "most powerful" <note>original: κράτισον</note>. This reading certainly fits best with what follows: "alluding in writing to the king of the world ruling."

<margin>beside him in the world <note>original Greek: παρ’ αὐτῷ ἐν τῷ κόσμῳ</note></margin> I think this gap <note>a "lacuna" or missing piece of text in the manuscript</note> should be filled by inserting the word "is governed" <note>original Greek: κυβερνᾶται</note> or "exists," so it is understood that the world is ruled by him no differently than a royal house, which is contained within the circuit of the world.

<margin>from that of honey, from that of the sting <note>original Greek: ἐκ τῆς τοῦ μέλιτος ἐκ τῆς τοῦ κέντρου</note></margin> I believe the gaps in this sentence should be filled so that you read: "from the sweetness of the honey and from the power of the animal's sting, a king should be both kind and vigorous for administration."

<margin>they paint a crocodile <note>original Greek: κροκόδειλον ζωγραφουσι</note></margin> Regarding which, see Pliny, Book 8, Chapter 25.

<margin>self-bearing and downward-tending <note>original Greek: αὐτότοκον καὶ κατωφερὲς</note></margin> "Self-bearing" <note>original: αὐτότοκον</note> means giving birth by itself and easily. For a little before he said it was "prolific." I have explained "downward-tending" <note>original: κατωφερὲς</note> as being "prone to lust." This name also signifies being bent down toward the ground and looking downward.

<summary>This page analyzes the symbolic contrast between the stork (representing filial piety and justice) and the hippopotamus (representing injustice and violence), while also providing philological corrections to the manuscript's descriptions of kingship and crocodiles.</summary>
<keywords>stork, hippopotamus, piety, justice, Suidas, Aristotle, Pliny the Elder, Alciatus, emblems, philology, kingship, crocodile</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the Hieroglyphica, shifting from the symbolism of the stork and hippopotamus to a series of specific observations on the text of Book II. The author, likely Jean Mercier or a contemporary humanist, provides textual emendations and classical references for various Egyptian symbols.</meta>

<header>OBSERVATIONS</header>
<meta>Catchword: ὡς (as)</meta>

# ON THE SE-
### cond Book.

"And always a chick" <note>original Greek: Καὶ ἀεὶ νεοσσὸν</note>. I read this as "chick" <note>original Greek: νεοσσὸς; changing the case from the original transcription</note>, and where in the Venetian edition a little later "circularly" <note>original Greek: κυκλοειδὸν</note> was read, I read it as "in a circular fashion" <note>original Greek: κυκλοειδῶς</note>. Furthermore, I believe the beginning of this book is damaged <note>meaning the manuscript source was incomplete</note>, primarily because while the author very diligently provided the reasoning for the other symbols, he has remained silent on the reasoning for these, up until that chapter titled "What they signify when they draw a swallow" <note>original Greek: τί δηλοῦσι, χελιδόνα γράφοντες</note>. Unless perhaps the author himself decided the reasons for all these should be omitted, either because they were entirely unknown, or—as is more likely—because they were common knowledge among the Egyptians.

"Two feet joined together" <note>original Greek: δύο πόδες συνηγμένοι</note>. I read this as either "brought together," "joined," or if you prefer, "gathered" <note>original Greek variants: συνηγμένοι, συνημμένοι, συναγμένοι</note>. Indeed, once the Sun has progressed to the furthest point of Cancer or Capricorn, it seems to remain fixed there, because it no longer moves further south or north, but begins either to move away from us or to draw closer to us again. The Greeks, considering the "turning" of the sun, called these the "winter and summer turnings" <note>original Greek: τροπὰς χειμερινὰς καὶ θερινὰς</note>, which the Latins called the winter and summer "solstices" <note>original Latin: hyberna æstiuáque solstitia</note>, a name taken from the Sun's standing still <note>Latin: *sol* (sun) + *sistere* (to stand)</note>, because it seems to stand there.

<margin><image-desc>A small hand with a pointing finger (manicule) pointing towards the text.</image-desc></margin> "Genitals held by a hand" <note>original Greek: αἰδοῖον χειρὶ κρατούμενον</note>. Regarding this, we read that there was once a certain philosopher who, in his sleep, would move one hand to his mouth and the other to his genitals, implying that these are the two parts of a man that require moderation and are the most difficult to restrain.

"Anemone flowers" <note>original Greek: ἄνθη δὲ ἀνεμώνης</note>. Regarding the anemone as a type of poppy, see Pliny's *Natural History*, Book 22, Chapter 23, and Erasmus in his proverb "You are comparing a rose to an anemone" <note>original Greek: ῥοδὸν ἀνεμώνῃ συγκρίνεις; a phrase used when comparing something beautiful or valuable to something inferior</note>. See also the medical writers, especially Dioscorides, Book 2, Chapter 169.

"A pregnant woman" <note>original Greek: γυναῖκα ἔγγυον</note>. See whether it should be read as "pregnant" <note>original Greek: ἔγκυον</note>, and as for what follows, "signifies" <note>original Greek: σημαίνει</note>, the requirements of the sentence structure and the sense demand that it be changed to "is painted" <note>original Greek: ζωγραφεῖται</note>.

"As if having wings" <note>original Greek: οἷον πτέρυγας ἔχων</note>. I believe it should be read as "having" <note>original Greek: ἔχοντα</note>, which the phrasing itself sufficiently indicates.

"Signifies an hour" <note>original Greek: ὥραν δηλοῖ</note>. This can be translated as either "an hour" or as "the most beautiful part of the year" <note>The Greek word *hōra* can mean a specific hour, a season, or the "prime" of life/year</note>. But since the author's reasoning for this is not apparent, it results in us not quite grasping which way it should be taken here.

<summary>This page contains philological notes on the second book of Horapollo's Hieroglyphica, where the commentator suggests textual corrections and explains the symbolism of the solstice, the anemone, and images of restraint.</summary>
<keywords>solstice, philology, textual criticism, anemone, Pliny the Elder, Erasmus, Dioscorides, Egyptian symbolism, Greek grammar</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the "Hieroglyphica" of Horapollo, focusing on Book II. The commentator provides textual corrections and citations from ancient natural historians like Pliny the Elder and Dioscorides to explain the symbolic animals and plants mentioned in the text.</meta>

# OBSERVATIONS

"As if hunting" <gloss>the air</gloss> <note>original Greek: ὡς θήραν τὸν ἀέρα</note>. *This passage is also mutilated, and it is not easy to restore unless you remove the phrase "the air" as being superfluous.*

"Seven things" <note>original Greek: πράγματα ἑπτά</note>. *I would prefer to read "seven letters" <note>original Greek: γράμματα</note> along with the old translator. But we are also ignorant of the reason for this matter.*

"They paint an ichneumon" <note>original Greek: ἰχνεύμονα ζωγραφῶσιν</note>. *Regarding the ichneumon <note>a mongoose-like animal famed in antiquity for attacking snakes</note>, see Pliny’s "Natural History," Book 8, chapter 24.*

"They hieroglyphically represent oregano" <note>original Greek: ὀρίγανον ἱερογλυφοῦσι</note>. *This herb is so named because it "rejoices in the mountains" <note>from the Greek 'oros' (mountain) and 'ganos' (joy)</note>, or as others prefer, because it sharpens the sight. Regarding this and its various types, see Pliny, Book 20, chapter 17. This is also called "cunila." See the author Dioscorides <note>an ancient Greek physician and botanist</note>, Book 3, chapter 28.*

"Lashing out" <note>original Greek: ἐκσοΐζοντα</note>. *I have translated this, following the old translator, as "lashing with its tail." This expression properly means the same as "those casting out from the portico," as if driving something away from its porch or cave.*

"Through <unclear>the</unclear> is the flesh" <note>original Greek: διὰ τῆς σάρξ ἐστι</note>. *On this matter, see Pliny, Book 8, chapter 44.*

"A bull toward the left" <note>original Greek: ταῦρον ἐπὶ τὰ ἀριστερὰ</note>. *See the same Pliny, in the same book, chapter 45.*

"An eagle carrying a stone" <note>original Greek: ἀετὸν λίθον βασάζοντα</note>. *Regarding eagles, see Pliny, Book 10, chapter 3.*

"Ears" <note>original Greek: ὦτα</note>. *Pliny thinks this bird <note>likely the long-eared owl</note> is so named because it has prominent feathery ears, which he describes in Book 10, chapters 22 and 23.*

"He breaks himself upon the ground, and holes," etc. <note>original Greek: ῥήσσει ἑαυτὸν ἐπὶ τὴν γῆν, καὶ ὀπῶν</note>. *Instead of "breaks" <note>rhēssei</note>, some read "throws" <note>rhiptei</note>; likewise, instead of "breach" <note>rhēgmatos</note>, they read "throw" <note>rhimmatos</note>, and for "holes" <note>opōn</note>, they read "dusting" <note>konōn</note>. I do not disapprove of this reading.*

"He puts mouth into mouth" <note>original Greek: στόμα στόματι ἐμβάλλει</note>. *The male viper, says Pliny in Book 10, chapter 62, inserts his head into the female's mouth, which she bites off in the sweetness of pleasure <note>a common ancient biological myth regarding viper reproduction</note>.*

"For this in the" <gloss>is not born</gloss> <note>original Greek: αὕτη γὰρ ἐν τῇ οὐ τίκτεται</note>. *It seems that "in the natural order" <note>naturali ordine ac ratione</note>, or something similar, is missing.*

"They paint a salamander" <note>original Greek: σαλαμάνδραν ζωγραφῶσιν</note>. *On this, see Pliny, Book 10, chapter 66.*

"They paint a mole" <note>original Greek: ἀσπάλακα ζωγραφῶσι</note>. *That moles do not have sight, yet the likeness of eyes is present if one pulls back the membrane stretched over them, is the authority of Pliny, Book 11, chapter 37.*

"A man deceived" <note>original Greek: ἄνθρωπον διαυθέντα</note>, *from the verb "to soak" or "to moisten" <note>diainein</note>, which word sometimes signifies "to deceive" and "to circumvent."*

"Having eaten fleabane" <note>original Greek: φαγόντα κόνυζαν</note>. *On which, see Dioscorides, Book 3, chapter 116.*

"A pregnant bear" <note>original Greek: ἄρκτον ἐγκυμονοῦσαν</note>. *Pliny, Book 8, chapter 36.*

"An elephant and a ram" <note>original Greek: ἐλέφαντα καὶ κριὸν</note>. *The old translator read "deer" <note>elaphos</note> instead of "elephant" <note>elephas</note>, just as a little later where mention is made of the elephant's teeth.*

"The herb maidenhair" <note>original Greek: ἀδίαντον τὴν βοτάνην</note>. *This is the plant commonly called "Venus's hair," having obtained its name <note>adianton means 'unwetted'</note> because its leaf, when sprinkled with water, does not get wet—* <note>The text cuts off mid-word</note>

<summary>This page contains a series of philological notes on the second book of the Hieroglyphica, using references from Pliny the Elder and Dioscorides to explain or correct the descriptions of animals and plants like the ichneumon, oregano, and the viper.</summary>
<keywords>Pliny the Elder, Dioscorides, philology, ichneumon, oregano, viper, salamander, maidenhair fern, natural history, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 286 ---
<meta>This page concludes the philological observations on the "Hieroglyphica" of Horapollo, wrapping up the scholarly commentary with references to classical literature and natural history before the printer’s colophon. It maintains the same academic tone as the previous page, focusing on textual emendations.</meta>

<header>OBSERVATIONS ON HORAPOLLO</header>

*...so that it does not get wet, but always appears dry. Regarding this, see Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note>, Book 4, chapter 120.*

"Wherefore it is also called *kammeros*" <note>original Greek: διὸ καὶ κάμμηρος λέγεται</note>) Some read *kammeros*, which amounts to the same meaning, from "bending the thigh" <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ τοῦ κάμπτειν τὸν μηρόν; likely a reference to a plant or anatomical term</note>.

"Wine-heavy, with the eyes of a dog, but the heart of a deer" <note>original Greek: οἰνοβαρὲς κυνὸς ὄμματα ἔχων, κραδίην δ’ ἐλάφοιο</note>) Homer, *Iliad*, Book 1. These are the words of Achilles as he is insulting Agamemnon. Hessus <note>Helius Eobanus Hessus, a famous 16th-century German humanist who translated the Iliad into Latin verse</note> translates it thus:

> *Alas, overcome by wine, with a face distorted like a dog's,*
> *You who have the trembling heart of a fearful, most cowardly deer.*

"Having a *lameia*" <note>original Greek: λάμειαν ἔχοντα</note>) I read *laimeian* <note>original Greek: λαίμειαν</note>, in the sense of "gluttony" <note>original Greek: λαιμαργίαν</note>.

"A feathered sparrow" <note>original Greek: στρουθίον πτερίτιν</note>) Regarding this kind of sparrow, see the works of Paul of Aegina <note>a 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician</note>, Book 1.

"An ostrich feather" <note>original Greek: στρουθοκάμηλος πτερόν</note>) Regarding the ostrich, which is the largest of birds, see Pliny <note>the Elder</note> at the very beginning of Book 2.

---

->And let this be the end of our Observations. Although we have not restored every passage that was mutilated in this author, you, dear Reader, will regard this favorably for the reason that the old manuscript did not contain the whole of the first book, and the Venetian edition, from which these were first transcribed, was full of many errors; indeed, I suspect it had no fewer than what has been printed here for many years. Meanwhile, fare you well, and enjoy these works.<-

# Printed by Guillaume Morel for Jacques Kerver, in Paris, 1551.
